## Lesson 12 — The state as an ethical institution

Progress: 12 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block B — I. Organic theory of the state

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §§3 and 6's organic entries and the Advanced companion §4's "ethical institution" definition; read against the chapter's sub-heading **THE STATE AS AN ETHICAL INSTITUTION** at PDF p.222 (printed 204) and the biological school's three marks of an organism at PDF p.223 (printed 205).
CA search: "state as ethical institution moral function perfectionism political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of the ethical state. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. From the second half of the Greek formula

✅ The chapter derives this section explicitly from the unused half of the formula: the second implication of **'the State comes into existence for the sake of life and continues for the sake of good life'** is that the state **is an instrument of good life**. ✅ Living in a state helps man **not only survive but also secure an excellent life**; the state has **a moralizing effect on the life of man**, so that **by performing his duties and enjoying his rights as a citizen of the state, man is able to achieve moral excellence**.

🔑 Note the mechanism: moral excellence is achieved **by performing duties and enjoying rights** — that is, through citizenship itself, not through anything the state teaches. ⚠️ The state is not a moral instructor; it is a moral *gymnasium*.

### 2. The uncomfortable foundation: differentiation of functions

⚠️ The chapter then does something a sympathetic textbook would not: it shows what the ethical claim rests on.

✅ **The ethical foundations of the state, as envisaged by the organic theory, rest on the differentiation of functions.** ✅ Aristotle **believes in the natural differences between the capabilities of different individuals** and declares **'From the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule.'** ✅ From this, **some are destined to be masters and others to be slaves**; Aristotle therefore **treats slavery as a natural institution**, defending it on the ground that **the masters are endowed with a high degree of virtue which can be preserved by enjoying the leisure while the slaves can benefit from that virtue by serving their masters**. ✅ Thus the institution of slavery **is supposed to secure the good life both for the masters and the slaves**.

```text
   ETHICAL CLAIM        the state produces GOOD LIFE
         |
   rests on             DIFFERENTIATION OF FUNCTIONS
         |
   grounded in          "natural differences between the capabilities
                        of different individuals"
         |
   stated as            "From the hour of birth some men are marked out
                        for subjection and others for rule"
         |
   yields               SLAVERY as a NATURAL institution, defended as
                        securing good life FOR BOTH parties
```

⚠️ Do not soften this in an answer. 🔑 The chapter presents the defence of slavery as a consequence of the ethical theory rather than as an embarrassing aside, which is exactly why its third named weakness at Lesson 13 is **Denial of Equality** rather than something milder.

### 3. The biological school's three marks, and what they license

✅ Drawing **a clear distinction between the organic view and the mechanistic view**, the exponents of the biological school identified **three essential characteristics of an organism**:

| # | ✅ Characteristic | ✅ The chapter's illustration | ⚠️ What it licenses politically |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) | **An intrinsic relationship between the parts and the whole**; unlike a machine part, an organism's part **has no existence apart from its position within that organism** | **A wheel retains its essential character as a wheel whether it is fitted in a machine or is separated from it, while a hand loses its character as a hand as soon as it is separated from the body** | The individual cannot be withdrawn from the state without ceasing to be what they are |
| (b) | **Organic unity of its parts** and a tendency **to grow from within**; a machine's old parts can be replaced, an organism **cannot be altered by substituting new parts for old**, yet **can transform itself gradually through natural growth** | — | Reform must be gradual and internal; revolution is a category mistake |
| (c) | **An organism exists as an end-in-itself while a machine is, at best, a means to an end which exists outside itself** | — | The state is an end; individuals are not |

✅ The school maintained that **the state possesses all the three characteristics of an organism and, therefore, it should be regarded as organic in nature**. ✅ As an organism is **the real source of life and energy for its parts**, so the state is **the spring of good life for its citizens**.

⚠️ Mark (b) deserves a second look. 🔑 It is a conservative constitutional doctrine disguised as biology: if a political order cannot be improved by substituting new parts, then every project of designed institutional reform is misconceived, and Burke's position follows without further argument.

### 4. The escalation, and the conclusion about rights

✅ Some writers claimed that **the state makes an appeal to the rational nature of man** and therefore eulogised it as a **'Moral Organism'**, **'Super-organism'**, and an **'Organism of Organisms'**. ✅ Some **attributed personality to the state** and glorified it as a **'Real Person'** or a **'Super Person'**.

✅ Then the political conclusion, stated in the chapter's own words and worth memorising exactly:

> As individual organs of an organism, such as hands, feet or teeth, can have no real interests of their own apart from the interest of the organism itself, so the interests of individuals could not be distinguished from the interest of the state. Thus, the champions of the organic theory claimed that individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state. True freedom of the individual lies in obedience to the laws of the state.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.223 (printed 205)

🔑 Three moves in four lines: no separate interests, therefore no rights against the state, therefore freedom *is* obedience. ⚠️ Every one of the three weaknesses at Lesson 13 attacks one of those moves.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the answer to any stem on whether an ideal state can accommodate individual rights and liberties.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems pairing Plato and Aristotle on statism and individualism want the differentiation-of-functions material and the rights conclusion.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting the ethical state as benign perfectionism. ⚠️ The chapter grounds it in natural inequality and shows it defending slavery.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Quote the three-step conclusion on rights; it compresses the division's entire political content into one sentence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The second implication of the Greek formula is that the state **is an instrument of good life**. (PDF p.222)
2. ✅ The state has **a moralizing effect on the life of man**, who achieves **moral excellence** by **performing his duties and enjoying his rights as a citizen**.
3. ✅ **The ethical foundations of the state... rest on the differentiation of functions.**
4. ✅ Aristotle believes in **the natural differences between the capabilities of different individuals**.
5. ✅ **'From the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule.'**
6. ✅ He **treats slavery as a natural institution**, supposed to secure **the good life both for the masters and the slaves**.
7. ✅ Biological school, mark (a): a wheel keeps its character when removed, **while a hand loses its character as a hand as soon as it is separated from the body**.
8. ✅ Mark (b): an organism **cannot be altered by substituting new parts for old**, yet **can transform itself gradually through natural growth**.
9. ✅ Mark (c): an organism **exists as an end-in-itself while a machine is, at best, a means to an end**.
10. ✅ Honorifics: **'Moral Organism'**, **'Super-organism'**, **'Organism of Organisms'**, **'Real Person'**, **'Super Person'**.
11. 🔑 **Individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state.**
12. ✅ **True freedom of the individual lies in obedience to the laws of the state.**
13. ⚠️ Mark (b) is a conservative doctrine of reform in biological dress: designed replacement of institutions is ruled out.

### Concept check

**Question:** Reconstruct the route by which the organic theory reaches the conclusion that there can be no rights against the state, and identify the step at which the reasoning is weakest.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The route has four steps. ✅ First, the state is an organism and individuals are its organs, so a part **has no existence apart from its position within that organism**. ✅ Second, the organism **exists as an end-in-itself**, so the state is an end and its members are not. ✅ Third, organs such as **hands, feet or teeth, can have no real interests of their own apart from the interest of the organism itself**, so **the interests of individuals could not be distinguished from the interest of the state**. ✅ Fourth, therefore **individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state**, and **true freedom of the individual lies in obedience to the laws of the state**. ⚠️ The weakest step is the third. ⚠️ A hand has no interests because it has no point of view; a citizen has both, and the chapter's own critical estimate relies on exactly this, observing that the individual **is not allowed to take a stand at the instance of his own conscience**. 🔑 The analogy survives only while the organ lacks a conscience.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the ethical claim as separable from the hierarchy claim. ⚠️ The chapter states that the ethical foundations **rest on the differentiation of functions**, and immediately gives Aristotle's defence of slavery as the illustration — so an answer that praises the ethical state while ignoring its basis has suppressed the chapter's argument.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does the organic theory establish the state as an ethical institution, and on what does that ethical claim depend? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ It derives the claim from the second half of the formula that the state **continues for the sake of good life**: living in a state helps man **secure an excellent life**, and the state's **moralizing effect** enables moral excellence through **performing his duties and enjoying his rights as a citizen**. ✅ The biological school reinforces this by identifying the state as an organism that is **the spring of good life for its citizens** and **exists as an end-in-itself**, which licenses the honorifics **'Moral Organism'** and **'Real Person'**. ⚠️ The claim depends on inequality. ✅ **The ethical foundations of the state... rest on the differentiation of functions**, grounded in **natural differences between the capabilities of different individuals** and in Aristotle's dictum that **from the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule** — from which slavery is defended as a natural institution. 🔑 Ethical elevation purchased with natural hierarchy.

**Rubric [10]:** the derivation from the good-life formula **2** · the mechanism of moral excellence through citizenship **2** · the dependence on differentiation of functions **3** · Aristotle's dictum and the slavery consequence **2** · a closing line naming the cost **1**.

## Lesson 13 — A critical estimate of the organic theory

Progress: 13 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block B — I. Organic theory of the state

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's trap list and the Advanced companion §7's first entry, which records that the chapter's critique is severe; read against the chapter's sub-heading **A CRITICAL ESTIMATE** at PDF p.223 (printed 205) and its three named weaknesses across PDF pp.223-225 (printed 205-207), including the two MacIver passages.
CA search: "organic theory of state totalitarian implication critique contemporary 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's critique. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The concession that comes first

✅ The estimate opens generously: the theory **has been in vogue since ancient times**, was **challenged by the mechanistic theory in the seventeenth century**, and **its revival in the nineteenth century is ample proof of its strength**. ✅ It **views the state as the greatest institution which helps man achieve the highest development of his potential**. ✅ Then the turn: **However, in spite of some merit, the weaknesses of the organic theory are quite striking.**

⚠️ Copy this order. 🔑 A criticism that begins with the strongest available statement of the position criticised is worth more than one that begins with the objection.

### 2. Weakness one — Subordination of Man to the State

✅ **In this theory man is completely overshadowed by the state.** ✅ His personality is **not only subordinated but submerged in the personality of the state**. ✅ Man is treated as **no more than a conduit pipe for the divine energy**, as **a passive creature for whom things must be done, not as a being who finds fulfilment in positive activity**. ✅ **The individual is not allowed to take a stand at the instance of his own conscience** because **he cannot rise above his predetermined position within the framework of the state**.

⚠️ Then the ideal-versus-actual move taught at Lesson 7, in its original location:

```text
   The theory describes an 'IDEAL' state and prescribes relations
   between man and the state accordingly
                  |
   In ACTUAL practice "the state is represented by a set of men
   exercising the powers and functions of the state"
                  |
   "there is no mechanism to ensure that these men will display the
    same degree of wisdom, sincerity and responsibility"
                  |
   so obedience is owed to legislators, ministers, judges, bureaucrats
   and military commanders "who may be as imperfect as their subjects,
   if not more than they are"
                  |
   decisions may follow "narrow selfish interests", "a whim" or "lack
   of vision"
                  |
   if such authority is "absolute and beyond challenge" and all citizens
   must "sacrifice everything in the name of the state", such decisions
   "are bound to destroy the state itself"
                  |
   EVIDENCE: "the Nazi and Fascist regimes in Germany and Italy
   respectively during the period between the two World Wars"
```

🔑 The argument is self-defeat, not immorality: the theory's own goal — the preservation of the whole — is destroyed by the obedience it demands.

### 3. Weakness two — Distorted View of Freedom

✅ The theory **postulates that man can have no rights against the state, and thus leaves no safeguards for his liberty**. ✅ **In fact, it reduces the liberty of the individual to obedience to the commands of the state.** ✅ And it **ignores the distinction between the state and society while determining the extent of the authority of the state**.

✅ MacIver, *The Web of Government* (1965), is then quoted twice. ⚠️ The first passage is the state/society warning taught at Lesson 5. ⚠️ The second extends it into a lineage:

> This is the road Rousseau opened up when he spoke of 'forcing men to be free.' This is the road Fichte and Hegel prepared, making them the forerunners of the ruinous and finally nihilistic doctrine of Fascists and Nazis. This is the road that under the signs of liberty and unity invites men to the concentration camp and the death of the creative spirit.
>
> — MacIver, *The Web of Government* (1965), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF pp.224-225 (printed 206-207)

⚠️ Two cautions before this is used. ⚠️ First, it is MacIver's judgment, reported by the chapter, not a claim the chapter independently argues. ⚠️ Second, it is a genealogical claim — that one formulation prepared the ground for a later doctrine — and genealogies of this kind are contested. ✅ The chapter's own framing is that **this sort of queer logic has been invoked by several thinkers and statesmen for a complete suppression of man's freedom**, which attributes the abuse to the invokers rather than making Rousseau responsible for the camps. 🔑 Report the passage, attribute it, and mark the distance.

### 4. Weakness three — Denial of Equality

✅ **The organic theory of the state is essentially undemocratic.** ✅ Following the analogy, it argues that **since the toes cannot dictate to the brain, so there is no question of representation of the ordinary people to the seat of authority**. ✅ Thus **wisdom and authority become the monopoly of the chosen few and the masses are alienated from the process of decision-making**. ✅ This is **a clever device for preserving the inequality of status and opportunity on the pretext of supposed ethical principles**.

✅ The chapter then names who uses such arguments: **all those who have vested interests in the prevailing injustice in society**, whether justifying **slavery, racial supremacy, imperialism or any other form of exploitation**.

✅ And it supplies the empirical rebuttal: **There is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people.** ✅ **Most differences in the excellence of people in any sphere of life are the product of the socio-economic system.** ✅ **Educational development, economic opportunities, adequate health-care and cultural participation, wherever provided, have always helped develop their excellence** — a development **never recognized by the champions of the organic theory of the state**.

🔑 That last sequence is the chapter's most forward-looking passage in this division: it replaces a natural explanation of difference with a social one, and so anticipates the class, feminist and post-colonial perspectives, each of which treats inequality as produced rather than given.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson is the ready-made critical half of any organic-theory, idealist-state or Plato-and-Aristotle answer.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "Critically examine" wants all three weaknesses in the chapter's order; "Is the ideal state compatible with individual rights?" wants weaknesses one and two.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that the organic theory *is* fascism. ⚠️ The chapter's claim is narrower: the theory leaves no safeguard, and the inter-war regimes show what follows when its demand for absolute obedience is honoured.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Close on the social explanation of excellence; it converts a historical criticism into a contemporary argument.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Concession: **its revival in the nineteenth century is ample proof of its strength**; it **helps man achieve the highest development of his potential**. (PDF p.223)
2. ✅ **In spite of some merit, the weaknesses of the organic theory are quite striking.**
3. ✅ Weakness 1, **Subordination of Man to the State**: personality **not only subordinated but submerged**; man as **a conduit pipe for the divine energy**.
4. ✅ **The individual is not allowed to take a stand at the instance of his own conscience.**
5. ✅ In practice the state is **a set of men**, with **no mechanism** to ensure their **wisdom, sincerity and responsibility**; they **may be as imperfect as their subjects**.
6. ✅ Absolute, unchallengeable authority plus total sacrifice **are bound to destroy the state itself** — evidenced by **the Nazi and Fascist regimes in Germany and Italy**.
7. ✅ Weakness 2, **Distorted View of Freedom**: it **reduces the liberty of the individual to obedience to the commands of the state**.
8. ✅ It **ignores the distinction between the state and society**; MacIver calls this **the dangerous road to totalitarianism**.
9. ✅ MacIver's lineage: Rousseau's **'forcing men to be free'**, then **Fichte and Hegel**, then **Fascists and Nazis**, ending in **the concentration camp and the death of the creative spirit**.
10. ⚠️ That lineage is MacIver's reported judgment, not the chapter's own argument; the chapter blames **this sort of queer logic** as **invoked by several thinkers and statesmen**.
11. ✅ Weakness 3, **Denial of Equality**: **the toes cannot dictate to the brain**, so **wisdom and authority become the monopoly of the chosen few**.
12. ✅ It is **a clever device for preserving the inequality of status and opportunity on the pretext of supposed ethical principles**, used to justify **slavery, racial supremacy, imperialism**.
13. 🔑 **There is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people**; most differences **are the product of the socio-economic system**.
14. ✅ **Educational development, economic opportunities, adequate health-care and cultural participation** have **always helped develop their excellence**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter's first criticism of the organic theory is that it subordinates man to the state. Show why the chapter treats this as a practical objection rather than a purely moral one, and state the evidence it offers.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because the objection turns on who actually exercises the state's authority. ✅ The theory **visualizes the attributes of an 'ideal' state and prescribes relations between man and the state accordingly**, but **in actual practice... the state is represented by a set of men exercising the powers and functions of the state**, and **there is no mechanism to ensure that these men will display the same degree of wisdom, sincerity and responsibility as attributed to an 'ideal' state**. ✅ Obedience therefore runs to **legislators, ministers, judges, bureaucrats, military commanders** who **may be as imperfect as their subjects, if not more than they are**, and whose decisions may follow **narrow selfish interests**, **a whim** or **the lack of vision**. ⚠️ The objection is practical because it predicts a result the theory itself must regret: if such authority is treated as **absolute and beyond challenge**, the decisions **are bound to destroy the state itself**. ✅ The evidence offered is **the Nazi and Fascist regimes in Germany and Italy respectively during the period between the two World Wars**. 🔑 The theory defeats its own purpose.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present the inter-war regimes as the organic theory's logical conclusion. ⚠️ The chapter offers them as what happens when absolute obedience is actually enforced, and attributes the abuse to those who invoke **this sort of queer logic**; MacIver's genealogy through Rousseau, Fichte and Hegel is quoted as his judgment, not adopted as the chapter's demonstration.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Critically examine the organic theory of the state. *(Critically examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The theory makes the state an organism and individuals its organs, so that their **existence and worth** depend on the whole and some groups are **naturally fit** for superior positions; it treats the state as both a **natural** and an **ethical** institution, concluding that there are **no rights against the state** and that **true freedom... lies in obedience**. ⚠️ Its merit is real: it explains interdependence and, by the chapter's own admission, **helps man achieve the highest development of his potential**. ⚠️ Three weaknesses are decisive. ✅ It **submerges** personality so that conscience cannot act, and transfers absolute obedience to officials who **may be as imperfect as their subjects** — the inter-war regimes being the instance. ✅ It **reduces the liberty of the individual to obedience** and merges state with society. ✅ It is **essentially undemocratic**, for **the toes cannot dictate to the brain**, though **there is no scientific evidence** of such natural differences. 🔑 A profound account of belonging, an indefensible account of authority.

**Rubric [10]:** the theory stated before it is criticised **2** · a genuine concession **1** · all three named weaknesses **4** · at least two pieces of chapter evidence, such as the officials argument or the absence of scientific evidence **2** · a closing judgment distinguishing what survives from what fails **1**.

## Lesson 14 — The mechanistic turn

LIBERAL-INDIVIDUALIST PERSPECTIVE** and the chapter's opening paragraphs at PDF p.225 (printed 207).
CA search: "mechanistic conception of society non-interference origins liberalism 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of the mechanistic turn. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The division's opening claim

✅ **Liberal-individualist perspective on the state is based on mechanistic view of the state.** ✅ It **arose in a particular historical setting when several factors contributed to its development**, the first of which is the growth of **the physical sciences in seventeenth century Europe**.

⚠️ The chapter then lists what reinforced it: **This perspective on the state was particularly strengthened from two sides: (a) the exponents of 'social contract' sought to trace the origin of the state in a way that fitted into liberal mode of thought; and (b) the exponents of modern economics made a strong case for laissez-faire individualism which confirmed liberal-individualist view of the state.**

```text
            MECHANISTIC VIEW OF THE STATE  (p.225)
                        |
         +--------------+---------------+
         |                              |
  (a) SOCIAL CONTRACT            (b) LAISSEZ-FAIRE INDIVIDUALISM
      traces the ORIGIN in a         makes the ECONOMIC case for
      way that fits liberal          non-interference
      thought
         |                              |
   pp.226-236                      pp.236-244
   Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau         Smith, Bentham, James Mill,
   + A CRITICAL APPRAISAL          Spencer + libertarian revival
```

🔑 Division II therefore has exactly two halves, and the chapter's own sentence supplies the division. ⚠️ Lessons 15-22 take branch (a); Lessons 23-25 take branch (b).

### 2. The inference, and the interest

⚠️ The argument from physics to politics is short enough to reproduce in full, and its last step is where the chapter stops being a reporter:

- ✅ **Since 'nature' itself was a machine, governed by universal laws to be discovered by observation and reason, so society and the state should also be understood as mechanisms.**
- ✅ **Thus, the social order came to be understood as a part of the 'natural order' and any interference with the social system was thought to be detrimental to its smooth functioning.**
- ✅ **This idea of 'non-interference' highly suited the interests and aptitudes of the new middle class—the merchants and the industrialists who flourished in the climate of a 'free market' society.**
- ✅ **The liberal theory represented the social and economic philosophy of this class.**

⚠️ The third and fourth bullets are a sociology of knowledge, not a refutation. 🔑 Showing that a doctrine suited an interest does not show that it is false — but it does place a burden of proof, and the chapter returns to that burden in the critical appraisal at Lesson 22 and again at the welfare division's closing verdict at Lesson 32.

### 3. The hidden tension inside the mechanistic image

⚠️ There is an instability here that repays noticing, because it becomes the engine of the whole division.

⚠️ If society is **part of the 'natural order'** and runs by **universal laws**, then it is natural — and the mechanistic theory has borrowed the organic theory's strongest premise while denying its conclusion. ⚠️ Yet the mechanistic theory also says the state is **an artificial contrivance**, deliberately made. 🔑 So the mechanistic liberal must hold that *society* is natural and self-regulating while the *state* is artificial and dispensable — which is precisely MacIver's position two hundred pages of argument later, and precisely what the class theory inverts by calling society natural and the state an imposition.

⚠️ Keep that structure in view: much of this chapter is a quarrel about which of the pair is natural.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Any stem asking for the "basis" or "origins" of liberalism is answered from this page: physical science, the natural order, non-interference, the middle class.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems contrasting liberal and Marxist accounts of the state often reward the observation that the chapter itself attributes liberalism to a class interest.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying liberalism begins with Locke's politics. ⚠️ The chapter begins it with a change in the image of nature.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The two-branch structure — contract and laissez-faire — is the cleanest possible skeleton for a twenty-mark answer on liberalism's view of the state.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Liberal-individualist perspective on the state is based on mechanistic view of the state.** (PDF p.225)
2. ✅ **The growth of the physical sciences in seventeenth century Europe tended to transform men's ideas about society and the state.**
3. ✅ Since nature **was a machine, governed by universal laws**, society and the state **should also be understood as mechanisms**.
4. ✅ The social order became **a part of the 'natural order'**, so interference was **detrimental to its smooth functioning**.
5. ✅ Non-interference **highly suited the interests and aptitudes of the new middle class—the merchants and the industrialists**.
6. ✅ **The liberal theory represented the social and economic philosophy of this class.**
7. ✅ Branch (a): the exponents of **'social contract'** traced the state's origin **in a way that fitted into liberal mode of thought**.
8. ✅ Branch (b): the exponents of **modern economics** made the case for **laissez-faire individualism**.
9. ⚠️ Showing that a doctrine suited an interest is a burden of proof, not a refutation.
10. 🔑 The mechanistic liberal treats society as natural and the state as artificial; the class theory keeps the first and transforms the second into an imposition.
11. ⚠️ Division II runs PDF pp.225-244; its two halves divide at PDF p.236.
12. ⚠️ Liberalism in this chapter begins with a change in the image of nature, not with a political event.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trace the chapter's inference from seventeenth-century physical science to the policy of non-interference, and state what the chapter adds once the inference is complete.

**Model answer:** ✅ The inference has four steps. ✅ Nature itself **was a machine, governed by universal laws to be discovered by observation and reason**. ✅ Therefore **society and the state should also be understood as mechanisms**. ✅ Therefore the social order is **a part of the 'natural order'**, and **any interference with the social system was thought to be detrimental to its smooth functioning**. ✅ Therefore non-interference. ⚠️ What the chapter adds is an attribution of interest: the idea **highly suited the interests and aptitudes of the new middle class—the merchants and the industrialists who flourished in the climate of a 'free market' society**, and **the liberal theory represented the social and economic philosophy of this class**. ⚠️ The addition is not a logical objection; the inference may be valid and the interest real at once. 🔑 It shifts the burden: a doctrine that happens to enrich its authors needs independent support, which is exactly what Smith, Bentham and Spencer then try to supply.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read the attribution to the middle class as the chapter endorsing the Marxist account of liberalism. ⚠️ The chapter later credits liberal theory with **evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government** and calls that its **great merit**; the class attribution is a qualification, not a verdict.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Account for the rise of the liberal-individualist perspective on the state. *(Account for — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter grounds it in a change in the image of nature: the growth of **the physical sciences in seventeenth century Europe** made nature **a machine, governed by universal laws**, so that **society and the state should also be understood as mechanisms**. ✅ The social order thereby became **part of the 'natural order'**, and interference became **detrimental to its smooth functioning**. ✅ This conclusion **highly suited the interests and aptitudes of the new middle class—the merchants and the industrialists**, whose **social and economic philosophy** the liberal theory represented. ✅ Two further developments consolidated it: the **social contract** theorists traced the state's origin **in a way that fitted into liberal mode of thought**, and **the exponents of modern economics made a strong case for laissez-faire individualism**. ⚠️ Intellectual change, class interest and two reinforcing doctrines therefore combine. 🔑 The perspective is an achievement of science, economics and commerce together.

**Rubric [10]:** the scientific inference reconstructed **3** · the class attribution with the chapter's wording **2** · both reinforcing branches named **3** · the combination stated rather than listed **1** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 15 — The social contract as a theory

Progress: 15 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's social-contract definition and §6's Hobbes-Locke-Rousseau entry; with the Advanced companion §4's "artificial device" definition, read against the chapter's sub-heading **THEORY OF THE SOCIAL CONTRACT** and the sub-sections *Exponents of the Theory* and *Outline of the Theory* at PDF pp.226-227 (printed 208-209).
CA search: "social contract theory origin development relevance political thought 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's framing of contract theory. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What kind of explanation a contract explanation is

✅ **The theory of the social contract presents a typical version of the origin of the state from the liberal point of view.** ✅ It **treats the state as the product of the mutual agreement of men, created with a definite purpose, to serve certain social needs**.

✅ The underlying mechanistic commitment is stated immediately: the theory **treats the state as an artificial contrivance**, **postulates deliberate efforts behind the formation of the state**, and therefore **implies the possibility of two life-patterns; one before the origin of the state, the other after the creation of the state**. ✅ The state is **not a natural institution, but an artificial device or instrument invented by men for their mutual benefit**; it is **intended to serve the interests of all individuals or all sections of society**; it is **the product of the will of society**, hence **an expression of 'common will'**.

⚠️ One qualification the chapter inserts here is easily missed and often rewarded. ✅ Some later thinkers, **like T.H. Green (1836-82) and H.J. Laski (1893-1950), joined the liberal stream without subscribing to the mechanistic concept**. 🔑 So mechanism is the contract branch's premise, not liberalism's.

### 2. Ancient traces, and why they do not make the theory ancient

| ✅ Trace | ✅ Where | ⚠️ Status |
|---|---|---|
| A **pointed reference** to creation of the state through a contract | **Kautilya's *Arthashastra*** | A rudimentary form in ancient thought |
| The state as **an outcome of the contract between men** | Some **ancient Greek sophists** | A rudimentary form |
| **Some traces of this theory** | **Ancient Roman law** | A rudimentary form |

✅ The chapter then shuts the inference down: **all these references should not be taken to mean that the theory of the social contract has been prevalent from the ancient times**. ✅ **On the contrary, this theory was systematically formulated at a particular point of European history, in order to drop a curtain on the values of the feudal system and to introduce the new values of the capitalist system.**

🔑 That sentence is the chapter's thesis about the whole contract branch, placed before the exposition rather than after it. ⚠️ It reappears, expanded, as the last paragraph of the critical appraisal at Lesson 22, where the feudal/capitalist contrast is made explicit: **the relations of the feudal society are determined by tradition; those of the capitalist society are determined by contract**.

### 3. The three exponents, and the political work each was doing

| ✅ Thinker | ✅ Dates | ✅ Work and year | ✅ Political purpose the chapter assigns |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Thomas Hobbes** | 1588-1679 | *Leviathan* (1651) | Tutor to Charles II; **sought to justify the absolute power of the sovereign**; **condemned the Civil War of 1642 as he saw in it the forces of disintegration**; sought **absolute sovereignty of the state as an essential condition of social solidarity** |
| **John Locke** | 1632-1704 | *Two Treatises of Civil Government* (1690) | **Sought to justify the Glorious Revolution of 1688**; **an ardent advocate of constitutional monarchy**; if the monarch behaved **in a despotic manner, the people had the right to remove him from authority**; **not the sworn enemy of monarchy**, but sought to establish it **'in the consent of the people'** |
| **Jean Jacques Rousseau** | 1712-78 | — | **Had no such particular purpose to serve**; a **brilliant writer** whose ideas **induced the revolutionary upsurge that shook the French polity to its foundations**; **the source of inspiration of the great French Revolution (1789)** |

✅ The chapter adds that **this theory held the field in Europe in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries**, and that later thinkers — **Immanuel Kant (1724-1804)**, **Herbert Spencer (1820-1903)**, **John Rawls (1921-2002)** and **Robert Nozick (1938-2002)** — **made use of this theory to elaborate their own systems of thought**.

⚠️ 🔶 One dating caution, from the canonical owners rather than from the chapter: Locke's *Two Treatises* was published in 1689 with a title page dated 1690, and textual scholarship places most of its composition in **c.1679-1683**, during the Exclusion Crisis. ⚠️ The safe formulation is that the argument **came to be read** as the Revolution's philosophical defence, not that it was written to celebrate a completed event. ❌ Do not assert composition after 1688.

### 4. The outline, and the honest admission

✅ The theory **implies that there was a time when men lived or would have lived without any recognized civil law, without the state** — the **'state of nature'**. ✅ Then the state **was created through the voluntary agreement of all individuals who constitute the state**. ✅ Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau **have drawn different pictures of the 'state of nature', 'terms of the contract' and the character of sovereignty**.

✅ And the admission: **the whole theory of the social contract is based on speculation**. ✅ **It seeks to trace the origin of the state through logic, not through historical or scientific evidence.** ✅ **Locke refers to a historical fact to illustrate this point, but illustration is no evidence.**

🔑 The theory concedes its own status before it is attacked. ⚠️ That matters for answer-writing: the criticism at Lesson 22 is not a discovery but an elaboration of something the chapter has already granted.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson alone answers a ten-mark stem on the origin and development of contract theory.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems asking whether contract theory addresses rights adequately want the mechanism here plus the criticism at Lesson 22.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating Kautilya and the sophists as proof that contract theory is ancient. ✅ The chapter expressly forbids it.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The feudal-to-capitalist function is the single most distinctive point available on this topic; state it early.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The theory **treats the state as the product of the mutual agreement of men, created with a definite purpose, to serve certain social needs**. (PDF p.226)
2. ✅ The mechanistic theory **treats the state as an artificial contrivance** with **deliberate efforts** behind its formation.
3. ✅ It implies **two life-patterns; one before the origin of the state, the other after**.
4. ✅ The state is **the product of the will of society**, an expression of **'common will'**.
5. ✅ **T.H. Green (1836-82) and H.J. Laski (1893-1950) joined the liberal stream without subscribing to the mechanistic concept.**
6. ✅ Rudimentary traces in **Kautilya's *Arthashastra***, **ancient Greek sophists** and **ancient Roman law**.
7. ✅ But the theory **was systematically formulated at a particular point of European history, in order to drop a curtain on the values of the feudal system and to introduce the new values of the capitalist system**.
8. ✅ **Hobbes** (1588-1679), *Leviathan* (1651): absolute sovereignty; condemned **the Civil War of 1642**.
9. ✅ **Locke** (1632-1704), *Two Treatises of Civil Government* (1690): the Glorious Revolution of 1688; monarchy **'in the consent of the people'**.
10. ✅ **Rousseau** (1712-78): **no such particular purpose**; inspiration of **the great French Revolution (1789)**.
11. ✅ Later users: **Kant (1724-1804)**, **Spencer (1820-1903)**, **Rawls (1921-2002)**, **Nozick (1938-2002)**.
12. ✅ The theory **is based on speculation**; it traces origin **through logic, not through historical or scientific evidence**.
13. ✅ **Locke refers to a historical fact to illustrate this point, but illustration is no evidence.**
14. ⚠️ 🔶 Owner caution: the *Two Treatises* were largely composed **c.1679-1683** and published in 1689; say the argument **came to be read** as the Revolution's defence.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter notes rudimentary traces of contract thinking in ancient Indian, Greek and Roman sources, then denies that the theory is ancient. Explain the denial and the historical function the chapter assigns to the theory instead.

**Model answer:** ✅ The traces are real — **Kautilya's *Arthashastra* makes a pointed reference to it**, **some ancient Greek sophists described the state as an outcome of the contract between men**, and **some traces of this theory are also found in ancient Roman law**. ✅ But **all these references should not be taken to mean that the theory of the social contract has been prevalent from the ancient times**. ⚠️ The denial turns on the difference between an idea occurring and a theory being **systematically formulated**: scattered references do not constitute a doctrine with a state of nature, stated terms and a derived account of sovereignty. ✅ The function assigned instead is historical and specific — the theory was formulated **in order to drop a curtain on the values of the feudal system and to introduce the new values of the capitalist system**, because **the relations of the feudal society are determined by tradition; those of the capitalist society are determined by contract**. 🔑 Contract theory universalised the characteristic transaction of the society that produced it.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not conclude that the theory is therefore merely ideological and can be dismissed. ⚠️ The chapter calls it **a theoretical justification for the new pattern of human relations**, and simultaneously credits the liberal tradition with **evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government** — the contract's historical function and its philosophical yield are separate questions.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Present a brief account of the origin and development of the social contract theory. *(Account — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The theory treats the state as **the product of the mutual agreement of men, created with a definite purpose**, resting on the mechanistic view of the state as **an artificial contrivance** implying **two life-patterns**, before and after. ✅ Rudimentary traces appear in **Kautilya's *Arthashastra***, in **ancient Greek sophists** and in **ancient Roman law**, but the theory was **systematically formulated at a particular point of European history**, to displace feudal values with capitalist ones. ✅ Its exponents are **Hobbes** (*Leviathan*, 1651), defending absolute sovereignty after the Civil War of 1642; **Locke** (*Two Treatises*, 1690), defending constitutional monarchy founded **in the consent of the people**; and **Rousseau**, whose work inspired **the great French Revolution (1789)**. ✅ **Kant**, **Spencer**, **Rawls** and **Nozick** later used it for their own systems. ⚠️ Throughout, it remains **based on speculation**, tracing origin **through logic, not through historical or scientific evidence**. 🔑 A logical device with a datable political task.

**Rubric [10]:** the definition and mechanistic premise **2** · the ancient traces with the chapter's denial **2** · three exponents with works, dates and purposes **3** · later users named **1** · the speculative status acknowledged **1** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 16 — The state of nature — Hobbes

Progress: 16 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Hobbes entry and the Advanced companion §5's Hobbes entry; read against the chapter's sub-heading **THE STATE OF NATURE** and its *Hobbes* sub-section at PDF pp.227-228 (printed 209-210).
CA search: "Hobbes state of nature war of all against all contemporary political theory 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Hobbes's account as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What a state of nature is for

✅ The chapter defines the device before applying it: the state of nature **denotes how men live or would have lived without the authority of civil law, state or political control**. ✅ At this stage **there is no industry, no systematic production**; men **have to depend on the bounty of nature**; their behaviour is **largely governed by their inner impulses, unrestrained by civil law**, although **a 'natural law' is supposed to have existed**; and they **have no recognized rights, although they enjoy some 'natural rights'**.

✅ Then the warning that explains why three accounts differ: **as all these conditions are determined by logic or particular lines of argument, not on the basis of any scientific evidence, they do not lead to any uniform conclusions**.

🔑 The state of nature is a thought experiment whose content is fixed by the theorist's view of human nature. ⚠️ So the real disagreement between Hobbes, Locke and Rousseau is not about prehistory; it is about what people are like when nothing restrains them.

### 2. Hobbes's premises and the picture they produce

```text
  PREMISE   "man is selfish by nature; self-interest is the mainspring
             of human action"
  PREMISE   men are moved "not by intellect or reason, but by their
             appetites, desires and passions"
  PREMISE   physical and mental powers "are nearly alike", so
             "nobody is able to overpower the other"
       |
       v
  CONSEQUENCE  "perpetual struggle, ceaseless conflict and constant
                warfare" -- "every man is enemy to every man"
       |
  CONSEQUENCE  men "stand in natural fear of each other"
               => "a state of total insecurity"
       |
  CONSEQUENCE  "no room for industry"; "'Might is right' is the order
                of the day"; "a state of perfect anarchy"
       |
  RESULT       life is "solitary, poor, nasty, brutish and short"
```

⚠️ The equality premise is the one candidates drop, and it is load-bearing. 🔑 If people were unequal in power, one would dominate and a rough order would emerge; it is **because** their powers **are nearly alike** that no one can win, and fear becomes permanent.

✅ The chapter also inserts Hobbes's own methodological caveat: he **is quite clear that he is not describing a historical fact, but only trying to demonstrate what would happen if there were no settled government for any length of time**. ⚠️ Note how closely that matches Aristotle's logical-not-historical move at Lesson 11, from the opposite direction.

### 3. Rights, morality and law in the Hobbesian state of nature

✅ There **can be no morality or consciousness of duty or obligation in the state of nature, because these are possible only after the establishment of law and government**. ✅ Natural rights are therefore **nothing more than the natural powers of men, used to oppress others**. ✅ At best, natural liberty is **nothing but 'the liberty each man hath to preserve his own life'**.

✅ This urge for **self-preservation** is **embodied in the law of nature or natural law**, which **conforms to the rules of prudence and expediency**, and **prompts men to abandon the state of nature and to establish law and government**. ✅ It consists in rules of self-preservation, **particularly as follows**:

| ✅ Rule | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| (a) | **Everybody should aim at securing peace** |
| (b) | **Men should be willing, in concert with others, to give up their natural rights** |
| (c) | **Men should keep their contracts** |
| (d) | **Men should show gratitude or return beneficence for beneficence** |

✅ **Thus, the requirements of self-preservation itself created a sense of duty in the minds of men which prompts them to form the state.**

⚠️ That last sentence is more interesting than it looks. 🔑 Hobbes has just denied that there is morality before the state, and then derived four duty-like rules from prudence alone. ⚠️ The resolution is that these are not moral obligations but counsels of self-interest — which is why rule (b) asks men to surrender rights that were never more than powers.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Hobbes supplies the security-first pole of every liberty question in this chapter, and the contrast class for Locke and Rousseau.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Any stem on absolute sovereignty, on the justification of authority by fear, or on security versus liberty begins here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Describing Hobbes's state of nature as historical. ✅ The chapter twice denies it.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Name the equality-of-powers premise; it distinguishes a serious answer from a recitation of the famous phrase.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The state of nature denotes life **without the authority of civil law, state or political control**. (PDF p.227)
2. ✅ **No industry, no systematic production**; dependence on **the bounty of nature**; conduct governed by **inner impulses**.
3. ✅ **No recognized rights**, but **some 'natural rights'**; a **'natural law' is supposed to have existed**.
4. ✅ The accounts differ because the conditions are **determined by logic or particular lines of argument**, giving **no uniform conclusions**.
5. ✅ Hobbes: **man is selfish by nature; self-interest is the mainspring of human action**.
6. ✅ Men are moved **not by intellect or reason, but by their appetites, desires and passions**.
7. ✅ Powers are **nearly alike**, so **nobody is able to overpower the other**, and men **stand in natural fear of each other**.
8. ✅ Result: **perpetual struggle, ceaseless conflict and constant warfare**; **every man is enemy to every man**.
9. ✅ Life is **'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish and short'**; **'Might is right'**; **a state of perfect anarchy**.
10. ✅ Hobbes is **not describing a historical fact**, but what would happen **if there were no settled government for any length of time**.
11. ✅ **No morality or consciousness of duty** before law; natural rights are **nothing more than the natural powers of men, used to oppress others**.
12. ✅ Natural liberty is **'the liberty each man hath to preserve his own life'**.
13. ✅ Four rules of natural law: secure peace; give up natural rights in concert; **keep their contracts**; **return beneficence for beneficence**.
14. 🔑 **The requirements of self-preservation itself created a sense of duty** — prudence, not morality, drives men to form the state.

### Concept check

**Question:** Hobbes denies that there is any morality in the state of nature, yet derives four rules of natural law from it. Show how both claims can stand together, and identify the premise without which his picture of universal insecurity collapses.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ They stand together because the four rules are counsels of prudence, not moral duties. ✅ Hobbes holds that **there can be no morality or consciousness of duty or obligation in the state of nature, because these are possible only after the establishment of law and government**, and that natural law **conforms to the rules of prudence and expediency**; its content — secure peace, surrender natural rights in concert with others, **keep their contracts**, **return beneficence for beneficence** — is what self-interested agents would adopt to stay alive. ✅ The chapter's own summary makes the point: **the requirements of self-preservation itself created a sense of duty**. ⚠️ The indispensable premise is rough equality of power: **the physical and mental powers of natural men are nearly alike**, so **nobody is able to overpower the other**. ⚠️ Without it, the strongest would impose order and the condition would be tyranny rather than universal insecurity — and Hobbes's argument for a constructed sovereign would lose its urgency. 🔑 Equality of strength is what makes everyone unsafe.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say Hobbes thinks people are wicked. ⚠️ The chapter's premise is that men are moved **by their appetites, desires and passions** rather than by reason, in a situation with no law and no umpire; the result is a predicament, not a verdict on character — which is why identical agents behave peaceably once a sovereign exists.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain Hobbes's account of the state of nature and show how it generates the need for a sovereign. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Hobbes postulates that **man is selfish by nature** and moved **not by intellect or reason, but by their appetites, desires and passions**. ✅ Because men's **physical and mental powers... are nearly alike**, **nobody is able to overpower the other**, so all **stand in natural fear of each other** in **a state of total insecurity**. ✅ The condition is one of **perpetual struggle, ceaseless conflict and constant warfare** in which **every man is enemy to every man**, there is **no room for industry**, **'Might is right'**, and life is **'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish and short'**. ⚠️ Since there is no morality before law, natural rights are only **the natural powers of men, used to oppress others**, and natural liberty is merely **the liberty each man hath to preserve his own life**. ✅ Natural law, as prudence, therefore directs men to seek peace, surrender those rights in concert and keep contracts. 🔑 Self-preservation itself produces the sovereign.

**Rubric [10]:** the three premises including equality of powers **3** · the resulting condition with the chapter's phrases **3** · the status of natural rights and liberty **2** · the prudential derivation of natural law **1** · a closing line connecting insecurity to sovereignty **1**.

## Lesson 17 — The state of nature — Locke

Progress: 17 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Locke entry with its dating caution, and the Advanced companion §5's Locke entry; read against the chapter's *Locke* sub-section under **THE STATE OF NATURE** at PDF pp.228-229 (printed 210-211), including the W.T. Jones passage reproduced there.
CA search: "Locke state of nature natural rights life liberty property contemporary citation 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Locke's account as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A different picture from a different premise

✅ **Locke draws an entirely different picture of the state of nature.** ✅ It is **not a state of constant warfare**; on the contrary it is **a state of 'peace, goodwill, mutual assistance and preservation'**. ✅ It is **a state of liberty, not a state of licence**.

✅ The reason is a different anthropology. ✅ **The majority of people at this stage obey the law of nature, that is the law of inward morality.** ✅ **Men are by nature rational beings, impelled by their inner nature to treat humanity—whether in their own person or in that of any other person—in every case as an end, never as a means only.**

| ⚠️ Element | ✅ Hobbes | ✅ Locke |
|---|---|---|
| Human motive | **Appetites, desires and passions** | **Rational beings** obeying **inward morality** |
| Condition | **Perpetual struggle**, **total insecurity** | **Peace, goodwill, mutual assistance and preservation** |
| Natural law | **Rules of prudence and expediency** | **Rules of morality implanted in the human conscience** |
| Natural rights | **Natural powers... used to oppress others** | **Perfect freedom and equality** to preserve **life, liberty and estate**, and to judge and punish breaches |
| Defect | Insecurity — life itself is at risk | **Inconvenience** — no impartial judge |
| What is surrendered | Everything, **once and for all** | Only the right to **be judges in case of breaches of law** |

🔑 The whole of Locke's politics is in the two bottom rows. ⚠️ A smaller defect permits a smaller surrender, and a smaller surrender leaves rights standing against the government.

### 2. The defect, stated precisely

✅ **But still, there are a few persons who set aside the rules of morality in pursuance of their self-interest.** ✅ **In the absence of any established authority in the state of nature, it becomes very difficult to deal with such offenders.** ✅ **If men become judges of their own cases, justice would not be secured.** ✅ **In this respect, the state of nature proves to be inconvenient.** ✅ **In order to rectify this defect, men abandon the state of nature and enter into civil or political society by means of a contract.**

⚠️ Note the word **inconvenient**. 🔑 Hobbes's state of nature is intolerable; Locke's is merely inconvenient — and a government created to remove an inconvenience cannot claim unlimited powers.

### 3. Natural law, natural rights, and what survives the contract

✅ Natural law **consists in the rules of morality implanted in the human conscience**. ✅ Natural rights consist in the **'perfect freedom and equality' of every man 'not only to preserve his property, that is his life, liberty and estate, against the injuries and attempts of other men, but to be judge of, and punish the breaches of natural law' committed by others**.

✅ On entering political society, men **surrender their natural rights to be judges in case of breaches of law**; **this power is now vested in the community instead of in individuals**. ✅ **But they still retain their natural rights to 'life, liberty and property'.**

⚠️ Observe the definition built into the quotation: **property, that is his life, liberty and estate**. 🔑 Property in Locke's usage here is the wide term covering all three, which is why the narrower triad **life, liberty and property** at the end is a restatement rather than an addition.

### 4. The status of the account — W.T. Jones's correction

✅ **Locke's picture of the state of nature is, again, hypothetical, not a historical account.** ✅ The chapter reproduces W.T. Jones's elucidation:

> Locke means by the natural law ... a rule for human behaviour only, and one which is not a description of how men do behave, but a statement of how they ought to behave. Hence by his state of nature, paradoxically enough, Locke primarily means something which is not natural or historical ... By saying, for instance, that in a state of nature men are free and equal he does not merely mean that there was a time in the past when men were, in fact, free and equal; he means rather to assert that they ought to be free and equal.
>
> — W.T. Jones, *Masters of Political Thought*, Vol. 2 (1971), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.229 (printed 211)

🔑 This converts Locke's description into a normative claim, and it is the single most useful sentence in the division for answer-writing. ⚠️ It means the state of nature is not evidence for the rights it describes; it is a device for displaying rights that are argued for on other grounds.

⚠️ 🔶 Carry the owners' dating caution with Locke throughout: the *Two Treatises* were largely composed **c.1679-1683** and published in 1689 with a 1690 title page, so the work **came to be read** as the Revolution's defence rather than being written to celebrate it.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Locke supplies the rights-first pole of every liberty question here, and the premise of every later argument about limited government.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on whether contract theory handles human rights adequately turn on the inconvenience-versus-insecurity contrast and on Jones's normative reading.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying Locke's men surrender nothing. ✅ They surrender the right to judge and punish in their own case.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Jones's **ought to be free and equal** is the line that lifts an answer from exposition to interpretation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Locke's state of nature is **not a state of constant warfare** but one of **'peace, goodwill, mutual assistance and preservation'**. (PDF p.228)
2. ✅ It is **a state of liberty, not a state of licence**.
3. ✅ **The majority of people at this stage obey the law of nature, that is the law of inward morality.**
4. ✅ Men are **by nature rational beings**, treating humanity **in every case as an end, never as a means only**.
5. ✅ **A few persons... set aside the rules of morality in pursuance of their self-interest.**
6. ✅ **If men become judges of their own cases, justice would not be secured** — the state of nature is **inconvenient**.
7. ✅ Natural law is **the rules of morality implanted in the human conscience**.
8. ✅ Natural rights are **'perfect freedom and equality'** to preserve **'his property, that is his life, liberty and estate'** and to **judge of, and punish the breaches of natural law**.
9. ✅ On contracting, men surrender only the right **to be judges in case of breaches of law**, vested now **in the community**.
10. ✅ **They still retain their natural rights to 'life, liberty and property'.**
11. ✅ The picture is **hypothetical, not a historical account**.
12. ✅ Jones: Locke's natural law is **not a description of how men do behave, but a statement of how they ought to behave**.
13. ✅ Jones: Locke **means rather to assert that they ought to be free and equal**.
14. ⚠️ 🔶 Owner caution: composition **c.1679-1683**, publication 1689 (title page 1690) — say the work **came to be read** as the Revolution's defence.

### Concept check

**Question:** Locke's state of nature is peaceful, yet men still leave it. Explain the defect that drives them out, and show how the size of that defect limits the government they create.

**Model answer:** ✅ The defect is adjudicative, not existential. ⚠️ Most men obey **the law of inward morality**, but **a few persons... set aside the rules of morality in pursuance of their self-interest**, and **in the absence of any established authority** such offenders cannot be dealt with: **if men become judges of their own cases, justice would not be secured**. ✅ The state of nature is therefore **inconvenient** rather than intolerable. ⚠️ The limit follows directly. ⚠️ Because only impartial adjudication is missing, only the right **to be judges in case of breaches of law** is surrendered, and that right passes **to the community instead of in individuals**, while men **still retain their natural rights to 'life, liberty and property'**. ⚠️ A government created to supply an umpire therefore has no title to powers beyond umpiring, which is why Locke's state is a trust and its breach licenses replacement. 🔑 Small defect, small surrender, limited state.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Locke's state of nature as a historical claim that would be refuted by anthropology. ⚠️ The chapter calls it **hypothetical**, and Jones explains that natural law is **not a description of how men do behave, but a statement of how they ought to behave**, so the device displays a normative standard rather than reporting a past condition.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Compare the Hobbesian and Lockean states of nature and show how each determines the terms of the resulting contract. *(Compare — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ For Hobbes, men moved by **appetites, desires and passions** and nearly equal in power produce **perpetual struggle** and **total insecurity**, with life **'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish and short'**; natural rights are merely **natural powers... used to oppress others**. ✅ For Locke, **rational beings** obeying **the law of inward morality** produce **'peace, goodwill, mutual assistance and preservation'**, defective only because **if men become judges of their own cases, justice would not be secured** — an **inconvenient** condition. ⚠️ The terms follow from the diagnosis. ⚠️ Hobbes's predicament is total, so the surrender is total and irrevocable, producing absolute sovereignty and no right of revolt. ⚠️ Locke's defect is partial, so only the right to judge and punish passes **to the community**, while **life, liberty and property** are retained, producing a government that is a trust and may be replaced. 🔑 The severity of the diagnosis fixes the scope of the cure.

**Rubric [10]:** both pictures with their premises **4** · the insecurity/inconvenience contrast named **2** · the differing surrender described **2** · the consequence for sovereignty and revolt **1** · a closing line linking diagnosis to cure **1**.

CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Rousseau's account as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Two books, two verdicts on the same institution

✅ Rousseau, in his ***Discourse on Inequality* (1755)**, **presents a fascinating picture of the state of nature**. ✅ He describes natural man as a **'noble savage'**, **living a life of idyllic blissfulness and primitive simplicity**; men in the state of nature **are equal, self-sufficient and contented**.

```text
  DISCOURSE ON INEQUALITY (1755)
     'noble savage' -- equal, self-sufficient, contented
            |
     "with the rise of civilization inequalities raise their head"
            |
     "with the development of arts and science, PRIVATE PROPERTY comes
      into existence, with the consequent DIVISION OF LABOUR"
            |
     "This necessitates establishment of a civil society"
            |
     VERDICT: "The state is thus an EVIL; its formation becomes
     necessary due to INEQUALITIES among men"
            |
     chapter's note: "Here Rousseau seems to ANTICIPATE MARX as regards
     the origin of the state"

  THE SOCIAL CONTRACT (1762)
     "a MODIFIED view of the civil state"
            |
     justified "not as a manifestation of the inequalities in society
      but as an INSTRUMENT FOR THE PROTECTION OF LIBERTY"
            |
     opening: "Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains."
            |
     he "immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'" and attempts
     "a justification of this 'change'"
```

🔑 The two books give opposite verdicts, and the chapter does not hide the fact — it records that Rousseau **takes a modified view** and that he **immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'**. ⚠️ That is an unusual candour about a founding text, and it is directly usable.

### 2. The anticipation of Marx, stated with care

⚠️ The chapter's sentence is **Here Rousseau seems to anticipate Marx as regards the origin of the state** — and the three words doing the limiting are **seems**, **anticipate** and **as regards the origin**.

| ⚠️ What is shared | ⚠️ What is not |
|---|---|
| Private property appears and inequality follows | Marx grounds property in the **mode of production**; Rousseau in **arts and science** |
| The state becomes necessary **because of** that inequality | Marx makes the state an instrument of a **class**; Rousseau makes it a remedy for a **condition** |
| The state is in some sense an evil | Marx predicts the state's **withering away**; Rousseau justifies the civil state as a protector of liberty |

⚠️ So the resemblance is at the point of origin only. ❌ Do not write that Rousseau is a proto-Marxist.

### 3. Where legitimate authority comes from

✅ Rousseau **seeks justification of authority in the natural agreement among men**:

> Since no man has a natural authority over other men, and since might never makes right, it follows that agreements are the basis for all legitimate authority among men.
>
> — Rousseau, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.229 (printed 211)

⚠️ The sentence is a two-premise argument with a conclusion. 🔑 **No natural authority** removes the organic theory's option; **might never makes right** removes conquest; therefore **agreement** is the only remaining ground. ⚠️ The structure is worth imitating in answers because it shows exhaustive elimination rather than assertion.

### 4. The balance sheet of the contract

✅ When men **abandon the state of nature to enter into civil society through the social contract, their loss is handsomely compensated**. ✅ **What man loses by the social contract is his natural liberty and unlimited right to all which attracts him and which he can obtain; what he gains is civil liberty and the property of what he possesses.**

| ⚠️ Column | ✅ Lost | ✅ Gained |
|---|---|---|
| Liberty | **Natural liberty** | **Civil liberty** |
| Holdings | **Unlimited right to all which attracts him and which he can obtain** | **The property of what he possesses** |

⚠️ The exchange is of a limitless but unsecured claim for a limited but secured one. 🔑 That is also, in miniature, the welfare division's later argument: a smaller formal freedom that can actually be exercised beats a larger one that cannot.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Rousseau is the hinge of the entire division and is taught again at Lessons 21 and 58; this lesson supplies the premises on which both depend.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on natural versus artificial inequality, on the origin of inequality, and on legitimate authority all begin here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Quoting **Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains** as Rousseau's conclusion. ✅ It is his opening, and he proceeds to justify the chains.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The loss-and-gain table is the fastest way to show that Rousseau's contract is an exchange rather than a surrender.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ ***Discourse on Inequality* (1755)**: natural man as a **'noble savage'**, **living a life of idyllic blissfulness and primitive simplicity**. (PDF p.229)
2. ✅ Men in the state of nature are **equal, self-sufficient and contented**.
3. ✅ **With the rise of civilization inequalities raise their head.**
4. ✅ **With the development of arts and science, private property comes into existence, with the consequent division of labour.**
5. ✅ **This necessitates establishment of a civil society.**
6. ✅ **The state is thus an evil; its formation becomes necessary due to inequalities among men.**
7. ✅ **Here Rousseau seems to anticipate Marx as regards the origin of the state.**
8. ✅ ***The Social Contract* (1762)** takes **a modified view of the civil state**, justifying it **as an instrument for the protection of liberty**.
9. ✅ **'Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains.'**
10. ✅ He **immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'** and attempts **a justification of this 'change'**.
11. ✅ **Since no man has a natural authority over other men, and since might never makes right, it follows that agreements are the basis for all legitimate authority among men.**
12. ✅ Lost: **natural liberty and unlimited right to all which attracts him and which he can obtain**.
13. ✅ Gained: **civil liberty and the property of what he possesses**.
14. 🔑 The resemblance to Marx is **as regards the origin** only; Rousseau ends by justifying the state, not by predicting its disappearance.

### Concept check

**Question:** The chapter says Rousseau "seems to anticipate Marx as regards the origin of the state". State the ground for that resemblance and three respects in which it fails.

**Model answer:** ✅ The ground is the sequence in the *Discourse on Inequality*: men are originally **equal, self-sufficient and contented**, but **with the development of arts and science, private property comes into existence, with the consequent division of labour**, which **necessitates establishment of a civil society**, so that **the state is thus an evil; its formation becomes necessary due to inequalities among men**. ⚠️ Property generates inequality, and inequality generates the state — which is Marx's order of explanation. ⚠️ It fails in three respects. ⚠️ First, Rousseau derives property from **arts and science** and civilisation, whereas Marx derives it from the development of the means of production. ⚠️ Second, Rousseau's state answers a general condition of inequality, whereas Marx's is **an instrument devised by a dominant class for its own benefit**. ⚠️ Third, Rousseau ends by justifying the civil state **as an instrument for the protection of liberty**, whereas Marx looks to the **withering away** of the state. 🔑 The same first step, three different second steps.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Rousseau's two books as one consistent doctrine. ⚠️ The chapter records that in the later work he **takes a modified view of the civil state**, and that after the famous opening he **immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'** — the tension is in the text, and noticing it is worth more than smoothing it over.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine Rousseau's account of the state of nature and the transition to civil society, and assess the consistency of his two treatments. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ In the *Discourse on Inequality* (1755) Rousseau's natural man is a **'noble savage'** living in **idyllic blissfulness and primitive simplicity**, **equal, self-sufficient and contented**. ✅ Civilisation disturbs this: **with the rise of civilization inequalities raise their head**, and **with the development of arts and science, private property comes into existence, with the consequent division of labour**, which **necessitates establishment of a civil society**. ✅ The verdict is severe — **the state is thus an evil** — and the chapter observes that here Rousseau **seems to anticipate Marx as regards the origin of the state**. ✅ In *The Social Contract* (1762) the verdict changes: the civil state is justified **not as a manifestation of the inequalities in society but as an instrument for the protection of liberty**. ⚠️ The famous opening, **'Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains'**, appears to restate the earlier charge, but Rousseau **immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'** and to justify the **change**. ✅ The justification is a two-premise elimination: **since no man has a natural authority over other men, and since might never makes right, it follows that agreements are the basis for all legitimate authority among men**. ✅ The exchange is then stated as a balance sheet — lost: **natural liberty and unlimited right**; gained: **civil liberty and the property of what he possesses**. ⚠️ The two treatments are reconcilable only by distinguishing the state's *cause* from its *justification*: it arises from a corruption, yet may be redeemed by resting on agreement. ⚠️ Rousseau does not argue that reconciliation; he sets the question aside. 🔑 An unresolved tension carried in the text itself.

**Rubric [15]:** the 1755 account with its sequence **4** · the Marx anticipation stated with its limits **2** · the 1762 reversal and the chapter's note that he sets the question aside **3** · the two-premise argument for agreement **2** · the loss-and-gain balance sheet **2** · a reasoned assessment of consistency **2**.
