## Lesson 19 — Terms of the contract — Hobbes

Progress: 19 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Hobbes entry on absolute sovereignty and the Advanced companion §5's note that liberty is surrendered for peace and protection; read against the chapter's sub-heading **TERMS OF THE CONTRACT** and its *Hobbes* sub-section at PDF p.230 (printed 212).
CA search: "Hobbes sovereign not party to the contract absolute indivisible inalienable 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the Hobbesian terms as the chapter gives them. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. One contract, and what that single number decides

✅ **Hobbes postulates a single contract by which men abandon the state of nature and establish society and state together.** ✅ Through this **historical fiction** he tries to convey **a philosophical truth—that the government does not rest on sheer force; it rests on the will of the people**.

⚠️ Pause on that last clause, because it is the opposite of what candidates expect from Hobbes. 🔑 The absolutist conclusion is reached from a popular premise: the sovereign's power is unlimited *because* it was created by everyone, not in spite of it.

```text
  ONE contract -> society AND state created TOGETHER
        |
  therefore repudiating the contract dissolves BOTH
        |
  "repudiation of the contract would result not only in an overthrow
   of the government but a disintegration of society itself"
        |
  therefore NO right to revolt; "he condemned the civil war of 1642"
        |
  therefore sovereignty is "ABSOLUTE, INDIVISIBLE and INALIENABLE"
        |
  therefore "He creates UNLIMITED POLITICAL OBLIGATION"
```

### 2. The sovereign is not a party, and why

✅ **The social contract... is concluded among the people themselves who emerge from the state of nature.** ✅ **The sovereign is not a party to the contract.** ✅ The reason is temporal: **the sovereign did not exist before the conclusion of the contract—he comes into existence as a result of this contract, hence he cannot be a party to the contract.**

✅ It is therefore **a contract of each with all and of all with each, to set up a sovereign authority**. ✅ By it **every man gave up his natural rights and powers to a 'common power' who would 'keep them in awe' and give them security**, as if every man should say to every man:

> I authorize and give up my right of governing myself to this man, or to this assembly of men, on this condition, that thou give up the right to him, and authorize all his actions in like manner.
>
> — Hobbes, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.230 (printed 212)

🔑 Two features of that formula are examinable. ⚠️ First, **authorize** — the subjects do not merely obey the sovereign's acts, they own them, which is why complaining of injury by the sovereign is complaining of self-inflicted injury. ⚠️ Second, **on this condition, that thou give up the right to him** — the condition binds the other subjects, never the sovereign, so the sovereign can breach nothing.

### 3. Why the rights cannot be taken back

✅ **All natural rights of men are surrendered to the sovereign once and for all.** ✅ **The powers conferred on him cannot be withdrawn, because if men chose to revive their natural rights, they would revert into the state of nature, characterized by anarchy and total insecurity.** ✅ **Hobbes, therefore, does not admit people's right to revolt or revolution.**

⚠️ The argument is not that resistance is wicked but that it is self-defeating: the only alternative to this sovereign is no sovereign, because the single contract created society along with the state.

### 4. The flaw the chapter itself presses

✅ **Hobbes's theory of the social contract would appear flawless only if a perfect and infallible person or assembly could be found and established as sovereign.** ✅ **But how can imperfect mortals justify the exercise of such universal and absolute authority in the real world?** ✅ **Hobbes cleverly evades this fundamental** question.

⚠️ Recognise the move. 🔑 It is the ideal-versus-actual objection from Lesson 7, used here against Hobbes exactly as it was used against the organic theory at Lesson 13 — and the chapter immediately says that **Locke tries to overcome this difficulty**, which is why Lesson 20 follows.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Hobbes supplies the strongest case for unlimited obligation, which every later limit in the chapter is constructed against.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on absolute sovereignty, on whether duty precedes rights, and on the limits of obligation route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying Hobbes's sovereign rules by force. ✅ The chapter's point is the reverse: government **rests on the will of the people**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The single-contract point explains everything else in two sentences; lead with it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Hobbes postulates a single contract** establishing **society and state together**. (PDF p.230)
2. ✅ Through this **historical fiction** he conveys that **government does not rest on sheer force; it rests on the will of the people**.
3. ✅ **The sovereign is not a party to the contract**, because he **comes into existence as a result of this contract**.
4. ✅ It is **a contract of each with all and of all with each, to set up a sovereign authority**.
5. ✅ Men give up natural rights to a **'common power'** who would **'keep them in awe'** and give them security.
6. ✅ **I authorize and give up my right of governing myself to this man, or to this assembly of men, on this condition, that thou give up the right to him, and authorize all his actions in like manner.**
7. ✅ **All natural rights of men are surrendered to the sovereign once and for all.**
8. ✅ Withdrawal would mean reverting **into the state of nature, characterized by anarchy and total insecurity**.
9. ✅ **Hobbes... does not admit people's right to revolt or revolution**; on this basis **he condemned the civil war of 1642**.
10. ✅ Since state and society arise together, repudiation **would result not only in an overthrow of the government but a disintegration of society itself**.
11. ✅ Sovereignty is therefore **absolute, indivisible and inalienable**; **he creates unlimited political obligation**.
12. ✅ The theory **would appear flawless only if a perfect and infallible person or assembly could be found**.
13. ✅ **How can imperfect mortals justify the exercise of such universal and absolute authority in the real world?**
14. 🔑 **Hobbes cleverly evades this fundamental** question — and Locke's several-stage contract is the chapter's reply.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Hobbes insists that the sovereign is not a party to the social contract, and show how that single structural feature produces his whole account of political obligation.

**Model answer:** ✅ The reason is temporal and logical: **the sovereign did not exist before the conclusion of the contract—he comes into existence as a result of this contract, hence he cannot be a party to the contract**. ⚠️ The consequences are cumulative. ⚠️ If the sovereign is not a party, he undertakes no obligations and can breach no terms, so no act of his can release the subjects. ⚠️ The agreement is instead **of each with all and of all with each**, and its condition — **that thou give up the right to him** — binds only the other subjects. ⚠️ Because each subject has **authorized** the sovereign's actions, those actions are in a sense his own. ⚠️ And because the same single contract created **society and state together**, repudiation **would result not only in an overthrow of the government but a disintegration of society itself**. ✅ Hence sovereignty is **absolute, indivisible and inalienable**, there is no **right to revolt or revolution**, and Hobbes **creates unlimited political obligation**. 🔑 One structural choice, four doctrines.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say Hobbes derives absolute authority from the sovereign's superior power or wisdom. ⚠️ The chapter states the opposite: the fiction is meant to show that **government does not rest on sheer force; it rests on the will of the people**, and the chapter's own criticism is precisely that nothing guarantees the sovereign's quality — **how can imperfect mortals justify the exercise of such universal and absolute authority in the real world?**

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does Hobbes derive absolute sovereignty from a contract made by free and equal individuals? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ He postulates **a single contract** that establishes **society and state together**, concluded **among the people themselves**, with **the sovereign... not a party** because he **comes into existence as a result of this contract**. ✅ Each says to every other: **I authorize and give up my right of governing myself to this man, or to this assembly of men, on this condition, that thou give up the right to him**. ⚠️ Three consequences follow. ✅ The surrender is **once and for all**, since reviving natural rights would return men to **anarchy and total insecurity**. ✅ Repudiation would dissolve society as well as government. ✅ Therefore sovereignty is **absolute, indivisible and inalienable** and there is no **right to revolt or revolution**. ⚠️ The derivation is valid but conditional: it **would appear flawless only if a perfect and infallible person or assembly** could be found. 🔑 Popular origin, absolute outcome.

**Rubric [10]:** the single contract and its joint creation of society and state **3** · the sovereign's non-party status with its reason **2** · the authorisation formula **2** · the irreversibility argument **2** · the chapter's own objection noted **1**.

## Lesson 20 — Terms of the contract — Locke

Progress: 20 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Locke entry on constitutional government and resistance, and the Advanced companion §5's note that the state becomes a trust limited by rights; read against the chapter's *Locke* sub-section under **TERMS OF THE CONTRACT** at PDF pp.231-232 (printed 213-214), including the two Waldron passages reproduced there.
CA search: "Locke trust government right to revolution consent property taxation 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the Lockean terms as the chapter gives them. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Several stages instead of one

✅ **Locke tries to overcome this difficulty by postulating several stages of the contract.** ✅ The chapter reproduces Jeremy Waldron's statement of them:

> Contract and consent have three stages in Locke's description: first, men must agree unanimously to come together as a community and pool their natural powers so that they can act together to uphold one another's rights; second, the members of this community must agree by a majority vote to set up legislative and other institutions; third, the owners of property in a society must agree, either personally or through their representatives to whatever taxes are imposed on the people.
>
> — Jeremy Waldron, in *The Blackwell Encyclopaedia of Political Thought*, ed. David Miller (1987), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.231 (printed 213)

```text
  STAGE 1   UNANIMOUS agreement to form a COMMUNITY and pool natural
            powers "to uphold one another's rights"
               |
  STAGE 2   MAJORITY vote to set up "legislative and other institutions"
               |
  STAGE 3   CONSENT of property-owners, personally or through
            representatives, "to whatever taxes are imposed"
```

⚠️ The decision rules differ by stage — unanimity, then majority, then consent of the taxed — and that gradation is the architecture of constitutional government in miniature. 🔑 Nothing is decided by the same rule twice, so no single body holds the whole power.

### 2. The structural consequence: society outlives government

✅ **This theory of several stages of the contract marks a clear departure from Hobbes's position.** ✅ **Hobbes postulates that society and state are formed together; they are dissolved together.** ✅ **On the contrary, Locke believes that society and state were created in different steps: creation of society is the primary step; setting up of the government is a secondary step.** ✅ **So, if the government is dissolved, society does not disintegrate. Order will be restored by setting up another government in its place.**

🔑 This is the single most consequential sentence in the division. ⚠️ Hobbes's prohibition on revolt rested entirely on the claim that losing the government means losing society; Locke removes the premise, and the prohibition falls with it.

### 3. Government as a trust

✅ **In Locke's system of thought the nature of government resembles a 'trust'.** ✅ **In other words, a government, like a trust, is bound to act within the terms of its constitution.** ✅ By distinguishing the formation of society from that of the state, **Locke places government under the control of society**, which **leaves no scope for absolutism**. ✅ Waldron again:

> Absolutism of the kind Thomas Hobbes envisaged is ruled out on the grounds that people hold their natural rights to life and liberty as a sort of trust from God and therefore cannot transfer them to the arbitrary power of another. Since government is set up to protect property and other rights, and not to undermine them, the government may not take or redistribute property without consent!
>
> — Waldron, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.231 (printed 213)

| ⚠️ Question | ✅ Hobbes | ✅ Locke |
|---|---|---|
| Surrender | **Unconditional** | **Conditional and partial** |
| What is given up | All natural rights, **once and for all** | Only the right **to judge of and punish the offenders of natural law** |
| Why not more | — | Some natural rights **are fundamental; they cannot be given up because they are the cornerstone of human freedom** |
| Who holds the surrendered power | The sovereign | The **community, 'common power' or the government**, which **assumes the role of 'umpire'** |
| Condition attached | None binding the sovereign | That rights to **life, liberty and property** will be **protected and kept intact** |
| Who supervises | Nobody | **Society retains the authority to watch whether the government is exercising its powers strictly in accordance with the terms of the contract** |

### 4. Two implications, and the property argument

✅ The chapter draws out two implications in its own numbering. ✅ **(a)** Government **must govern with the 'consent of the people'**: it is the people **who are endowed with the faculty of 'reason'—conscience, sense of morality, knowledge of right and wrong**; government, **as an artificial device**, **cannot embody 'superior reason'**. ✅ **(b)** **If in any case the government falters from its duty, the people have the right to overthrow that government and set up another government in its place.** ✅ **Thus, Locke recognizes the people's right to revolution**, on which basis **he justifies the 'bloodless' or 'Glorious' revolution of 1688, which had led to the flight of James II from England and the establishment of William and Mary on the throne.**

✅ On property: **Locke treats 'right to property' as a fundamental natural right which cannot be surrendered to the government.** ✅ He proceeds on the assumption that property consists in one's **'fruit of labour'**: when an individual **mixes his labour with natural resources, this act is sufficient to establish his exclusive right on the product which does not depend on the consent of other people**. ✅ His theory of taxation follows: **the government should take only what is necessary to carry on its business**, and **has no power to take anything more without the owner's consent**.

⚠️ 🔶 Carry the owners' dating caution: the *Two Treatises* were largely composed **c.1679-1683**; the work **came to be read** as the Revolution's vindication after publication in 1689.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Locke supplies constitutionalism, consent, resistance and the labour theory of property in four compact moves.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on limited government, on the right to resist, on taxation by consent and on whether rights precede the state route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Locke's people surrender nothing, or that the right to revolution is unconditional. ✅ It is triggered only when government **falters from its duty**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The society-outlives-government point is the cleanest refutation of Hobbes available in the chapter.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Locke postulates **several stages of the contract**. (PDF p.231)
2. ✅ Waldron's three stages: **unanimous** community formation; **majority** establishment of institutions; property-owners' consent **to whatever taxes are imposed**.
3. ✅ Hobbes forms society and state together and dissolves them together; **Locke believes that society and state were created in different steps**.
4. 🔑 **If the government is dissolved, society does not disintegrate. Order will be restored by setting up another government in its place.**
5. ✅ Government **resembles a 'trust'** and **is bound to act within the terms of its constitution**.
6. ✅ Locke **places government under the control of society**, which **leaves no scope for absolutism**.
7. ✅ Waldron: people hold natural rights **as a sort of trust from God** and **cannot transfer them to the arbitrary power of another**.
8. ✅ **The government may not take or redistribute property without consent.**
9. ✅ Locke postulates **conditional and partial surrender**, because some natural rights **are the cornerstone of human freedom**.
10. ✅ What is given up: the right **to judge of and punish the offenders of natural law**; the recipient **assumes the role of 'umpire'**.
11. ✅ Implication (a): government must govern with the **'consent of the people'**, who hold **the faculty of 'reason'**; an artificial device **cannot embody 'superior reason'**.
12. ✅ Implication (b): **the people have the right to overthrow that government** — Locke **recognizes the people's right to revolution**.
13. ✅ He justifies the **'bloodless' or 'Glorious' revolution of 1688**, the **flight of James II** and the accession of **William and Mary**.
14. ✅ Property is the **'fruit of labour'**; mixing labour with natural resources establishes an exclusive right **which does not depend on the consent of other people**; taxation beyond necessity requires **the owner's consent**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Locke's contract has several stages. Explain how multiplying the stages defeats Hobbesian absolutism, and identify the specific premise of Hobbes's argument that is thereby removed.

**Model answer:** ✅ The stages are three: **unanimous** agreement to form a community pooling natural powers **to uphold one another's rights**; a **majority vote to set up legislative and other institutions**; and consent of property-owners **to whatever taxes are imposed**. ⚠️ Multiplying the stages separates the creation of society from the creation of government, so that **creation of society is the primary step; setting up of the government is a secondary step**. ✅ Consequently **if the government is dissolved, society does not disintegrate** and **order will be restored by setting up another government in its place**. ⚠️ The premise removed is precisely Hobbes's: he held that the single contract made society and state together, so that **repudiation of the contract would result not only in an overthrow of the government but a disintegration of society itself**. ⚠️ Once that is false, resistance no longer entails a return to the state of nature, and the prohibition on revolt loses its ground. ✅ Government becomes a **'trust'** supervised by a society that **retains the authority to watch** it. 🔑 Separate the two creations and absolutism has no premise left.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present Locke's right of revolution as a general licence. ⚠️ It arises only **if in any case the government falters from its duty**, and the duty is fixed by the trust — protecting **life, liberty and property**. ⚠️ The chapter also records that Locke was **not the sworn enemy of monarchy**; he sought to found it **'in the consent of the people'**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain how Locke converts the social contract into a theory of limited government. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ By three devices. ✅ First, he postulates **several stages of the contract** — unanimous community formation, majority establishment of institutions, and taxation by the consent of property-owners — so that no single act creates the whole of political power. ✅ Second, he separates society from government, so that **if the government is dissolved, society does not disintegrate**, and government is placed **under the control of society**, which **leaves no scope for absolutism**. ✅ Third, he makes the surrender **conditional and partial**: only the right **to judge of and punish the offenders of natural law** passes to the community as **'umpire'**, while **life, liberty and property** remain. ✅ Government accordingly **resembles a 'trust'**, must govern with the **consent of the people**, **may not take or redistribute property without consent**, and may be replaced when it **falters from its duty**. 🔑 Limited powers, retained rights, supervised trust.

**Rubric [10]:** the three stages **3** · the society/government separation with its consequence **2** · partial surrender and retained rights **2** · the trust and consent requirements **2** · the conditional right of revolution **1**.

## Lesson 21 — Terms of the contract — Rousseau and the general will

Progress: 21 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Rousseau entry on popular sovereignty and the Advanced companion §5's note that the general will can absorb individual dissent; read against the chapter's *Rousseau* sub-section under **TERMS OF THE CONTRACT** at PDF pp.232-234 (printed 214-216), including the MacIver and Patrick Riley passages reproduced there.
CA search: "general will popular sovereignty forced to be free Rousseau interpretation 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the general will as the chapter presents it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Hobbes's structure, inverted

✅ **Rousseau, like Hobbes, postulates a single contract, and thereby creates absolute, indivisible and inalienable sovereignty.** ✅ **But Rousseau distinguishes himself as an exponent of popular sovereignty.** ✅ Sovereignty **is not vested in a ruler apart from society itself as Hobbes had assumed; instead, it is vested in the people themselves**.

✅ When people contract, **they relinquish their natural rights in their individual capacity; they surrender these rights to their collective whole**. ✅ **Thus, what they lose in their individual capacity, they get back in their corporate capacity, in improved form.** ✅ **No one is a loser in the bargain. Everybody is a gainer, because when any one is attacked, society as a whole comes to his rescue.** ✅ **Sovereignty is indivisible, yet it is shared by each member of the civil society.** ✅ In Rousseau's words:

> Each of us puts his person and all his power in common under the supreme direction of the general will, and in our corporate capacity we receive each member as an indivisible part of the whole.
>
> — Rousseau, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.232 (printed 214)

✅ MacIver's gloss states the inversion exactly: **The secret of Rousseau's doctrine is found in the substitution, for a sovereign, of the sovereign. His sovereign is the 'general will', and he is perfectly ready to apply to it all the sweeping attributes which Hobbes delivers to his 'one man or assembly of men.' It too is one and indivisible, inerrant, indestructible, omnipotent.**

🔑 Same absolutism, different holder. ⚠️ That is both Rousseau's achievement — **the creation of popular sovereignty by vesting it in the general will is a unique contribution... which laid the foundations of modern democracy** — and the source of every subsequent worry about him.

### 2. Why natural liberty had to be given up

✅ Rousseau **maintains that liberty in the state of nature is a great boon**. ✅ But **when population increases and the treasures of nature start depleting, it is no longer possible for men to enjoy natural liberty as before**. ✅ **When the forces of nature no longer sustain men, they have to consolidate their own force to save themselves.** ✅ **They, therefore, create the civil society to maintain their freedom.**

✅ MacIver again: **The precarious liberty of the state of nature is well lost for the assured and enlarged liberty of the social order.** ✅ The idea that **law was not merely consistent with liberty, not merely a possible guardian of it, but the very form of its realization, was of profound importance for the true interpretation of the state**. ✅ It **gave a new and most significant setting to the problem of political obligation**: the problem **solves itself when government is vested in the true sovereign, the 'general will'**, so that **each, coalescing with all, may nevertheless obey only himself, and remain as free as before**.

🔑 Law as **the very form of** liberty's realisation is the hinge that will reappear in Green at Lesson 30 and in the whole welfare division.

### 3. Forced to be free, and the two wills

✅ **When man acts in the state against his own will under the direction of the general will, he is not losing his freedom.** ✅ **In fact, the constraint of the general will is instrumental for securing his larger and ultimate freedom, which reconciles freedom of each with freedom of all.** ✅ Thus Rousseau postulates a condition when **'man can be forced to be free.'**

✅ Patrick Riley is quoted on the concept: **Rousseau himself insists that 'the general will is always right', that it is 'the will that one has as a citizen'—when one thinks of the common good and not of one's own particular will as a private person.**

✅ Subsequent writers have used the distinction between **actual will** and **real will** to explicate Rousseau's distinction between **particular will** and **general will**:

| ⚠️ Feature | ✅ Actual will | ✅ Real will |
|---|---|---|
| Motive | **Immediate, selfish interest** | **Ultimate, collective interest** |
| Which self it expresses | The **ordinary 'self'** | The **'better self'** |
| What it prompts | **Gratification of his desires** | **Acts of reason** |
| Stability | **Transient, unstable and inconsistent**; it **changes from moment to moment** | **Stable, constant, consistent and determinate** |

✅ **Man's freedom consists in overcoming his actual will and following the direction of the real will.** ✅ **Real will expresses his true freedom**, subordinating self-interest to **common interest or common good—which is shared by each and all**.

✅ But **individual by himself is imperfect**: **at times he may not be able to discriminate between his actual and real will**. ✅ **This dilemma is resolved by the transition from the 'particular' to the 'general' will.** ✅ **The general will harmonizes the interests of each with those of all.** ✅ **It is not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor, but an expression of the highest in every man.** ✅ **It is the spirit of citizenship in its concrete shape.** ✅ **Man's particular will may create confusion; but the general will always shows him the right way.**

### 4. The drift the chapter records

✅ **In this line of argument, Rousseau travels much ahead of his original position.** ✅ **He starts with describing civil society as an expression of 'superior will' but ends with treating it as an expression of 'superior reason'.** ✅ **He starts with the mechanistic view of the state but ends with the organic view of the state.** ✅ **He starts as an ardent liberal but ends as an ardent idealist.** ✅ **Rousseau was the most brilliant writer of his time but he is also the most confusing. Liberals and idealists adore him alike; they also condemn him alike.**

⚠️ Four drifts in four clauses. 🔑 This is the chapter's own verdict, and it is why Lesson 58 returns to Rousseau as the hinge of the whole chapter.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The general will is the chapter's bridge from liberal contract to idealist state, and every later criticism of the organic theory touches it.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on popular sovereignty, on freedom as obedience, and on the dangers of collective will route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating the general will as majority opinion. ✅ It is **not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor** but **an expression of the highest in every man**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The four-clause drift sentence is the most quotable judgment in the division; use it to close.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Rousseau postulates **a single contract**, creating **absolute, indivisible and inalienable** sovereignty — but **popular**. (PDF p.232)
2. ✅ Rights are relinquished **in their individual capacity** and surrendered **to their collective whole**; what is lost individually is regained **in their corporate capacity, in improved form**.
3. ✅ **Each of us puts his person and all his power in common under the supreme direction of the general will.**
4. ✅ MacIver: the substitution **for a sovereign, of the sovereign**; the general will is **one and indivisible, inerrant, indestructible, omnipotent**.
5. ✅ Popular sovereignty is **a unique contribution of Rousseau which laid the foundations of modern democracy**.
6. ✅ Natural liberty becomes unsustainable as **population increases and the treasures of nature start depleting**.
7. ✅ MacIver: **the precarious liberty of the state of nature is well lost for the assured and enlarged liberty of the social order**; law is **the very form of its realization**.
8. ✅ **Each, coalescing with all, may nevertheless obey only himself, and remain as free as before.**
9. ✅ Constraint by the general will secures **his larger and ultimate freedom**: **'man can be forced to be free.'**
10. ✅ Riley: **'the general will is always right'**; it is **'the will that one has as a citizen'**.
11. ✅ Actual will — immediate, selfish, **transient, unstable and inconsistent**; real will — ultimate, collective, **stable, constant, consistent and determinate**.
12. ✅ The general will **is not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor, but an expression of the highest in every man**; **the spirit of citizenship in its concrete shape**.
13. 🔑 Rousseau **starts with the mechanistic view of the state but ends with the organic view**; **starts as an ardent liberal but ends as an ardent idealist**.
14. ✅ **Liberals and idealists adore him alike; they also condemn him alike.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how Rousseau can claim that a man compelled by the general will remains free, and state the structural feature of his contract that makes the claim possible and also makes it dangerous.

**Model answer:** ✅ The claim rests on the two-wills analysis. ✅ Man has an **actual will** motivated by **immediate, selfish interest**, **transient, unstable and inconsistent**, and a **real will** motivated by **ultimate, collective interest**, **stable, constant, consistent and determinate**; **man's freedom consists in overcoming his actual will and following the direction of the real will**. ⚠️ Since the general will **harmonizes the interests of each with those of all** and is **an expression of the highest in every man**, constraint by it enforces what the person most truly wills, so **the constraint of the general will is instrumental for securing his larger and ultimate freedom**. ✅ Hence **'man can be forced to be free'**. ⚠️ The enabling feature is the single contract that creates **absolute, indivisible and inalienable** sovereignty, merely relocating it from a ruler to the people — MacIver's **substitution, for a sovereign, of the sovereign**. ⚠️ That is why it is dangerous: all of Hobbes's **sweeping attributes** survive, now attached to a will whose content nobody can be outvoted about. 🔑 Absolutism with a new address.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not equate the general will with the will of the majority or with the sum of private preferences. ⚠️ The chapter is explicit that it **is not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor**, and Riley's gloss fixes it as **'the will that one has as a citizen'** when thinking of the common good rather than as a private person.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine Rousseau's doctrine of the general will and assess the chapter's judgment that he begins as a liberal and ends as an idealist. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Rousseau retains Hobbes's single contract and its **absolute, indivisible and inalienable** sovereignty, but vests it **in the people themselves**: each **puts his person and all his power in common under the supreme direction of the general will**, so that what is lost individually returns **in their corporate capacity, in improved form**. ✅ MacIver names the move the **substitution, for a sovereign, of the sovereign**, with the general will inheriting Hobbes's **sweeping attributes** — **one and indivisible, inerrant, indestructible, omnipotent**. ✅ The doctrine is explicated through two wills: the **actual will** of immediate selfish interest, **transient, unstable and inconsistent**, and the **real will** of ultimate collective interest, **stable, constant, consistent and determinate**. ✅ Since the general will is **an expression of the highest in every man** and **the spirit of citizenship in its concrete shape**, compulsion by it secures a **larger and ultimate freedom**, so that **man can be forced to be free**. ⚠️ The chapter's judgment is well founded. ⚠️ A liberal contract theory justifies authority by the consent of separate individuals and measures it by their retained rights; Rousseau begins there but ends by making the citizen's true self the one that already wills what the collective wills — so dissent becomes error rather than disagreement. ✅ Hence he **starts with describing civil society as an expression of 'superior will' but ends with treating it as an expression of 'superior reason'**, and **starts with the mechanistic view of the state but ends with the organic view**. ⚠️ The gain is real: popular sovereignty **laid the foundations of modern democracy**. 🔑 Democratic in its holder of power, idealist in its theory of the self.

**Rubric [15]:** the popular inversion of Hobbes with MacIver's gloss **3** · the corporate-capacity exchange **2** · the two-wills analysis with the four contrasts **3** · the forced-to-be-free derivation **2** · a reasoned assessment of the liberal-to-idealist drift **4** · a balanced closing judgment **1**.

## Lesson 22 — A critical appraisal of the social contract

Progress: 22 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's contract traps and the Advanced companion §7's second entry, which records that the contract narrative is criticised for weak history and weak logic; read against the chapter's sub-heading **A CRITICAL APPRAISAL** at PDF pp.234-236 (printed 216-218), including the Hume, Maine, Mayflower, Burke and Paine material.
CA search: "criticism of social contract theory Hume Maine historical evidence 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's appraisal. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The verdict, then seven grounds

✅ **The social contract theory of the origin of the state has been described as 'bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy'.** ✅ **It has been subjected to searching criticism from various angles.**

```text
  1. HUME        government arose because people saw it was to their
                 advantage to support any authority enforcing justice;
                 the contract was "historically improbable and
                 philosophically unnecessary to explain allegiance";
                 SCRUTON: "the criterion of TACIT CONSENT is
                 inapplicable", most people "constrained by cultural,
                 linguistic and habitual ties to stay where they are"
  2. NO EVIDENCE  no historical validity for a state of nature followed
                 by mutual agreement; the MAYFLOWER PACT 1620 "cannot be
                 treated as an evidence" -- it was drawn by men "already
                 familiar with government"
  3. MAINE       research on early institutions found no deliberate
                 contract; the movement has been "from one of 'status'
                 to one of 'contract'" -- the reverse direction
  4. BAD LOGIC   contract is "an individualist idea"; primitive man was
                 "not at all aware of this idea"; life was governed by
                 CUSTOM at the levels of "family, clan, phratry, tribe,
                 gen"
  5. NO SANCTION  a valid contract "requires the force or sanction of the
                 state, which exists above and apart from the contracting
                 parties" -- the social contract "precedes the
                 establishment of the state itself"
  6. BURKE       rights "are the product of a developed social
                 consciousness and are enforced by institutionalized
                 law"; the contract, if it existed, "certainly marked the
                 SURRENDER of natural rights"
  7. PAINE       no contract is "eternally binding on all generations";
                 "a dead weight on the wheel of progress"; "Every age and
                 generation must be as free to act for itself..."
```

⚠️ Seven grounds, and they are not of one kind. 🔑 Grounds 2 and 3 are historical, 4 and 5 are logical, 6 and 7 are philosophical — which is exactly the tripartite charge in the opening quotation. ⚠️ An answer that sorts them this way is instantly better organised than one that lists them.

### 2. The three strongest, examined

⚠️ **Hume's.** ✅ Government **arose because people realized that it was to their advantage to support any authority that enforced the rules of justice effectively**, so the contract is **both historically improbable and philosophically unnecessary to explain allegiance**. ✅ Roger Scruton's elucidation adds the modern form: **the criterion of tacit consent is inapplicable, most people being inevitably constrained by cultural, linguistic and habitual ties to stay where they are, whatever the government that should exert jurisdiction over them**. 🔑 Staying is not consenting if leaving was never a real option.

⚠️ **Maine's.** ✅ Sir Henry Maine's **historical research on early political institutions has found no evidence to show that social and political organization owes its origin to any deliberate contract among men**. ✅ His theory is that **the movement has been from one of 'status' to one of 'contract'**: **in primitive society, membership of a social group determined the status of an individual**, and **the idea of a contract began with the dawn of the modern era, when the individual could change his status through a free contract**. ⚠️ So **to shift the modern idea of the contract to the earliest times is... bad logic**: the explanandum has been imported into the explanans.

⚠️ **The sanction objection.** ✅ **A contract, in order to be valid, requires the force or sanction of the state, which exists above and apart from the contracting parties.** ✅ **The so-called social contract had no such sanction behind it, because it precedes the establishment of the state itself.** ⚠️ This is the cleanest of the seven because it is purely internal: the theory needs the institution it claims to create.

### 3. The social objection, and the historical function

✅ Then a different kind of criticism, pointing forward to the class and feminist divisions. ✅ The theory **tries to demonstrate that the state is the product of the will of all individuals comprising the state** and **establishes the state as an instrument of harmonizing the interests of all individuals and all sections of society**. ✅ **But, in any society, particularly in modern capitalist society, some dominant sections or the chosen few are so well-organized and vocal that they become self-styled representatives of the will of society, and seek to justify their authority on this ground.** ✅ **The complex problem of social inequality and injustice needs a more searching analysis. It cannot be solved through a simplistic formula of the 'social contract'.**

✅ Finally the historical verdict that closes the branch:

> It is significant that the theory of the social contract was advanced at a juncture when the system of feudal relations was giving way to the norms of market society, which laid the foundations of the capitalist system. The relations of the feudal society are determined by tradition; those of the capitalist society are determined by contract. The doctrine of the social contract played a historical role by providing for a theoretical justification for the new pattern of human relations necessitated by the emergence of the capitalist society.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.236 (printed 218)

🔑 Tradition versus contract is the chapter's compressed sociology of the whole doctrine. ⚠️ It also explains why the theory keeps returning: as long as market relations remain the paradigm of legitimate dealing, the contract will look like the natural model of political authority.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the ready-made critical half of every contract-theory answer, and the three-way sort maps directly onto the opening quotation.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems asking whether contract theory addresses rights adequately should use Burke's objection and the dominant-sections objection together.
- ❌ **Trap.** Using the Mayflower Pact as evidence for the theory. ✅ The chapter uses it as evidence against: its signatories were **already familiar with government**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Close on the feudal-to-capitalist function; it converts a list of objections into a historical explanation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The theory has been described as **'bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy'**. (PDF p.234)
2. ✅ **David Hume (1711-76)**: government **arose because people realized that it was to their advantage to support any authority that enforced the rules of justice effectively**.
3. ✅ The contract was **both historically improbable and philosophically unnecessary to explain allegiance**.
4. ✅ Scruton: **the criterion of tacit consent is inapplicable**, people being constrained by **cultural, linguistic and habitual ties to stay where they are**.
5. ✅ The **Mayflower Pact of 1620** is **often cited** but **cannot be treated as an evidence**: its signatories were **already familiar with government**.
6. ✅ **Sir Henry Maine**: no evidence of any **deliberate contract**; the movement has been **from one of 'status' to one of 'contract'**.
7. ✅ Contract is **an individualist idea**; primitive life was governed by **custom** at the levels of **family, clan, phratry, tribe, gen**.
8. ✅ Law, sovereignty and political institutions grew **in a very slow and gradual manner, not by a compact of freely contracting individuals**.
9. ✅ A valid contract **requires the force or sanction of the state**, which the social contract **precedes**.
10. ✅ Rights **are the product of a developed social consciousness and are enforced by institutionalized law**; **Burke (1729-97)** held the contract **marked the surrender of natural rights**.
11. ✅ **Tom Paine (1737-1809)**: the theory is **a dead weight on the wheel of progress**; **Every age and generation must be as free to act for itself in all cases as the age and generations which preceded it.**
12. ✅ **Dominant sections... become self-styled representatives of the will of society**; inequality **cannot be solved through a simplistic formula of the 'social contract'**.
13. ✅ **The relations of the feudal society are determined by tradition; those of the capitalist society are determined by contract.**
14. 🔑 The doctrine **played a historical role by providing for a theoretical justification for the new pattern of human relations necessitated by the emergence of the capitalist society**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The social contract is charged with being "bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy". Assign at least one of the chapter's objections to each head and explain why it belongs there.

**Model answer:** ✅ **Bad history:** Maine's research **found no evidence to show that social and political organization owes its origin to any deliberate contract among men**, and the movement has in fact been **from one of 'status' to one of 'contract'**; the **Mayflower Pact of 1620** fails as evidence because its signatories were **already familiar with government**. ⚠️ These are empirical claims about the past. ✅ **Bad logic:** a contract **requires the force or sanction of the state, which exists above and apart from the contracting parties**, yet the social contract **precedes the establishment of the state itself**; and the idea of contract is **an individualist idea** of which **primitive man was not at all aware**. ⚠️ These are internal inconsistencies, true independently of any evidence. ✅ **Bad philosophy:** Burke holds that rights **are the product of a developed social consciousness** and cannot pre-exist law; Paine holds that no contract can be **eternally binding on all generations**. ⚠️ These attack the normative work the theory is doing. 🔑 Three charges, three kinds of defect.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Hume's objection as merely historical. ⚠️ He says the contract is **both historically improbable and philosophically unnecessary to explain allegiance**, and Scruton's gloss that **tacit consent is inapplicable** is conceptual: it denies that remaining within a jurisdiction can count as consenting at all.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Evaluate whether the social contract theory adequately explains the origin and the authority of the state. *(Evaluate — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ The theory explains authority better than origin, and even its account of authority is incomplete. ✅ On origin it is **based on speculation**, tracing the state **through logic, not through historical or scientific evidence**; the chapter endorses the verdict **'bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy'**. ✅ Maine found **no evidence** of a deliberate contract and showed the movement running **from one of 'status' to one of 'contract'**; the **Mayflower Pact of 1620** fails because its signatories were **already familiar with government**; and the device is circular, since a contract **requires the force or sanction of the state** it purports to create. ✅ On authority the theory is stronger: it makes government answerable to the purposes for which it was made, yields Locke's **trust**, consent and right of replacement, and yields Rousseau's popular sovereignty, which **laid the foundations of modern democracy**. ⚠️ Yet the authority account also fails twice. ✅ Hume shows that **tacit consent is inapplicable** where people are **constrained by cultural, linguistic and habitual ties**; Paine shows that no generation can bind the next, each being **as free to act for itself**. ✅ And the harmonising claim conceals power: **dominant sections** become **self-styled representatives of the will of society**, so that **the complex problem of social inequality and injustice... cannot be solved through a simplistic formula**. ⚠️ What survives is the regulative idea that authority must be justified to those subject to it. 🔑 A false history that yielded a durable standard.

**Rubric [15]:** the speculative status conceded with the chapter's phrase **2** · at least three historical or logical objections with names **4** · the genuine achievements on the authority side **3** · Hume and Paine on consent and generations **3** · the dominant-sections objection **2** · a qualified verdict naming what survives **1**.

## Lesson 23 — Laissez-faire individualism and Adam Smith

Progress: 23 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's laissez-faire definition and §6's Smith entry; with the Advanced companion §4's "necessary evil / night-watchman" definition, read against the chapter's sub-heading **THEORY OF LAISSEZ-FAIRE INDIVIDUALISM** at PDF pp.236-239 (printed 218-221), the displaced-geometry page PDF p.237 and the narrow-column spill at PDF p.238, together with the boxed definition of **Mercantilism** at PDF p.239.
CA search: "invisible hand natural liberty minimal state functions classical economics 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of laissez-faire. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The doctrine, in the chapter's own order

✅ Laissez-faire individualism **was developed by classical liberalism—which started taking shape in the eighteenth century and was systematically formulated in the nineteenth century**. ✅ It **placed individual at the centre of its philosophy** and argued that the individual **is endowed with the faculty of 'reason' which enables him to find what is most conducive to his interests**.

✅ From reason, four freedoms: **freedom of trade, freedom of contract, freedom to bargain and freedom of enterprise**. ✅ **It postulated private property as the condition of progress, because property was viewed as a product of individual's labour, ingenuity and enterprise.** ✅ Since all these freedoms **ensued from the element of 'reason', they were regarded valuable for society**; therefore **the profit motive of the individuals and their open competition were... regarded as 'functional' and, hence, conducive to social progress**.

✅ Government's function is accordingly **to protect individual's freedom or liberty, to enforce contracts, to guarantee peaceful employment of property and to provide the external conditions of law and order**.

✅ **Laissez-faire**, **a French term which means 'leave alone'**, **signified non-intervention by the state in the economic activities of individuals**; the phrase **was in common usage in mercantile and industrial circles in nineteenth century England**. ✅ The doctrine **regards property rights of the individual as a necessary condition of liberty**, and **seeks to set definite and circumscribed limits on the regulatory powers vested in the government over social and economic processes**.

✅ Hence the famous label, with its reason attached: the state is **a 'necessary evil'** — **it is evil because it imposes regulations and restricts the freedom of the individual, yet it is necessary because, without its regulation, the freedom of the individual cannot be safeguarded**.

🔑 The chain is **reason → freedoms → property → functional profit → minimal government**. ⚠️ Attack any link and the conclusion fails, which is how the welfare division proceeds at Lesson 26.

### 2. The exponents, and one that does not fit

✅ The exponents **include Adam Smith (1723-90), Jeremy Bentham (1748-1832), James Mill (1773-1836) and Herbert Spencer (1820-1903), British economists and political thinkers**. ✅ **Besides, John Stuart Mill (1806-73)... made an important contribution to the theory of laissez-faire individualism, but he sought to transform it from negative liberalism into positive liberalism, and thereby made a unique contribution to liberal theory.**

⚠️ That sentence is the chapter's own bridge to division III. 🔑 J.S. Mill is introduced inside the laissez-faire section and taught inside the welfare section, which is why Lesson 28 belongs where it does.

### 3. Adam Smith, in four movements

| ✅ Movement | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| **Influence of Physiocrats** | A **French school of economic thought which flourished during the eighteenth century**, holding, **deriving ultimately from Rousseau**, **the goodness and bounty of nature and the goodness of man**. The aim of government **should be to conform to nature**: so long as men do not **interfere with each other's liberty** or **combine among themselves in order to encroach upon the liberty of others**, governments **should leave them free to find their own salvation**. **Criminals, mad men, and monopolists should be eliminated.** From this followed **free trade between nations on grounds of both justice and economy**: the greater the competition, the more each will **economize on the cost of his labour to the general advantage**. ⚠️ Smith **sought to eliminate their errors**, rejecting **that agriculture was the sole source of the wealth produced** and holding that **commerce and industry, as well as agriculture, were the source of wealth** |
| **Concept of economic man** | Everyone has **a natural propensity to trade**; given **a free rein**, this **would stimulate economic activity**. The **profit motive is a natural instinct**, and **the selfish motive of the enterpriser is, nevertheless, conducive to promotion of the general good**, harmonising with **national prosperity, thereby benefiting all—government, business and labour**. ⚠️ Nineteenth-century critics used the **term 'economic man' to deprecate this view of human nature**, accusing **Adam Smith and other classical economists of having grossly distorted the true nature of man** by assuming that **virtually all economic activity must be motivated by purely selfish considerations**. ✅ But **Adam Smith never gave a blanket endorsement to the idea of beneficence of self-interest**; he held that in pursuing his own interests man **on occasion was 'led by an invisible hand to promote an end which was no part of his intention'**. ✅ The formulation was **soon refined through the instrumentality of hedonistic psychology**, accepted by **the Utilitarians—Jeremy Bentham and his followers**, who regarded man as **a highly rational creature who persistently endeavoured by means of the hedonistic calculus, to maximize pleasure and minimize pain** |
| **Concept of natural liberty** | A system of **'natural liberty'**, **implying perfect freedom of commerce and industry**. **The businessman knows his own interests far better than any government can tell him**, so **the only wise policy for a government to follow is laissez-faire**. ✅ In *Wealth of Nations*: **Every man, as long as he does not violate the laws of justice, is left perfectly free to pursue his own interest in his own way, and to bring both his industry and capital into competition with those of any other man or order of men. The sovereign is completely discharged from... the duty of superintending the industry of the private people, and of directing it toward the employments most suitable to the interest of the society** |
| **Functions of government** | **Three duties of great importance**: (a) **the defence of the nation against foreign aggression**; (b) **the protection of every member of society, as far as possible, from the injustice or oppression of every other member of it, i.e. establishing an exact administration of justice**; and (c) **the erection and maintenance of public works and running certain public institutions which could not be undertaken by an individual or a small number of individuals because the profit accruing from their maintenance would never repay the expenditure involved** |

✅ Smith's *Inquiry into the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations* (**1776**) **became the great source of the ideas and policies concerning laissez-faire individualism**; he **advocated the abolition of restrictions imposed on commerce and industry... in pursuance of the policy of mercantilism**, and urged that **all producers should be free to compete in a free market**.

⚠️ Duty (c) deserves emphasis. 🔑 Smith's own system contains a public-goods exception defined by unprofitability — so even the founding text of laissez-faire concedes a class of activities the market will not perform.

### 4. The box: Mercantilism

✅ The chapter supplies a boxed definition at PDF p.239 (printed 221), and it is the system Smith was arguing against:

> An economic theory prevailing in seventeenth century and early eighteenth century Europe which held that a nation's power was dependent on national prosperity. National prosperity could be enhanced by maximizing exports and minimizing imports. A nation should import cheap raw materials and export expensive finished goods. Mercantilism upheld the state's intervention in economy for the protection of indigenous industries. It recommended lowering of wages to keep the prices of its products quite low in the world market.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, boxed definition, PDF p.239 (printed 221)

⚠️ Note what the box makes visible. 🔑 Laissez-faire was not proposed against an absent state but against a very active one that intervened on behalf of producers and **recommended lowering of wages**. ⚠️ The historical target was an interventionism that already redistributed upward — which is exactly the point welfare liberals will later press in the other direction.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The **necessary evil** formula and Smith's three duties are the standard content of any minimal-state answer.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on negative liberty, on the state's economic role, or on whether the state is a necessary evil route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying Smith wanted no state economic role. ✅ Duty (c) is an explicit public-works exception.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use the mercantilism box to show what laissez-faire was *against*; it converts a doctrine into a historical argument.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Classical liberalism **placed individual at the centre of its philosophy**, endowed with **the faculty of 'reason'**. (PDF p.236)
2. ✅ Four freedoms: **trade, contract, bargain, enterprise**; **private property as the condition of progress**.
3. ✅ Profit motive and open competition are **'functional'** and **conducive to social progress**.
4. ✅ Government's function: protect liberty, **enforce contracts**, guarantee **peaceful employment of property**, provide **law and order**.
5. ✅ **Laissez-faire** is French for **'leave alone'** — **non-intervention by the state in the economic activities of individuals**.
6. ✅ The state is **a 'necessary evil'**: evil because it **restricts the freedom of the individual**, necessary because without regulation freedom **cannot be safeguarded**.
7. ✅ Exponents: **Adam Smith (1723-90)**, **Jeremy Bentham (1748-1832)**, **James Mill (1773-1836)**, **Herbert Spencer (1820-1903)**; **J.S. Mill (1806-73)** transforms it **from negative liberalism into positive liberalism**.
8. ✅ *Wealth of Nations* (**1776**) is **the great source of the ideas and policies concerning laissez-faire individualism**.
9. ✅ The **Physiocrats** held, **deriving ultimately from Rousseau**, **the goodness and bounty of nature and the goodness of man**; **criminals, mad men, and monopolists should be eliminated**.
10. ✅ Smith rejected their view that **agriculture was the sole source of the wealth**, holding **commerce and industry, as well as agriculture**, to be sources.
11. ✅ **Economic man**: a **natural propensity to trade**; self-interest **conducive to promotion of the general good**; **led by an invisible hand to promote an end which was no part of his intention**.
12. ✅ **Smith never gave a blanket endorsement to the idea of beneficence of self-interest.**
13. ✅ **Natural liberty**: every man **left perfectly free to pursue his own interest in his own way**; the sovereign **completely discharged from... superintending the industry of the private people**.
14. ✅ Three duties: **defence**; **exact administration of justice**; **public works... which could not be undertaken by an individual** because profit **would never repay the expenditure**.
15. ✅ **Mercantilism** box: national power depends on prosperity; **maximizing exports and minimizing imports**; import **cheap raw materials** and export **expensive finished goods**; **state's intervention... for the protection of indigenous industries**; **lowering of wages**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain what the label "necessary evil" asserts about the state in laissez-faire theory, and show that Adam Smith's own account of government is not purely negative.

**Model answer:** ✅ The label asserts a two-sided judgment with a stated reason on each side: the state **is evil because it imposes regulations and restricts the freedom of the individual, yet it is necessary because, without its regulation, the freedom of the individual cannot be safeguarded**. ⚠️ It is therefore a trade-off, not an insult: regulation is the price of secure liberty, and the doctrine's task is to **set definite and circumscribed limits** on it. ⚠️ Smith's account is not purely negative because the third of his **three duties of great importance** is **the erection and maintenance of public works and running certain public institutions which could not be undertaken by an individual or a small number of individuals because the profit accruing from their maintenance would never repay the expenditure involved**. ✅ The criterion is unprofitability, not ideology, so Smith concedes a class of socially necessary activities that markets will not perform. ⚠️ He is also not a simple egoist: he **never gave a blanket endorsement to the idea of beneficence of self-interest**, claiming only that men are **on occasion** led **by an invisible hand**. 🔑 Minimal, but not empty.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat laissez-faire as a demand for the absence of the state. ⚠️ The chapter shows it arising against **mercantilism**, a system that **upheld the state's intervention in economy for the protection of indigenous industries** and **recommended lowering of wages** — so the quarrel was about whose interests state action should serve, not about whether the state should exist.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Examine the theory of laissez-faire individualism and the functions it assigns to the state. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Classical liberalism places the individual, **endowed with the faculty of 'reason'**, at the centre, and derives from reason the freedoms of **trade, contract, bargain and enterprise**, with **private property as the condition of progress** and the profit motive as **'functional'**. ✅ **Laissez-faire**, meaning **'leave alone'**, is accordingly **non-intervention by the state in the economic activities of individuals**, and the state becomes a **'necessary evil'**. ✅ Smith's **system of 'natural liberty'** leaves every man **perfectly free to pursue his own interest in his own way** and discharges the sovereign from **superintending the industry of the private people**. ✅ Its three duties are **defence**, **an exact administration of justice**, and **public works... which could not be undertaken by an individual** because profit **would never repay the expenditure**. ⚠️ The theory is therefore minimal but not anarchic, and its third duty concedes market failure. 🔑 Limited functions, reasoned limits.

**Rubric [10]:** the derivation from reason to the four freedoms **2** · the laissez-faire definition and the necessary-evil formula **3** · Smith's natural liberty **2** · the three duties stated **2** · a closing line noting the public-works concession **1**.

## Lesson 24 — Bentham and James Mill

Progress: 24 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Bentham and James Mill entries and the Advanced companion §5's grouping of Smith, Bentham, James Mill, Spencer and Nozick; read against the chapter's *Jeremy Bentham* and *James Mill* sub-sections at PDF pp.239-242 (printed 221-224), including the seven criteria of the felicific calculus.
CA search: "utilitarianism greatest happiness principle legislation state function 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of utilitarianism. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why a theory of pleasure becomes a theory of the state

✅ Bentham contributed to laissez-faire individualism **as the great exponent of Utilitarianism**, which **stands for the revival of the classical hedonism of Epicurus** — the view that **man's behaviour should be governed by the consideration of advancement of pleasure and the avoidance of pain** — **adapted by Bentham to the conditions of eighteenth and nineteenth century Europe**.

✅ The move to politics is made by elimination. ✅ Bentham and his followers argued that **the concepts of absolute rights, absolute sovereignty and absolute justice had no relevance to the realities of social life**; **there was only one absolute standard of regulation of human affairs, viz. that of absolute expediency**. ✅ Political institutions and public policies **should not be rated as good or bad in relation to some visionary and arbitrary concepts of human rights and obligations; they should be judged by their fruits**. ✅ **The satisfaction of individual should furnish the yardstick of utility**, and for the whole society **the controlling principle should be the 'greatest happiness of the greatest number'**.

```text
  reject: absolute RIGHTS | absolute SOVEREIGNTY | absolute JUSTICE
                 |
  retain: ONE standard -- ABSOLUTE EXPEDIENCY
                 |
  institutions "judged by their FRUITS"
                 |
  unit of measurement: the SATISFACTION OF THE INDIVIDUAL
                 |
  social rule: "the GREATEST HAPPINESS OF THE GREATEST NUMBER"
```

🔑 Note what has been discarded: the whole natural-rights apparatus that Locke used to limit government. ⚠️ Bentham **rejected the ideology of natural rights and the social contract**, yet **subscribed to the sovereignty of 'reason'** and sought a formula **free from the pitfalls of metaphysical abstraction**.

### 2. The quantitative commitment and the calculus

✅ Bentham **interpreted happiness by the crude word 'pleasure'** and postulated: **'Nature has placed mankind under the governance of two sovereign masters, pain and pleasure'.** ✅ He and **James Mill** held that **men always desire only pleasure and are averse only to pain**; if they desire anything else **it is only because they have learnt by experience that these things bring pleasure and avert pain**. ✅ Utility is an action's **tendency to cause pleasure and to avert pain**, and the **right action** is **the one most likely to give the greatest balance of pleasure over pain to the persons liable to be affected by it**.

✅ Crucially, **pleasure and pain were susceptible to measurement**. ✅ Bentham **repudiated any qualitative difference between different kinds of pleasure, and emphasized their quantitative differences** — the bias captured in **"Quantity of pleasure being equal, pushpin is as good as poetry."**

✅ The **'hedonistic calculus' or 'felicific calculus'** has seven criteria:

| # | ✅ Criterion | ✅ The chapter's gloss |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Intensity** | **how strong is its feeling?** |
| 2 | **Duration** | **how long it lasts?** |
| 3 | **Certainty** | **how certain we feel to have it?** |
| 4 | **Proximity or propinquity** | **how near it is to us, i.e. how early we can have it?** |
| 5 | **Fecundity** | **does it also produce other types of pleasure?** |
| 6 | **Purity** | **no pain is mixed with it** |
| 7 | **Extent** | **how far it extends to others?** |

✅ **Of these the first six criteria are meant to judge the utility of a thing or action for the individual while the seventh criterion (extent) is relevant to judging public policy as expressed in the principle of 'greatest happiness of the greatest number.'**

🔑 That division is the examinable point: six private criteria, one public. ⚠️ **Extent** is the bridge from personal hedonism to legislation, and without it utilitarianism would have no politics at all.

### 3. Principles of legislation, equality, and the limits of state action

✅ Bentham **repudiated the theory of the general will as something transcending the will of the individual**, and **defined the interest of the community as the sum of the interests of the several members who compose it**. ✅ **Pleasure or happiness should not be taken as a shadowy attribute of some super-person, called a social organism, but must find actual expression in the lives and in the experience of definite individuals.**

⚠️ Two of the chapter's divisions are being refused in that sentence at once — Rousseau's general will and the organic theory's **super-person**. 🔑 Utilitarian individualism is thus anti-organic by construction.

✅ The legislator need only **calculate the pleasurable or painful consequences of an action, actual or proposed**, to know **whether it was right or wrong, sound or unsound**. ✅ **The business of government is to promote the happiness of society by a system of punishments and rewards**; **it had no other justification for its existence**; and **a government which employs ineffectual means in this sphere, loses its title to authority**.

✅ On equality: **each individual should be treated as one unit** and **none should be given special consideration**: **'each to count as one, and no one for more than one'**. ✅ Bentham **did not base his doctrine of equality on 'natural law'** but on the assumption that **men were born to be happy—that is the plain dictate of experience**; **since freedom is essential to happiness, men are entitled to freedom**; and **equal freedom of each individual postulates 'equality'**, so **man's liberty must be limited and conditioned by the ultimate test of general welfare**. ✅ Equality **was a political good, because it was the only practical way of dealing with large numbers of people**, and it curbed **the legislator's tendency of ignoring the happiness of the people in pursuance of their own moral standards**.

✅ Yet the scope of the state remains small. ✅ Since **each individual is the best judge of his own interests**, **the main function of the state is legislation**, whose **chief objective... is to remove all institutional restrictions on the free actions of individuals**. ✅ **The state cannot improve character of the people**; it should restrain only activities which **affect the general happiness adversely**, and **punishment of offenders is another main function**. ✅ **The state should not interfere in the activities of law-abiding citizens who are the best judges of right and wrong, moral and immoral.**

### 4. James Mill

✅ **James Mill was a close associate and follower of Jeremy Bentham**, propagating his Utilitarianism and his **principles of law, administration, education and psychology**. ✅ He founded **'philosophical radicalism'** on Bentham's Utilitarianism and **led a radical-reformist movement which aimed at extension of franchise and representation of the interests of the working class in British Parliament**. ✅ Besides Bentham, he studied **Hobbes's individualism and Adam Smith's classical political economy**, and is **well-known for popularizing the views of his favourite thinkers rather than for advancing an original view**.

✅ Analysing England's **aristocratic government, legal system and mercantilist economy**, he concluded that these **obstructed the way to 'the greatest happiness of the greatest number'**. ✅ In ***Essays on Government* (1825)** he **commended democratic government as good government which sought to work for the benefit of the citizens**, argued that **aristocratic government is motivated by self-interest which promotes corruption**, and held this preventable **by instituting a representative government based on universal suffrage, secret ballot and periodic elections**. ✅ He **particularly liked the middle rank, and hoped that when democratic institutions are established, the people would follow their footsteps**.

⚠️ ❌ The chapter prints **Essays on Government (1825)**. ⚠️ The work is widely catalogued as *Essay on Government*, with 1820 as the usual date of the *Encyclopaedia Britannica* supplement printing. ⚠️ The printed form is carried here as the chapter gives it, and the variance is recorded as an open uncertainty rather than silently corrected.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Utilitarianism supplies the consequentialist justification of the state and the entry point for J.S. Mill's revision at Lesson 28.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on the greatest happiness principle, on equality without natural rights, and on the purpose of legislation route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating Bentham as indifferent to equality. ✅ **Each to count as one, and no one for more than one** is a strict equality rule — only it is derived from utility, not from nature.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The six-plus-one structure of the calculus is the most economical way to show that utilitarianism has a built-in theory of public policy.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Utilitarianism **stands for the revival of the classical hedonism of Epicurus**. (PDF p.239)
2. ✅ Absolute **rights, sovereignty and justice** are rejected; the one standard is **absolute expediency**; institutions are **judged by their fruits**.
3. ✅ The controlling principle is **'the greatest happiness of the greatest number'**.
4. ✅ **'Nature has placed mankind under the governance of two sovereign masters, pain and pleasure'.**
5. ✅ Utility is a **tendency to cause pleasure and to avert pain**; right action gives **the greatest balance of pleasure over pain to the persons liable to be affected**.
6. ✅ Bentham **repudiated any qualitative difference between different kinds of pleasure**: **"Quantity of pleasure being equal, pushpin is as good as poetry."**
7. ✅ Felicific calculus: **intensity, duration, certainty, proximity or propinquity, fecundity, purity, extent**.
8. 🔑 **The first six criteria... judge the utility... for the individual while the seventh criterion (extent) is relevant to judging public policy.**
9. ✅ He **repudiated the theory of the general will** and defined community interest as **the sum of the interests of the several members**.
10. ✅ Happiness must not be **a shadowy attribute of some super-person, called a social organism**.
11. ✅ **The business of government is to promote the happiness of society by a system of punishments and rewards**; ineffectual government **loses its title to authority**.
12. ✅ **'Each to count as one, and no one for more than one'**; equality rests not on natural law but on the view that **men were born to be happy**.
13. ✅ **The state cannot improve character of the people**; its main function is **legislation**, chiefly to **remove all institutional restrictions on the free actions of individuals**.
14. ✅ **James Mill** founded **'philosophical radicalism'**, sought **extension of franchise**, and in ***Essays on Government* (1825)** commended **universal suffrage, secret ballot and periodic elections**; he favoured **the middle rank**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Bentham discards natural rights yet arrives at a strict rule of equality and at narrow limits on state action. Reconstruct both derivations from utility alone.

**Model answer:** ✅ **Equality.** Having rejected **absolute rights, absolute sovereignty and absolute justice** in favour of **absolute expediency**, Bentham needs an aggregation rule, and adopts **'each to count as one, and no one for more than one'**. ✅ He grounds it not on **'natural law'** but on the claim that **men were born to be happy—that is the plain dictate of experience**; **since freedom is essential to happiness, men are entitled to freedom**, and **equal freedom of each individual postulates 'equality'**. ✅ Equality is thus **a political good, because it was the only practical way of dealing with large numbers**, and it prevents legislators from substituting **their own moral standards**. ✅ **Limits.** Since **each individual is the best judge of his own interests** and **the state cannot improve character of the people**, the state's **main function... is legislation**, whose **chief objective... is to remove all institutional restrictions on the free actions of individuals**, together with **punishment of offenders**; it **should not interfere in the activities of law-abiding citizens**. 🔑 One premise about self-knowledge yields both the equal weighting and the narrow mandate.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say utilitarianism has no theory of the common good. ⚠️ It has a precise one — the **extent** criterion, the seventh of the calculus — but it refuses to treat the common good as **a shadowy attribute of some super-person, called a social organism**, insisting instead that it **find actual expression in the lives and in the experience of definite individuals**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain how Bentham's utilitarianism supports the theory of laissez-faire individualism. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Bentham makes **absolute expediency** the sole standard, so institutions are **judged by their fruits** against **the greatest happiness of the greatest number**, measured by the felicific calculus whose seventh criterion, **extent**, converts private hedonism into public policy. ⚠️ Three steps then produce laissez-faire. ✅ First, since **each individual is the best judge of his own interests**, state direction of conduct cannot raise utility. ✅ Second, **the state cannot improve character of the people**, so moral improvement is not a legitimate aim. ✅ Third, the **main function of the state is legislation**, whose **chief objective... is to remove all institutional restrictions on the free actions of individuals**, with **punishment of offenders** as the residue; it **should not interfere in the activities of law-abiding citizens**. ⚠️ Equality survives only as an aggregation rule — **each to count as one** — not as a ground for redistribution. 🔑 Maximise happiness, and the minimal state follows.

**Rubric [10]:** the expediency standard and the greatest-happiness principle **2** · the calculus with the extent criterion identified as the public one **2** · the three steps to minimal government **4** · the status of equality as an aggregation rule **1** · a closing line **1**.
