## Lesson 25 — Spencer and the libertarian revival

Progress: 25 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block C — II. Liberal-individualist perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Spencer and Nozick entries and the Advanced companion §5's grouping of libertarian thinkers; read against the chapter's *Herbert Spencer* sub-section at PDF p.242 (printed 224) and the *Recent Developments* sub-section at PDF pp.243-244 (printed 225-226).
CA search: "libertarianism nightwatchman state entitlement theory contemporary debate 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of the libertarian revival. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Spencer: laissez-faire at its limit

✅ **The sociological foundation of laissez-faire individualism was provided by Herbert Spencer**, in ***Social Statics* (1850)**, ***The Man versus the State* (1884)** and ***The Principles of Ethics* (1892-93)**.

✅ Starting **from an idea of universal evolution**, Spencer postulated **the tendency of all things to ultimate equilibrium** and **the consequent tendency of all things to transform themselves by a process of evolution in order to attain this equilibrium**. ✅ Accordingly, the individual **tends to equilibriate himself with his social environment by adaptation, and by inheritance of that adaptation, until he attains, in a perfect equilibrium, the blessedness of final anarchy**.

✅ In this process **the state has a very limited role to play, and that is the function of protection—administration of the law of equal freedom**. ✅ Spencer treated the state as **a 'joint-stock protection-company for mutual assurance'**; **it should not assume any other function, nor otherwise interfere with the process of natural evolution**.

✅ Hence the conclusions the chapter plainly disapproves: the state **should not undertake public health, nor give the poor any relief because that would defeat the operation of the law of natural selection**. ✅ Spencer subscribed to **'struggle for existence'** and **'survival of the fittest'** as guiding principles of social evolution, and argued that if **'family ethics' was applied to the state, it would retard progress by giving the weakling more than he deserved and perpetuating an undeserving life**.

✅ The chapter's own assessment: **Spencer stretched the concept of negative liberty to such an extreme that he considered elimination of the weak in the struggle for existence as part of the process of social progress.**

🔑 Spencer is the chapter's demonstration of a doctrine taken to its logical end. ⚠️ The welfare division at Lesson 26 does not need to refute him; it only needs to describe what the free market actually produced.

### 2. The revival, and why it happened

✅ **In the past few decades, the theory of laissez-faire liberalism was revived with a new vigour.** ✅ It **sought new grounds of keeping the state away from interference in the market forces** and is **variously described as neo-liberalism, neo-classical liberalism, or libertarianism**.

✅ The chapter supplies the intervening history in one paragraph: toward the end of the nineteenth century and the beginning of the twentieth, **liberalism tended to accommodate some tenets of socialist and idealist thought, which was responsible for the emergence of the theory of 'welfare state'**; **negative liberalism of the eighteenth century had given way to positive liberalism by the first half of the twentieth century**; the liberalism of **T.H. Green (1836-82)**, **L.T. Hobhouse (1864-1929)** and **Harold J. Laski (1893-1950)** **conceded positive role of the state in regulating economic system in the interests of the poor and deprived sections of society**; and **the principle of liberty was sought to accommodate the principles of equality and justice within the liberal frame of thought**.

✅ Then the reversal: **during the second half of the twentieth century some thinkers in the liberal tradition found the theory of 'welfare state' to be inimical to individual liberty, and sought to revive the original concern of this tradition with laissez-faire philosophy**. ✅ It is called **'libertarianism' in order to indicate its renewed concern with 'liberty' and to distinguish it from the recent changes in the liberal outlook**.

### 3. What libertarianism claims

| ✅ Claim | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| Scope of liberation | **Full autonomy and freedom of the individual**; **'liberation' from all institutions which tend to restrict his vision of the world, including the institutions of religion, family and customs of social conformity apart from political institutions** |
| Philosophical premise | It **repudiates the deterministic outlook of human life**: personality, character, thought and action **cannot be construed as an outcome of his circumstances**; it **treats man as maker of his destiny** |
| Economic demand | Economic activity **must be actively liberated from all restrictions** |
| Source of rights | Drawing on **the 'natural rights' theory of John Locke**, certain rights **precede his political life**, are **indefeasible** and **cannot be surrendered in favour of the collectivity** |
| Which rights | Especially **the right to acquire and hold property and freedom of contract**; these are **by no means the product of the state itself**, so the state cannot intervene **for any artificial balancing of rights** |
| On redistribution | It **condemns taxation of the rich for the benefit of the poor**, as **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**, serving as **a disincentive to individual** |
| Conclusion | **Laissez-faire capitalism is most conducive to social progress** |

✅ Two wings are distinguished. ✅ **An extreme form... holds that all government is illegitimate; hence it comes closer to 'anarchism'** — but **while anarchists project a vision of society wherein use of force would become redundant, extreme-libertarians look forward to the establishment of a system in which function of protection would be assigned to private protective agencies**. ✅ **Moderate-libertarians concede that government may legitimately engage in police protection and enforcement of contracts**, with **civil as well as criminal courts**, and **national defence, but nothing beyond these functions** — **at best, a 'nightwatchman state'**, with taxation **restricted to serve these purposes only**.

✅ The chief exponents are **F.A. Hayek (1899-1992)**, **Isaiah Berlin (1909-97)**, **Milton Friedman (1912-2006)** and **Robert Nozick (1938-2002)**.

🔑 The extreme-libertarian/anarchist distinction is examinable and often missed: both abolish the state, but one replaces it with no force and the other with **private protective agencies**.

### 4. Nozick's argument, and its one concession

✅ In ***Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974)**, **following Locke's method**, Nozick argues that **individuals do have certain rights in the state of nature**; **they would hire protective agencies for their property holdings**; and **the dominant protective agency, having de facto monopoly of force in a given territory, would emerge as a 'state-like entity'**. ✅ Accordingly **the state has no legitimate powers beyond the functions of protection, justice and defence; it is not authorized to engage in redistributive transfers among the citizens who were originally its clients**. ✅ **Hence, 'welfare state' is ruled out.**

✅ On property: **acquisition or transfer of property without force or fraud is just**. ✅ The right to property **is derived from the fact that an individual is 'entitled' to it**; **it is not necessary to prove that he morally deserves it**. ✅ He is entitled **either by virtue of just acquisition of an unowned property or by receiving property from someone who has just initial title to it**; **legal rights are, therefore, the product of voluntary exchanges**. ✅ **Inequalities at the level of production and voluntary transfers should not be sought to be rectified at the level of distribution.**

✅ And then the exception, stated in a single sentence: **However, if there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it.**

⚠️ That sentence is worth its weight. 🔑 It concedes that entitlement is defeasible where a holding is both unique and necessary — a crack in the theory that any critic will widen, and any defender must bound.

✅ Nozick finally **attempts to demonstrate that a libertarian society, which allows all individuals and groups to shape their life according to their wishes, fulfils the most plausible definition of a Utopian social order**.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the contemporary half of the minimal-state case and the direct foil to the welfare division.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on anarchism's relation to liberalism and socialism want the extreme-libertarian/anarchist distinction made here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating libertarianism as identical with classical laissez-faire. ✅ It argues from Lockean **indefeasible** rights, not from Spencer's natural selection.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The desert-water sentence is the single most useful concession in the division; cite it when asked for limits on entitlement.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Spencer: ***Social Statics* (1850)**, ***The Man versus the State* (1884)**, ***The Principles of Ethics* (1892-93)**. (PDF p.242)
2. ✅ Universal evolution tends to **ultimate equilibrium**, ending in **the blessedness of final anarchy**.
3. ✅ The state's only role is **protection—administration of the law of equal freedom**; it is **a 'joint-stock protection-company for mutual assurance'**.
4. ✅ No **public health**, no **poor relief**, because that **would defeat the operation of the law of natural selection**.
5. ✅ **'Family ethics'** applied to the state **would retard progress by giving the weakling more than he deserved**.
6. ✅ Spencer **stretched the concept of negative liberty to such an extreme that he considered elimination of the weak... as part of the process of social progress**.
7. ✅ Revival: **neo-liberalism, neo-classical liberalism, or libertarianism**, because the welfare state was found **inimical to individual liberty**. (PDF p.243)
8. ✅ Interim history: **Green**, **Hobhouse** and **Laski** **conceded positive role of the state**; negative liberalism **had given way to positive liberalism**.
9. ✅ Libertarianism seeks **'liberation' from... religion, family and customs of social conformity apart from political institutions**, and **treats man as maker of his destiny**.
10. ✅ It draws on **the 'natural rights' theory of John Locke**: rights that **precede his political life** are **indefeasible**.
11. ✅ It **condemns taxation of the rich for the benefit of the poor** as **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**.
12. ✅ Extreme libertarians assign protection to **private protective agencies**; moderates uphold **at best, a 'nightwatchman state'**.
13. ✅ Exponents: **Hayek (1899-1992)**, **Berlin (1909-97)**, **Friedman (1912-2006)**, **Nozick (1938-2002)**.
14. ✅ Nozick, ***Anarchy, State and Utopia* (1974)**: **the dominant protective agency** becomes **a 'state-like entity'**; entitlement not desert; **no... rectified at the level of distribution** — **however, if there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Distinguish extreme libertarianism from anarchism as the chapter distinguishes them, and state the one qualification Nozick places on the entitlement theory.

**Model answer:** ✅ Both reach beyond the minimal state — **an extreme form of libertarianism holds that all government is illegitimate; hence it comes closer to 'anarchism'** — but they differ on what replaces it. ✅ **Anarchists project a vision of society wherein use of force would become redundant**, whereas **extreme-libertarians look forward to the establishment of a system in which function of protection would be assigned to private protective agencies**. ⚠️ The difference is therefore about force, not about government: anarchism predicts its disappearance, extreme libertarianism predicts its privatisation. ✅ Moderate libertarians stop earlier, allowing **police protection and enforcement of contracts**, courts and **national defence, but nothing beyond these functions** — **at best, a 'nightwatchman state'**. ✅ Nozick's qualification is the desert case: although **inequalities at the level of production and voluntary transfers should not be sought to be rectified at the level of distribution**, nevertheless **if there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it**. 🔑 Entitlement yields where a holding is both unique and necessary to life.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the libertarian revival as a simple return to Spencer. ⚠️ Spencer argued from **universal evolution**, **struggle for existence** and **survival of the fittest**, and accepted **elimination of the weak** as progress; libertarianism argues from **the 'natural rights' theory of John Locke** and from **indefeasible** entitlements, which is a moral premise rather than a biological one.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Account for the revival of laissez-faire liberalism in the later twentieth century and examine the strength of the libertarian case against the welfare state. *(Account for and examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The chapter supplies a three-phase history. ✅ Negative liberalism **had given way to positive liberalism by the first half of the twentieth century**, as **Green**, **Hobhouse** and **Laski** **conceded positive role of the state in regulating economic system in the interests of the poor and deprived sections**. ✅ Then **some thinkers in the liberal tradition found the theory of 'welfare state' to be inimical to individual liberty**, and the older position was **revived with a new vigour** as **neo-liberalism, neo-classical liberalism, or libertarianism**. ✅ The case rests on rights rather than on efficiency: drawing on **the 'natural rights' theory of John Locke**, certain rights **precede his political life**, are **indefeasible** and **cannot be surrendered in favour of the collectivity** — especially **the right to acquire and hold property and freedom of contract**, which are **by no means the product of the state itself**. ✅ Redistribution is therefore condemned as **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**, and Nozick concludes that the state **is not authorized to engage in redistributive transfers**, so that **'welfare state' is ruled out**. ⚠️ The case is strong where consent is genuine and holdings are traceable to **just acquisition** or **just initial title**. ⚠️ It is weak where neither condition holds, and Nozick himself concedes the limiting case: **if there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it**. ⚠️ Division III's evidence — child labour, slum housing, labour as a commodity — multiplies such cases. 🔑 A principled objection with an admitted exception, and the exception is where most people live.

**Rubric [15]:** the three-phase history with named thinkers **4** · the Lockean rights premise as the ground of the revival **3** · the objection to redistribution in the chapter's wording **2** · Nozick's conclusion on the welfare state **2** · the desert-water concession used as the limiting case **3** · a qualified verdict **1**.

## Lesson 26 — Transition to positive liberalism

Progress: 26 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's welfare-state definition and §7's nineteenth-century illustrations; with the Advanced companion §7's note that laissez-faire is destabilised by its own outcomes, read against the restored division heading **III. WELFARE STATE PERSPECTIVE** at PDF p.244 (printed 226) and the sub-heading **TRANSITION TO POSITIVE LIBERALISM** that immediately follows it.
CA search: "free market society inequality labour as commodity historical critique welfare state 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of the transition. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The division's own statement of itself

✅ Division III opens with two sentences that define the whole perspective: **Liberal theory which stood for negative liberalism in its early phase, was transformed into positive liberalism in its later phase.** ✅ **Positive liberalism promoted the idea of welfare state, as it pleaded for positive role of the state in securing welfare of its citizens, particularly of their vulnerable sections.**

⚠️ Two things are fixed there. 🔑 The welfare state is a *liberal* development, not an import from socialism; and its distinguishing feature is a **positive role**, not a larger budget.

### 2. What the free-market society actually produced

✅ **Negative liberalism had sought to establish free-market society which promoted capitalism.** ✅ **The success of capitalism in the nineteenth century demonstrated that the free-market society created large inequalities among human beings and promoted oppression of the vulnerable sections—workers, peasants, consumers, etc.**

⚠️ The chapter's evidence is concrete, and the sequence matters because each item is a liberal freedom turning into its opposite:

```text
  FREEDOM OF CONTRACT
     + "the enormous growth of the labour force in industrial cities"
     = "freedom for factory-owners to hire and fire their workers to
        maximize their profits"
     => "the consequent insecurity and suffering of the workers"

  FREEDOM OF TRADE
     "was not restricted to commodities -- LABOUR WAS ALSO TREATED AS
      A COMMODITY"
     => "inhumane conditions for the workers, CHILD LABOUR, SLUM HOUSING,
        and free sale of POISONED MEAT, BAD GIN and other things
        injurious to health"

  FREEDOM OF ENTERPRISE
     interpreted as "the total absence of regulation on business and
     industry"
     => "disastrous consequences for the bulk of society, NOT the
        greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the
        classical economists"
```

🔑 The last line is the chapter's rhetorical hinge. ⚠️ It defeats laissez-faire on its *own* criterion: the utilitarian standard that justified the free market is the standard the free market failed. ⚠️ No external value is imported, which is precisely why the chapter can call this a transformation *within* liberalism.

### 3. Why the evidence had to be acted on

✅ The chapter adds the social and political pressure that converted observation into revision. ✅ **The working class was not only increasing in size, its condition was also deteriorating.** ✅ **Its voice could not be suppressed any longer.** ✅ **The socialists were incessantly pressing for a solution of the problems of the working class.** ✅ **The liberals were also forced to realize that their insistence on freedom and human rights had created conditions of oppression in society.** ✅ **They must resolve the contradictions of the liberal theory—as evidenced by the oppressive character of the capitalist system—otherwise the whole edifice would tumble down.**

✅ **As a result, the tenets of the liberal theory were revised toward the last quarter of the nineteenth century and this process of revision went on during the twentieth century.**

⚠️ Note the motive the chapter assigns: self-preservation of the doctrine. 🔑 **Otherwise the whole edifice would tumble down** is not a moral conversion narrative; it is an account of a theory repairing itself under pressure, and it pairs exactly with the chapter's later verdict that liberal theory is **often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the empirical case against negative liberty and the bridge from division II to division III.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems contrasting negative and positive liberty, or asking whether liberty without welfare is real, open here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that socialism produced the welfare state. ✅ The chapter credits socialist pressure but locates the transformation inside liberal theory.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use the internal-defeat point: laissez-faire failed the greatest-happiness test it had itself adopted.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Liberal theory which stood for negative liberalism in its early phase, was transformed into positive liberalism in its later phase.** (PDF p.244)
2. ✅ **Positive liberalism promoted the idea of welfare state**, pleading for a **positive role of the state in securing welfare of its citizens, particularly of their vulnerable sections**.
3. ✅ The free-market society **created large inequalities** and **promoted oppression of the vulnerable sections—workers, peasants, consumers**.
4. ✅ Freedom of contract became **freedom for factory-owners to hire and fire their workers to maximize their profits**.
5. ✅ **Labour was also treated as a commodity.**
6. ✅ Results: **inhumane conditions for the workers, child labour, slum housing, and free sale of poisoned meat, bad gin and other things injurious to health**.
7. ✅ Freedom of enterprise as **the total absence of regulation** brought **disastrous consequences for the bulk of society**.
8. 🔑 The outcome was **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists**.
9. ✅ **The working class was not only increasing in size, its condition was also deteriorating.**
10. ✅ **Its voice could not be suppressed any longer**; **the socialists were incessantly pressing for a solution**.
11. ✅ Liberals **were forced to realize that their insistence on freedom and human rights had created conditions of oppression in society**.
12. ✅ The contradictions had to be resolved, **otherwise the whole edifice would tumble down**.
13. ✅ Revision began **toward the last quarter of the nineteenth century** and **went on during the twentieth century**.
14. ⚠️ The transformation is internal to liberalism: the doctrine failed its own utilitarian test.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the chapter treats positive liberalism as a development inside liberal theory rather than as a concession to socialism, and identify the standard on which laissez-faire is shown to fail.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because the defeat is internal. ✅ The chapter's sequence shows each classical liberal freedom producing its own opposite: freedom of contract becoming **freedom for factory-owners to hire and fire their workers**, freedom of trade extending until **labour was also treated as a commodity**, and freedom of enterprise read as **the total absence of regulation**. ✅ The outcome is then measured against the classical economists' own criterion and found wanting — these were **disastrous consequences for the bulk of society, not the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists**. ⚠️ No socialist premise is required to reach that conclusion. ✅ Socialist pressure appears only as a cause of urgency — **the socialists were incessantly pressing for a solution** — alongside the liberals' own recognition that their **insistence on freedom and human rights had created conditions of oppression**. ⚠️ The decisive motive is doctrinal survival: the contradictions had to be resolved **otherwise the whole edifice would tumble down**. 🔑 Utility, the laissez-faire justification, became the laissez-faire indictment.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not describe positive liberalism as an abandonment of liberty for equality. ⚠️ The chapter frames it as liberty redefined, recording that **the principle of liberty was sought to accommodate the principles of equality and justice within the liberal frame of thought** — accommodation inside the frame, not replacement of it.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Why did negative liberalism give way to positive liberalism? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Because the free-market society it created **demonstrated that the free-market society created large inequalities among human beings and promoted oppression of the vulnerable sections—workers, peasants, consumers**. ✅ Freedom of contract meant **freedom for factory-owners to hire and fire their workers to maximize their profits**, with **consequent insecurity and suffering**; freedom of trade extended until **labour was also treated as a commodity**, producing **child labour, slum housing, and free sale of poisoned meat, bad gin**; freedom of enterprise, read as **total absence of regulation**, brought **disastrous consequences for the bulk of society**. ✅ The result was **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists** — a failure on liberalism's own test. ✅ With the working class growing, socialist pressure rising and the contradictions threatening that **the whole edifice would tumble down**, the tenets were revised from the **last quarter of the nineteenth century**. 🔑 Self-correction under its own standard.

**Rubric [10]:** the three freedoms shown turning into their opposites **4** · at least three of the chapter's concrete illustrations **2** · the internal failure against the greatest-happiness test **2** · the pressures and the survival motive **1** · the dating of the revision **1**.

## Lesson 27 — Bentham's dilemma: equality against productivity

Progress: 27 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §8's welfare-liberal row on inequality and the Advanced companion §7's note that positive liberalism corrects laissez-faire; read against the chapter's Macpherson passages at PDF pp.245-247 (printed 227-228), carried across the header/body split at PDF pp.246-247.
CA search: "diminishing marginal utility equality productivity Bentham Macpherson 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's statement of Bentham's dilemma. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A dilemma inside utilitarianism itself

✅ **Classical liberals had sought to justify a free-market society on the basis of the 'equality' of individuals.** ✅ **Bentham had argued that in aggregating individual utilities, each individual was to count as one.** ✅ He sought to justify the liberal state **as the state most calculated to maximize utilities—security of life, freedom of individual movement, security of property**, and postulated that a free-market society **enabled each individual to maximize his own utility**, bringing everyone **into productive relations which would thus maximize the aggregate utility of society**.

✅ **But in this process he was caught in a dilemma—to reconcile theoretical equality with practical inequality.**

✅ C.B. Macpherson, in ***Democratic Theory—Essays in Retrieval* (1973)**, states the first horn:

> Bentham was clear that the market must be left to determine the allocation of the material product among the individuals who contributed to it by their labour or land or capital, although he saw that this would mean persistent inequality. He acknowledged, indeed, that there was a case for equality of wealth or income. This followed from the principle of diminishing utility—the principle that a second loaf of bread doesn't give a hungry man as much satisfaction as the first loaf, or more generally that the more you have of anything the less the utility to you of any increment. Given this principle, and given that each individual's satisfaction was to count as one, it followed that the aggregate utility of the whole society would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth.
>
> — C.B. Macpherson, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.245 (printed 227)

```text
  PREMISE A   "each individual's satisfaction was to count as one"
  PREMISE B   DIMINISHING UTILITY -- "the more you have of anything the
              less the utility to you of any increment"
                        |
  CONCLUSION  "the aggregate utility of the whole society would be
               greatest if everyone had EQUAL amounts of wealth"
                        |
  so UTILITARIANISM ITSELF argues for EQUALITY of wealth or income
```

🔑 This is the chapter's most elegant argument, and candidates rarely have it. ⚠️ Egalitarian redistribution is derivable from Bentham's own two commitments without any appeal to rights, justice or socialism.

### 2. The escape, and its price

✅ **Bentham tried to escape from this dilemma by introducing another element—the criterion of productivity—and decided the case in favour of inequality.** ✅ Macpherson again, in the passage that runs across the chapter's header/body page split:

> As soon as Bentham had thus demonstrated the case for equality he argued that it had to yield to the case for productivity. Without security for unequal property, there would be no incentive to capital accumulation, and without capital accumulation there would be practically no productivity. Besides, without a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation, the market could not maximize productivity.
>
> — Macpherson, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF pp.246-247 (printed 228)

⚠️ Read the last sentence twice. 🔑 The second limb of the productivity defence is not that inequality rewards effort but that destitution is a *necessary input*: the market needs **a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation**. ⚠️ That concession, made inside the classical case, is what makes the welfare revision unavoidable — a theory that requires hunger to function cannot also claim to maximise happiness.

⚠️ 🔶 One page-handling note belongs here because the passage straddles it. ✅ PDF p.246 carries only the folio **228** and the running head; PDF p.247 carries the body with no folio. ✅ The quotation above therefore begins on one PDF page and completes on the next with no loss, and the printed page 228 exists in full.

### 3. Why later liberals could not use the escape

✅ **However, subsequent liberal thinkers could not escape from this dilemma so easily.** ✅ **The working class was not only increasing in size, its condition was also deteriorating.** ✅ **Its voice could not be suppressed any longer.** ✅ **The socialists were incessantly pressing for a solution of the problems of the working class.** ✅ **The liberals were also forced to realize that their insistence on freedom and human rights had created conditions of oppression in society.**

⚠️ The productivity defence has an empirical premise — that inequality buys growth which eventually benefits all. ⚠️ When the evidence of division III's opening page is in, that premise is contested: the growth occurred and the oppression occurred together. 🔑 So the dilemma returns, and this time only a positive role for the state resolves it.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the strongest single argument in the chapter for redistribution, and it is a liberal argument.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on liberty against equality, or on whether equality is compatible with utility, should run this dilemma.
- ❌ **Trap.** Attributing the diminishing-utility argument to socialists. ✅ Macpherson derives it from Bentham's own premises.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Quote the fear-of-starvation limb; it is the most damaging sentence the classical case contains about itself.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Classical liberals justified the free market **on the basis of the 'equality' of individuals**. (PDF p.245)
2. ✅ Bentham held that in aggregating utilities **each individual was to count as one**.
3. ✅ The liberal state was **the state most calculated to maximize utilities—security of life, freedom of individual movement, security of property**.
4. ✅ **He was caught in a dilemma—to reconcile theoretical equality with practical inequality.**
5. ✅ He **acknowledged... that there was a case for equality of wealth or income**.
6. ✅ The case follows from **the principle of diminishing utility**: **a second loaf of bread doesn't give a hungry man as much satisfaction as the first**.
7. 🔑 Given equal counting and diminishing utility, **the aggregate utility of the whole society would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth**.
8. ✅ Bentham escaped by **introducing another element—the criterion of productivity—and decided the case in favour of inequality**.
9. ✅ **Without security for unequal property, there would be no incentive to capital accumulation.**
10. ✅ **Without capital accumulation there would be practically no productivity.**
11. 🔑 **Without a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation, the market could not maximize productivity.**
12. ✅ **Subsequent liberal thinkers could not escape from this dilemma so easily.**
13. ✅ The pressures: a growing working class, deteriorating conditions, socialist demands, and liberal recognition of **conditions of oppression**.
14. ⚠️ 🔶 The Macpherson passage crosses the header/body split at PDF pp.246-247; printed page 228 is complete.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how an argument for equality of wealth can be derived from Bentham's own premises, and state exactly what he introduces to block it.

**Model answer:** ✅ Two premises suffice. ✅ First, **each individual's satisfaction was to count as one**. ✅ Second, **the principle of diminishing utility—the principle that a second loaf of bread doesn't give a hungry man as much satisfaction as the first loaf**, or generally that **the more you have of anything the less the utility to you of any increment**. ✅ From these it follows that **the aggregate utility of the whole society would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth**, and Macpherson records that Bentham **acknowledged, indeed, that there was a case for equality of wealth or income**. ✅ What blocks it is **the criterion of productivity**: **without security for unequal property, there would be no incentive to capital accumulation, and without capital accumulation there would be practically no productivity**, and further, **without a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation, the market could not maximize productivity**. ⚠️ The block is therefore empirical, not principled — and empirical blocks can be removed by evidence. 🔑 Utilitarianism argues for equality and then buys its way out with a forecast about growth.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say Bentham rejected equality. ⚠️ He asserted it as an aggregation rule — **each to count as one, and no one for more than one** — and conceded the case for equality of wealth; what he denied was that equality of wealth should be implemented, on the ground that it would reduce total output.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Utilitarianism contains an argument for equality which utilitarians declined to accept." Examine this claim. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ⚠️ The claim is well founded on the chapter's own evidence. ✅ Bentham held that in aggregating utilities **each individual was to count as one**, and accepted **the principle of diminishing utility**, that **the more you have of anything the less the utility to you of any increment**. ✅ Together these entail that **the aggregate utility of the whole society would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth**, and he **acknowledged... that there was a case for equality of wealth or income**. ✅ He declined to accept it by adding **the criterion of productivity**: inequality secures **incentive to capital accumulation**, and the market requires **a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation**. ⚠️ The refusal is therefore empirical and conditional, not a denial of the argument. ⚠️ Once nineteenth-century evidence showed growth accompanied by **child labour, slum housing** and destitution, the condition failed. 🔑 The argument survived its author's refusal.

**Rubric [10]:** both premises stated **3** · the egalitarian conclusion derived **2** · the productivity escape with both limbs **3** · the point that the escape is empirical and defeasible **1** · a closing judgment **1**.

## Lesson 28 — J.S. Mill — utility revised, liberty defended

Progress: 28 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's J.S. Mill entry and the Advanced companion §5's note that Mill begins within liberal individualism and moves toward social responsibility; read against the chapter's **EXPONENTS OF POSITIVE LIBERALISM** heading and the *John Stuart Mill* sub-sections *Revision of Utilitarianism* and *In Defence of Liberty* at PDF pp.247-250 (printed 228-231), including the Joad, Macpherson, Kramnick-Watkins and Barker passages.
CA search: "Mill On Liberty self-regarding other-regarding harm principle contemporary 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Mill's position as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Where the chapter places Mill

✅ **John Stuart Mill (1806-73), was the first prominent liberal thinker who started with a defence of laissez-faire individualism, but on realizing its weaknesses in the light of new socio-economic realities, he proceeded to modify it.** ✅ **In the event he proved himself to be the chief exponent of positive liberalism.**

✅ The chapter adds the structural reason he matters: he **played an important role in drawing a distinction between the political and economic spheres**, so that **in the political sphere he proved himself to be a strong supporter of constitutional and representative government**, while **in the economic sphere he showed socialist leanings and laid the foundations of the 'welfare state'**.

🔑 Two spheres, two Mills, one theory. ⚠️ That split is what allows him to defend the strongest case for individual liberty and simultaneously to open the door to redistribution.

### 2. Revision of utilitarianism

✅ Mill was **brought up in the Utilitarian tradition of Bentham** and was **the most ardent champion of individualism**. ✅ C.E.M. Joad's *Modern Political Theory* (1924) is quoted at length to place him: Mill **insists on regarding every political question in terms of the happiness or unhappiness of human beings, and not... in terms of an abstraction such as the General Will or the personality of the State**; while conceding that **it is only in the State that the individual can enjoy the fullest happiness of which his nature is capable**, he insists that this **does not mean that the State does not exist for the happiness of individuals**, and concludes that **it is the business of Government actively to promote the happiness of individuals**, and that **if it fails in this respect, it must give way to some other form of social organization that succeeds**.

⚠️ That last clause is the welfare state in embryo: performance, not origin, now licenses replacement.

✅ **Mill agreed with Bentham in identifying happiness with pleasure and unhappiness with pain.** ✅ **But he disagreed with Bentham's view that happiness could be measured by quantitative differences of pleasures, not by qualitative differences.** ✅ **Mill maintained that some pleasures were qualitatively superior to others**, which implied **a departure from Bentham's position on the method of aggregation and maximization of pleasure, happiness or utility**.

✅ Macpherson states the consequence: **Mill revolted against Bentham's material maximizing criterion of the social good.** ✅ **He could not agree that all pleasures were equal, nor that the market distributed them fairly.** ✅ **He held that men were capable of something better than the money-grubbing and starvation-avoiding existence to which Benthamism condemned them.** ✅ **He rejected the maximization of indifferent utilities as the criterion of social good, and put in its place the maximum development and use of human capacities—moral, intellectual, aesthetic, as well as material productive capacities.**

🔑 **Maximum development and use of human capacities** is the formula that converts utilitarianism into a doctrine of positive freedom, and it is the direct ancestor of Green's self-realisation at Lesson 30.

⚠️ Notice also the second clause of Macpherson's sentence: Mill **could not agree... that the market distributed them fairly**. ⚠️ That is the economic sphere opening.

### 3. In defence of liberty — two grounds, not one

| ✅ Ground | ✅ Content | ✅ Source quoted |
|---|---|---|
| **Social utility** | **Rational knowledge is the basis of social welfare**, and the only way of **confirming and extending true knowledge is to submit all ideas, old and new, to the test of free discussion and debate** | Joad: *On Liberty* is **perhaps the most famous vindication of freedom of thought, and the most powerful plea for the toleration of opinions we fail to understand, in the whole of literature**; Mill **insisted upon the extension of this freedom to 'cranks', on the ground that, while nine cranks out of ten are harmless idiots, the tenth is of greater value to mankind than all the normal men who seek to suppress him** |
| **Human dignity** | **Individual self-determination is a basic human right, indispensable to the development of any sort of moral responsibility**; **no line of thought or action, objectively true and useful though it may be, is morally significant unless it is followed, freely and consciously, as a matter of personal conviction**; **without liberty to choose between conflicting claims, a human being loses his or her rightful dignity as a moral and rational being** | Isaac Kramnick and F.M. Watkins, *The Age of Ideology—Political Thought, 1750 to the Present* (1979) |

🔑 Two independent grounds matter because they fail differently. ⚠️ The utility ground is hostage to evidence — if suppression produced better knowledge it would collapse. ⚠️ The dignity ground is not, because it concerns the manner in which a belief is held rather than its content.

### 4. Liberty of conduct, and Barker's objection

✅ On conduct Mill **draws a distinction between two types of actions of men: 'self-regarding actions' and 'other-regarding actions'**. ✅ He advocates **complete freedom of conduct for the individual in all matters not affecting the community**, while in **other-regarding actions** he **conceded the right of the community to coerce the individual if his conduct is prejudicial to its welfare**. ✅ **But the state could also interfere in the self-regarding action if it was thought to be very injurious to individual himself** — the state **would be perfectly justified in preventing a man from crossing a bridge which was known to be unsafe**.

✅ **Ernest Barker**, in ***Principles of Social and Political Theory* (1951)**, **has severely criticized Mill for separating the conduct of individual into two parts**:

> We cannot separate two different compartments of individual conduct; but we can separate the sphere of Society from that of the State. Because we cannot separate our individual conduct into two different compartments, and because we are bound to regard the whole of our conduct as concerning others no less than ourselves, we have to admit that the whole of our conduct is controllable—so far as the criterion of its concerning others is the criterion of judgment.
>
> — Ernest Barker, quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.249 (printed 230)

✅ Barker's alternative divides **the sphere of voluntary action, proceeding by the method of free cooperation**, which **should be left to society**, from **the sphere of uniform and regulated action, based, in the last resort, on the method of compulsory enforcement**, which **should be left to the state**. ✅ **Barker, in fact, has tried to shift liberal theory from an individualistic to a pluralistic base.**

⚠️ Two things follow. 🔑 Barker's objection is that Mill drew the line in the wrong place, not that no line exists — he relocates it from *inside the person* to *between institutions*. ⚠️ And that relocation is the chapter's own bridge to MacIver at Lesson 32 and to pluralism at Lesson 49.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Mill supplies the qualitative revision of utility, the classic defence of free expression and the self/other-regarding distinction in one lesson.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on freedom of expression, on paternalism, and on the limits of state interference route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Presenting Mill's self-regarding sphere as absolute. ✅ The unsafe-bridge case shows he admits interference for the person's own protection.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Pair the two grounds for liberty and say which survives contrary evidence; it converts recitation into analysis.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mill **started with a defence of laissez-faire individualism** and became **the chief exponent of positive liberalism**. (PDF p.247)
2. ✅ He distinguished the **political** sphere — constitutional and representative government — from the **economic**, where he **showed socialist leanings and laid the foundations of the 'welfare state'**.
3. ✅ Joad: Mill judges every political question **in terms of the happiness or unhappiness of human beings**, not of **the General Will or the personality of the State**.
4. ✅ Joad: **it is the business of Government actively to promote the happiness of individuals**, failing which **it must give way to some other form of social organization that succeeds**.
5. ✅ Mill **maintained that some pleasures were qualitatively superior to others**.
6. ✅ Macpherson: Mill **revolted against Bentham's material maximizing criterion of the social good** and could not agree **that the market distributed them fairly**.
7. 🔑 He substituted **the maximum development and use of human capacities—moral, intellectual, aesthetic, as well as material productive capacities**.
8. ✅ Liberty of thought, ground 1: **rational knowledge is the basis of social welfare**, confirmed only through **free discussion and debate**.
9. ✅ Joad on *On Liberty*: the extension of freedom to **'cranks'** — **the tenth is of greater value to mankind than all the normal men who seek to suppress him**.
10. ✅ Ground 2: **individual self-determination is a basic human right**; nothing is **morally significant unless it is followed, freely and consciously, as a matter of personal conviction**.
11. ✅ Conduct: **'self-regarding actions'** and **'other-regarding actions'**; coercion permitted where conduct is **prejudicial to** community welfare.
12. ✅ The state may also prevent **a man from crossing a bridge which was known to be unsafe**.
13. ✅ Barker: **we cannot separate two different compartments of individual conduct; but we can separate the sphere of Society from that of the State**.
14. ✅ Barker's division: **free cooperation** to society, **compulsory enforcement** to the state — shifting liberal theory **from an individualistic to a pluralistic base**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Mill defends liberty of thought on two grounds. Separate them, and explain why Barker nonetheless rejects the distinction Mill uses for liberty of conduct.

**Model answer:** ✅ The first ground is social utility: **rational knowledge is the basis of social welfare**, and knowledge is confirmed and extended only by submitting **all ideas, old and new, to the test of free discussion and debate** — hence the extension of freedom even to **'cranks'**, since **the tenth is of greater value to mankind than all the normal men who seek to suppress him**. ✅ The second is dignity: **individual self-determination is a basic human right**, and no view is **morally significant unless it is followed, freely and consciously, as a matter of personal conviction**, without which **a human being loses his or her rightful dignity as a moral and rational being**. ⚠️ The grounds are independent: the first is defeasible by evidence about how knowledge grows, the second is not. ✅ Barker rejects the conduct distinction because **we cannot separate two different compartments of individual conduct**, since **the whole of our conduct** concerns **others no less than ourselves**; the workable line is institutional — **free cooperation** to society, **compulsory enforcement** to the state. 🔑 Mill divides the person; Barker divides the institutions.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Mill's self-regarding sphere as a zone of absolute non-interference. ⚠️ The chapter records that **the state could also interfere in the self-regarding action if it was thought to be very injurious to individual himself**, instancing prevention of a crossing of **a bridge which was known to be unsafe** — so even Mill admits a protective exception.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does J.S. Mill revise utilitarianism, and what does the revision make possible for the theory of the state? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Mill keeps the identification of happiness with pleasure but rejects Bentham's purely **quantitative** measure, maintaining that **some pleasures were qualitatively superior to others**, which departs from Bentham **on the method of aggregation and maximization**. ✅ Macpherson states the substitution: Mill **rejected the maximization of indifferent utilities as the criterion of social good, and put in its place the maximum development and use of human capacities—moral, intellectual, aesthetic, as well as material productive capacities**, holding also that he could not agree **that the market distributed them fairly**. ⚠️ Three consequences follow for the state. ⚠️ A state may now be judged by whether it develops capacities, not merely by whether it sums satisfactions. ⚠️ Market outcomes become assessable rather than definitionally optimal. ⚠️ And government acquires a positive task: **actively to promote the happiness of individuals**, failing which **it must give way**. 🔑 A qualitative standard makes a positive state intelligible.

**Rubric [10]:** the qualitative revision stated **3** · Macpherson's substituted criterion quoted or closely reported **3** · the market-distribution doubt **1** · the three consequences for the theory of the state **2** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 29 — J.S. Mill — the positive role of the state

Progress: 29 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's note that Mill revises liberalism toward social welfare, and the Advanced companion §5's entry on qualified property rights; read against the chapter's *Positive Role of the State* sub-section at PDF pp.250-251 (printed 231-232), including the two Hobhouse passages from *Liberalism* (1911).
CA search: "taxation of unearned increment land rent social basis of property 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Mill's and Hobhouse's account of property. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The historical meaning of the self/other-regarding line

✅ The chapter insists that Mill's distinction **should... be appreciated in the historical perspective**. ✅ **It meant a clear departure from the early laissez-faire individualism which had tried to place most of the behaviour of the individual beyond regulation, in order to vindicate the 'free market society'.** ✅ **Mill was making an attempt to define a sphere where an individual's behaviour could be regulated in the interests of society.**

🔑 Read in that light, the famous distinction is not a restriction on the state but a licence for it. ⚠️ Against a doctrine that placed *everything* economic beyond regulation, naming a regulable class of actions is an expansion.

✅ **Thus he was contemplating a positive role for the state in securing community welfare even if it implied curbing the liberty of the individual to some extent.** ✅ **In his mature years, Mill definitely moved beyond traditional liberalism.** ✅ **Indeed, it was in pursuance of his liberal values that Mill sought to lay the foundations of a more humane society as against the one provided by nineteenth century capitalism.**

### 2. The revision on property

✅ The chapter dates the change precisely. ✅ **In the first edition of his *Principles of Political Economy* (1848), Mill had attacked Socialism, but in its last edition (1866), he revised his views on the right to property as he realized its implications as the basis of the capitalist system.**

| ✅ Claim | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| Property not absolute | **The right to property was not absolute or sacrosanct** |
| Inheritance | He advocated **considerable restrictions on the rights of inheritance and bequest** |
| Land | **The right to property in land was not sacrosanct because no man made the land; it was the original inheritance of all mankind** |
| Rent | **Rent was the effect of natural monopoly—not a product of an individual's effort: it was a fit subject for taxation** |
| Unearned increase | **Incomes of landlords continued to increase without any effort, risk or sacrifice on their part** |
| The state's title | If the state **appropriated the increase of their wealth or a part thereof for diverting it to the use of the community it was no violation of the principles on which the right to private property was founded or justified** |
| Who should receive it | The riches **should properly be diverted to the welfare of their real authors, that is the working class who were the real producers of wealth from the land** |

🔑 The argument's elegance is that it taxes landlords *by* the labour theory of property, not against it. ⚠️ Locke justified property as the **fruit of labour**; Mill observes that rent is not, and the justification therefore does not reach it.

✅ **This approach to the problem of taxation and the limitation of the right to property, heralded a new era of positive liberalism which was developed by later liberal thinkers who thoroughly rejected the tenets of laissez-faire individualism.**

### 3. Hobhouse: the social basis of property

✅ **L.T. Hobhouse**, in ***Liberalism* (1911)**, generalises Mill's move from land to wealth as such:

> The basis of property is social . . . The prosperous businessman who thinks that he has made his fortune entirely by self-help does not pause to consider what single step he could have taken on the road to his success but for the ordered tranquility which has made commercial development possible, the security by road, and rail, and sea, the masses of skilled labour, and the sum of intelligence which civilization has placed at his disposal, the very demand for the goods which he produces which the general progress of the world has created, the inventions which he uses as a matter of course and which have been built up by the collective effort of generations of men of science and organizers of industry. If he dug to the foundations of his fortune he would recognize that, as it is society that maintains and guarantees his possessions, so also it is society which is an indispensable partner in its original creation.
>
> — L.T. Hobhouse, *Liberalism* (1911), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF pp.250-251 (printed 231-232)

✅ From this he derives the theory of taxation: **The true function of taxation is to secure to society the element in wealth that is of social origin, or more broadly, all that does not owe its origin to the efforts of living individuals.** ✅ **When taxation, based on these principles, is utilized to secure healthy conditions of existence to the mass of people it is clear that this is no case of robbing Peter to pay Paul.**

✅ **Thus the idea of social origin of property and its corresponding responsibility toward society, as originally conceived by Mill, served as the basis of positive liberalism of the later era.**

⚠️ Set this directly against the libertarian claim at Lesson 25 that taxation is **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**. 🔑 Hobhouse's reply is that the fruit was never one man's alone, because **society... is an indispensable partner in its original creation** — the two positions disagree about the facts of production before they disagree about justice.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the liberal justification of redistributive taxation without any socialist premise.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on the right to property, on taxation, and on whether liberty and equality can be reconciled route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Writing that Mill became a socialist. ✅ The chapter says he **showed socialist leanings** in the economic sphere while remaining a constitutionalist in the political one.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use the edition dates — **1848** attack, **1866** revision — as evidence of a documented change of mind.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Mill's distinction marked **a clear departure from the early laissez-faire individualism** which placed most behaviour **beyond regulation**. (PDF p.250)
2. ✅ He was **defining a sphere where an individual's behaviour could be regulated in the interests of society**.
3. ✅ He contemplated **a positive role for the state in securing community welfare**.
4. ✅ ***Principles of Political Economy***: socialism attacked in the first edition (**1848**), views on property revised in the last (**1866**).
5. ✅ **The right to property was not absolute or sacrosanct**; he urged restrictions on **inheritance and bequest**.
6. ✅ Land is not sacrosanct **because no man made the land; it was the original inheritance of all mankind**.
7. ✅ **Rent was the effect of natural monopoly—not a product of an individual's effort: it was a fit subject for taxation.**
8. ✅ Landlords' incomes increase **without any effort, risk or sacrifice on their part**.
9. ✅ Appropriating that increase **was no violation of the principles on which the right to private property was founded**.
10. ✅ It should go to **the working class who were the real producers of wealth from the land**.
11. ✅ Hobhouse: **The basis of property is social.**
12. ✅ The businessman owes **ordered tranquility**, **security by road, and rail, and sea**, **masses of skilled labour**, **the very demand for the goods which he produces**, and **the inventions which he uses as a matter of course**.
13. ✅ **It is society which is an indispensable partner in its original creation.**
14. ✅ **The true function of taxation is to secure to society the element in wealth that is of social origin**; this is **no case of robbing Peter to pay Paul**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how Mill and Hobhouse justify taxation of wealth from within liberal premises rather than against them.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Both argue from the labour justification of property rather than against it. ✅ Mill observes that **the right to property in land was not sacrosanct because no man made the land**, that **rent was the effect of natural monopoly—not a product of an individual's effort**, and that landlords' incomes rise **without any effort, risk or sacrifice on their part**; so appropriating that increase **was no violation of the principles on which the right to private property was founded or justified**. ✅ Hobhouse generalises: **the basis of property is social**, since no fortune could have been made without **ordered tranquility**, **security by road, and rail, and sea**, **masses of skilled labour** and **the very demand for the goods which he produces**, so that **society... is an indispensable partner in its original creation**. ✅ Taxation then simply **secures to society the element in wealth that is of social origin**, and is **no case of robbing Peter to pay Paul**. 🔑 Property is defended as earned, and what is taxed is what was not earned.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present this as a socialist argument against private property. ⚠️ Both retain the right and restrict its scope; Hobhouse's criterion is **all that does not owe its origin to the efforts of living individuals**, which leaves what does owe its origin to such effort untouched.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine the transformation of the liberal theory of property from Locke to Hobhouse, and assess whether it abandons liberal premises. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Locke grounds property in labour: when an individual **mixes his labour with natural resources, this act is sufficient to establish his exclusive right on the product which does not depend on the consent of other people**, and the state **has no power to take anything more without the owner's consent**. ✅ Classical liberalism then treats property as **the condition of progress** and **a necessary condition of liberty**, and libertarianism later hardens this into entitlement, condemning redistribution as **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**. ✅ Mill breaks the chain at a specific joint. ✅ Revising his *Principles of Political Economy* between **1848** and **1866**, he holds that **the right to property was not absolute or sacrosanct**, that land is not sacrosanct **because no man made the land**, and that **rent was the effect of natural monopoly—not a product of an individual's effort**, so taxing the unearned increase **was no violation of the principles on which the right to private property was founded**. ✅ Hobhouse generalises the move: **the basis of property is social**, since **society... is an indispensable partner in its original creation**, and taxation therefore **secures to society the element in wealth that is of social origin**. ⚠️ Liberal premises are not abandoned but applied consistently. ⚠️ The labour justification is retained and simply denied to holdings that labour did not produce. ⚠️ What is abandoned is the *inference* from property-as-earned to property-as-untouchable. 🔑 Not a departure from Locke's principle but a refusal to extend it beyond its warrant.

**Rubric [15]:** Locke's labour justification stated **3** · the libertarian hardening noted **2** · Mill's two revisions with the edition dates **4** · Hobhouse's social-basis argument with the taxation criterion **4** · a reasoned verdict on continuity with liberal premises **2**.

## Lesson 30 — T.H. Green — moral freedom and rights

Progress: 30 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Green entry and the Advanced companion §5's note that rights arise from moral community; read against the chapter's *Thomas Hill Green* sub-sections *Concept of Moral Freedom* and *Theory of Rights* at PDF pp.251-253 (printed 232-234), including the Barker passages from *Political Thought in England—1848 to 1914* (1928).
CA search: "positive liberty self-realization moral consciousness rights recognition 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Green's account as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Where Green stands

✅ **T.H. Green is another outstanding exponent of positive liberalism.** ✅ **He sought to revise liberal theory of the state under the influence of idealist theory, derived from the teachings of Rousseau, Kant and Hegel.**

⚠️ That sentence names the chapter's own bridge between divisions. 🔑 Green imports idealist resources into liberalism — which is why Lesson 31 must then show where he stops short of idealism, and why communitarianism at Lesson 39 lists him among its ancestors.

### 2. Moral freedom: the two senses

✅ **At the outset, Green recognizes 'moral freedom' as the distinctive quality of man.**

| ⚠️ Sense | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| **Negative freedom** | **The satisfaction of one's desires, acting according to one's own choice or sweet will**; **the sphere where man enjoys being left alone** |
| **Positive freedom** | **Acting according to reason, achieving self-realization or self-perfection** |

✅ **True liberty or positive freedom of man, therefore, consists in the act of 'good will', whereby man identifies himself with his ideal self or character.** ✅ Barker's elucidation is the memorable form:

> Liberty can only be liberty for this good will: it can only be liberty for the pursuit of the objects which such a will presents to itself. Liberty is therefore no negative absence of restraint, any more than beauty is the absence of ugliness. It is 'a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying'.
>
> — Ernest Barker, *Political Thought in England—1848 to 1914* (1928), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.251 (printed 232)

⚠️ The beauty analogy is doing real work. 🔑 Removing ugliness does not produce beauty, and removing restraint does not produce freedom; both require something positive to be present. ⚠️ That is the argument division II never answers.

### 3. The theory of rights

✅ **Exercise of true liberty, according to Green, postulates rights.** ✅ **Rights do not emanate from any transcendental law as Locke had imagined, but they emanate from the moral character of man himself.**

✅ The derivation is reciprocal: **under a system of rights, each individual recognizes in his fellow, and each claims from his fellow, that he shall recognize in him the power of pursuing ideal objects**. ✅ **Since each individual is a moral being, and in this respect all individuals are alike, it follows that the ideal objects of all are common objects.** ✅ **In other words, rights imply permission to pursue ideal objects; and since these are the common objects of all men, theoretically there is no question of a clash between the rights of different individuals.**

🔑 That last clause is a very strong claim: rights, properly understood, cannot conflict. ⚠️ It is available in answers on rights-conflict questions as the strongest idealist position, and it is vulnerable exactly where ideal objects are genuinely plural.

✅ **Since rights exist within a social system, Green argues that there can be no unrecognized rights.** ✅ **But recognition does not mean that all rights are legal rights only.** ✅ **Green draws a clear distinction between the state and society and holds that the recognizing authority in the matter of rights is not the state, but the moral consciousness of the community.**

✅ **The consciousness of the community signifies an eternal consciousness.** ✅ **Human consciousness or consciousness of the ideal self is but a part of that eternal consciousness, not of the mechanical order of nature.** ✅ **Therefore, man can pursue his moral end and attain self-realization only in a social community, not in isolation.** ✅ So Green **is concerned not with legal rights, but with ideal rights**, realisable **when society is properly organized on the basis of 'good will'**.

✅ Barker again: **The rights of which Green speaks are relative to morality rather than law; and the recognition of which he speaks is recognition by a common moral consciousness rather than by a legislature.** ✅ The rights are relative to morality **in the sense that they are the... end; and the recognition is given by the moral consciousness, because it knows that they are the necessary conditions of its own satisfaction.**

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Green supplies the definition of positive liberty and a theory of rights that is neither natural-law nor legal-positivist.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems contrasting negative and positive liberty, or asking where rights come from, route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying Green derives rights from the state. ✅ **The recognizing authority... is not the state, but the moral consciousness of the community.**
- ✅ **Answer use.** Barker's beauty analogy is the most efficient refutation of pure negative liberty available in the chapter.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Green **sought to revise liberal theory of the state under the influence of idealist theory, derived from the teachings of Rousseau, Kant and Hegel**. (PDF p.251)
2. ✅ **Moral freedom** is **the distinctive quality of man**.
3. ✅ Negative freedom: **satisfaction of one's desires, acting according to one's own choice or sweet will**; **being left alone**.
4. ✅ Positive freedom: **acting according to reason, achieving self-realization or self-perfection**.
5. ✅ True liberty **consists in the act of 'good will', whereby man identifies himself with his ideal self or character**.
6. 🔑 Barker: liberty **is no negative absence of restraint, any more than beauty is the absence of ugliness**; it is **a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying**.
7. ✅ **Rights do not emanate from any transcendental law as Locke had imagined, but... from the moral character of man himself.**
8. ✅ Each **claims from his fellow** recognition of **the power of pursuing ideal objects**.
9. ✅ Since all are moral beings, **the ideal objects of all are common objects**, so **theoretically there is no question of a clash between the rights of different individuals**.
10. ✅ **There can be no unrecognized rights**, but **recognition does not mean that all rights are legal rights only**.
11. ✅ **The recognizing authority in the matter of rights is not the state, but the moral consciousness of the community.**
12. ✅ That consciousness **signifies an eternal consciousness**, of which human consciousness is **but a part**.
13. ✅ **Man can pursue his moral end and attain self-realization only in a social community, not in isolation.**
14. ✅ Barker: Green's rights are **relative to morality rather than law**, recognised **by a common moral consciousness rather than by a legislature**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain Green's claim that rights cannot clash, and identify the premise on which it depends.

**Model answer:** ✅ The claim is that **rights imply permission to pursue ideal objects; and since these are the common objects of all men, theoretically there is no question of a clash between the rights of different individuals**. ⚠️ The reasoning is reciprocal: each individual **recognizes in his fellow, and each claims from his fellow**, recognition of **the power of pursuing ideal objects**; and **since each individual is a moral being, and in this respect all individuals are alike, it follows that the ideal objects of all are common objects**. ⚠️ The load-bearing premise is therefore the identity of ideal objects across persons — that what each truly wills, qua moral being, is the same. ⚠️ If ideal objects are genuinely plural, the conclusion fails immediately, because two people may each be pursuing a worthwhile end that excludes the other's. ⚠️ Green's qualifier **theoretically** concedes as much. 🔑 The harmony of rights is purchased by a prior harmony of ends, which is exactly what later pluralist and communitarian arguments dispute.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Green's positive freedom as a licence for the state to impose virtue. ⚠️ The chapter keeps the recognising authority outside the state — it is **the moral consciousness of the community** — and Lesson 31 records Green's own view that the state's function is to remove obstacles, not to make members better.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** "Negative liberty seems to embody ultimate personal freedom, whereas positive liberty enables individuals to achieve their full potential." Examine this contrast through Green. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Green defines negative freedom as **the satisfaction of one's desires, acting according to one's own choice or sweet will** — **the sphere where man enjoys being left alone** — and positive freedom as **acting according to reason, achieving self-realization or self-perfection**, consisting in the act of **'good will'** by which man **identifies himself with his ideal self**. ✅ Barker shows why the first cannot be sufficient: liberty **is no negative absence of restraint, any more than beauty is the absence of ugliness**; it is **a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying**. ⚠️ The contrast therefore misstates the relation. ⚠️ Positive liberty does not compete with negative liberty but supplies its content, since an unrestrained agent without capacity or worthwhile objects is not free in any sense that matters. ⚠️ The risk is the converse one: an authority that defines **worth doing** may coerce in freedom's name. 🔑 Removing obstacles is necessary; it is not enough.

**Rubric [10]:** both definitions in Green's terms **3** · Barker's analogy used **2** · the argument that positive liberty supplies content rather than competing **3** · the risk of a defined "worth doing" **1** · a closing judgment **1**.
