## Lesson 31 — T.H. Green — the state, property and the dilemma

Progress: 31 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Green entry on the state as instrument and the Advanced companion §5's note that the state removes obstacles rather than embodying perfection; read against the chapter's *Role of the State* and *Right to Property* sub-sections at PDF pp.253-255 (printed 234-236), including Barker's "supreme limitation" passage and Macpherson's criticism.
CA search: "state removes obstacles compulsory education property in capital criticism 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Green's theory of the state. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The derivation of the state

✅ **The moral consciousness emanating from society—which impels men to pursue ideal objects—is also responsible for the creation of the state.** ✅ **In other words, the state is the product of moral consciousness.**

```text
  HUMAN CONSCIOUSNESS  postulates  LIBERTY
          LIBERTY      involves    RIGHTS
          RIGHTS       demand      THE STATE
                            |
  therefore the state is "an INSTRUMENT of perfection as the liberal
  theory holds; it is NOT an EMBODIMENT of perfection -- as the
  idealist theory claims"
```

🔑 That one contrast — **instrument** against **embodiment** — is where Green stops being an idealist. ⚠️ It is the single most examinable sentence in this sub-section.

✅ The state **owes its origin to the social nature of men**; **genuine human personality is essentially a social phenomenon**; **it is inconceivable that an isolated natural man should be a moral agent**. ✅ Man exercises moral freedom **within the social organization, for which he needs rights**; **but rights are maintained by the state; hence the state serves as an essential base for moral freedom**.

### 2. Where Green joins the idealists and where he leaves them

| ⚠️ Move | ✅ Green's position |
|---|---|
| With the idealists | He **favours subordinating individual to the community as Rousseau and Hegel had maintained**, and **insists on duty of the citizen to follow the general will and devote himself to the common good** |
| Against the idealists | He **does not treat the state and society as coterminous** — **this marks his departure from the idealist tradition** |
| With the liberals | He **adheres to the liberal tradition by insisting on individual's rights** |
| On the source of rights | **State recognizes and maintains rights but it is not the source of rights**; **the real authority behind rights is the moral consciousness of the community. The state must obey that authority** |
| Consequence | **Positive law can be criticized and improved upon in the light of the state's ideal purpose: the moral perfection of men** |

✅ **Green exalts society or the community as the primary and eternal source of moral consciousness. The state is something secondary, a means or an instrument.** ✅ Therefore the state **cannot serve the end of moral freedom directly, but it can create favourable conditions for the exercise of moral freedom**; **the state and its law can regulate only the external order of society**.

✅ Barker states the limit:

> The supreme limitation on the State lies in its own essence. Its function is essentially, Green conceives, a negative function. It is limited to the removal of the obstructions that hamper human capacity when it seeks to do 'things worth doing'. The state has no positive moral function of making its members better: it has the negative moral function of removing the obstacles which prevent them from making themselves better.
>
> — Barker, *Political Thought in England 1848 to 1914* (1928), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.253 (printed 234)

✅ **In other words, the function of government is to maintain conditions of life in which morality shall be possible.** ✅ **Morality consists in the disinterested performance of self-imposed duties, not in obeying the commands of the state.**

⚠️ A terminological trap follows immediately, and the chapter defuses it. ✅ Green **tends to describe the function of the state as negative, only to distinguish it from the positive function of the community**. ✅ **He is by no means a champion of negative liberalism.** ✅ **When compared to laissez-faire liberalism, Green is definitely an exponent of positive liberalism.** ✅ **The state's function of removing obstacles in the way of men's pursuit of ideal objects is a positive function.** ✅ And the practical consequence: **his conception of the state as an agent for moral improvement led him to favour the intervention of the state to secure the welfare of the citizens. That is why he argued that the state is entitled to make education compulsory.**

🔑 **Negative** in Green names a *relation to the community*, not a minimal state. ⚠️ Confusing the two converts Green into Spencer.

### 3. Property, functional inequality, and the dilemma

✅ **As a defender of rights, Green upholds the right to property as a means of realizing a will potentially directed to the social good.** ✅ **On this ground he even defends property in capital.** ✅ Barker's summary: there is **nothing in its essence which is anti-social**; **on the contrary, it is constantly being distributed through the community in wages to labourers and in profits to those who are engaged in exchange**; **nor is there anything in the fact that labourers are hired in masses by capitalists to prevent them from being, on a small scale, capitalists themselves**. ✅ **On the same ground of potential social value Green also defends inequality of property.**

✅ The reason is functional: **the freedom of the individual postulates freedom to acquire and possess material goods according to one's potentiality to contribute to the social good**; **the social good requires that different individuals should fill different positions in the social whole**; hence **differences in property are 'functional' from the point of view of the social good, which should be recognized by the social conscience**.

✅ **But on this point—the question of the inequality of property—Green is faced with a dilemma.** ✅ **When the right to property creates conditions under which some men take an unduly large share and others are prevented from acquiring property as a means of their 'self-realization', this right becomes an obstacle in the exercise of moral freedom by the many.**

✅ Green locates the cause in **the system of 'landed property' as it existed in England**, which he **disapproved**. ✅ Barker: such property **is unique** in being **limited** — **the capital gained by one is not taken from another, but one man cannot acquire more land without others having less** — and in being **the basis on which the whole tower of modern society rests**: **from it alone can be derived the materials necessary for any industry: on it men must find house-room; over it they must pass in communicating with each other**. ✅ **It is thus to the system of landed property that Green seems inclined to assign the creation of a proletariat, neither holding nor acquiring property.**

### 4. Macpherson's verdict

✅ **Green, of course, made a significant contribution to liberal political theory by discovering the moral foundations of social life, and by subordinating the state to the will of society which alone embodies moral consciousness.** ✅ **But he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system.** ✅ Macpherson:

> Green recognized that the existence of a proletariat—his own word—was inconsistent with the rationale of private property, which required that everybody should have enough property, over and above a bare subsistence, to enable him to develop and perfect himself. But he had so little insight into the nature of capitalism that he could attribute the existence of a proletariat not to the nature of capitalist enterprise but to the continuing effect of an original forcible seizure of land in feudal times, and subsequent 'unrestricted landlordism'. By putting the blame on feudalism, and on the continuing rights of unproductive landowners, he exempted capitalism from responsibility for the condition of the bulk of the people.
>
> — Macpherson, *Democratic Theory—Essays in Retrieval* (1973), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.254 (printed 235)

🔑 **He exempted capitalism from responsibility** is the chapter's own verdict delivered through a quotation, and it is one of the authorial judgments identified at Lesson 9 — the words **he made a great mistake** are Gauba's own.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Green supplies the welfare state's philosophical justification and its sharpest internal criticism in a single sub-section.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on positive liberty, on compulsory education, or on whether liberal theory can explain poverty route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Reading Green's **negative function** as minimal-state doctrine. ✅ The chapter expressly denies it.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Pair the instrument/embodiment contrast with Macpherson's criticism; one shows what Green achieved, the other what he missed.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **The state is the product of moral consciousness.** (PDF p.253)
2. ✅ **Human consciousness postulates liberty; liberty involves rights; rights demand the state.**
3. 🔑 The state is **an instrument of perfection**, **not an embodiment of perfection—as the idealist theory claims**.
4. ✅ Green **favours subordinating individual to the community as Rousseau and Hegel had maintained**, but **does not treat the state and society as coterminous**.
5. ✅ **State recognizes and maintains rights but it is not the source of rights**; **the state must obey** the moral consciousness of the community.
6. ✅ **Positive law can be criticized and improved upon in the light of the state's ideal purpose: the moral perfection of men.**
7. ✅ Barker: the state's function is **limited to the removal of the obstructions that hamper human capacity when it seeks to do 'things worth doing'**.
8. ✅ **The state has no positive moral function of making its members better**, only **the negative moral function of removing the obstacles**.
9. ✅ **Morality consists in the disinterested performance of self-imposed duties, not in obeying the commands of the state.**
10. ✅ **He is by no means a champion of negative liberalism**; removing obstacles **is a positive function**.
11. ✅ He held **the state is entitled to make education compulsory**.
12. ✅ He defends **property in capital** and **inequality of property**, since differences in property are **'functional' from the point of view of the social good**.
13. ✅ The dilemma: where some take **an unduly large share**, the right **becomes an obstacle in the exercise of moral freedom by the many**.
14. ✅ Barker on land: **one man cannot acquire more land without others having less**; it is **the basis on which the whole tower of modern society rests**.
15. ✅ Macpherson: by blaming feudalism and **'unrestricted landlordism'**, Green **exempted capitalism from responsibility for the condition of the bulk of the people**; Gauba: **he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Green calls the state's function negative yet is classified as a positive liberal. Resolve the apparent contradiction, and state the criticism that this very position attracts.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The word **negative** in Green marks a contrast with the community, not with an active state. ✅ The chapter says he **tends to describe the function of the state as negative, only to distinguish it from the positive function of the community as the source of moral consciousness**, and adds that **he is by no means a champion of negative liberalism** and that **the state's function of removing obstacles in the way of men's pursuit of ideal objects is a positive function**. ✅ Barker's formulation is consistent with this: the state **has no positive moral function of making its members better** but **the negative moral function of removing the obstacles**; obstacle-removal is nonetheless action, which is why Green held **the state is entitled to make education compulsory**. ⚠️ The criticism follows from what he counted as an obstacle. ✅ He defends **property in capital** and **functional** inequality, and traces the proletariat to **landed property** and **'unrestricted landlordism'** rather than to capitalist enterprise — so, in Macpherson's words, he **exempted capitalism from responsibility for the condition of the bulk of the people**. 🔑 An activist state pointed at the wrong obstacle.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say Green makes the state the source of rights. ⚠️ The chapter is explicit: **state recognizes and maintains rights but it is not the source of rights**, and **the real authority behind rights is the moral consciousness of the community. The state must obey that authority.**

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine T.H. Green's conception of the state and assess the objection that it misidentifies the sources of social injustice. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Green derives the state from morality: **human consciousness postulates liberty; liberty involves rights; rights demand the state**, so the state is **an instrument of perfection... not an embodiment of perfection—as the idealist theory claims**. ✅ It is secondary to society, must **obey** the **moral consciousness of the community**, and can only **create favourable conditions for the exercise of moral freedom**, since it **can regulate only the external order of society**. ✅ Barker fixes its limit to **the removal of the obstructions that hamper human capacity when it seeks to do 'things worth doing'**, with **no positive moral function of making its members better**. ⚠️ This is nonetheless an activist doctrine: the chapter insists Green **is by no means a champion of negative liberalism** and notes his support for **compulsory** education. ⚠️ The objection concerns which obstacles he recognised. ✅ Green defends **property in capital** and treats differences in property as **'functional' from the point of view of the social good**, locating the proletariat's existence in **landed property**, which alone is **limited** — **one man cannot acquire more land without others having less**. ✅ Macpherson replies that he **had so little insight into the nature of capitalism** that he blamed feudal seizure and **'unrestricted landlordism'**, thereby exempting **capitalism from responsibility for the condition of the bulk of the people**; Gauba agrees that **he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system**. ⚠️ The framework survives the objection; the diagnosis does not. 🔑 Right instrument, wrong obstacle.

**Rubric [15]:** the three-step derivation and the instrument/embodiment contrast **4** · the obstacle-removal limit with Barker **3** · the clarification that "negative" does not mean minimal **2** · the property and functional-inequality defence **2** · Macpherson's criticism and Gauba's own judgment **3** · a verdict separating framework from diagnosis **1**.

## Lesson 32 — Laski, MacIver and the verdict on liberal theory

Progress: 32 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block D — III. Welfare state perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Laski and MacIver entries and the Advanced companion §5's note that MacIver gives liberalism a pluralist-service orientation; read against the chapter's *Harold J. Laski* and *Robert M. MacIver* sub-sections at PDF pp.255-260 (printed 236-241) and the division's closing judgment at PDF p.260.
CA search: "democratization of economic power service state welfare liberal democracy 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's treatment of Laski and MacIver. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### Part A — Laski: socialism's goal by liberal democracy's method

✅ **Laski has further developed the liberal theory of the state in the light of his experience of the momentous events which shook the world in the first half of the twentieth century** — **the First World War (1914-18)**, **the Socialist Revolution in Russia (1917)**, **the formation of the League of Nations (1920)**, **the rise of fascism in Italy (1919-20)** and **the Great Depression in Europe (1929)**.

✅ **He was deeply concerned with the crisis of capitalism, yet he saw no promise in the outcome of the socialist revolution.** ✅ **In the event, he sought to achieve the socialist goal through the mechanism of liberal democracy.** ✅ At times he **advocated the abolition of the right to property**, but **he was so deeply attached to liberal democratic values that ultimately he compromised in favour of making necessary changes in the capitalist system so as to make it an instrument of securing social justice**.

✅ In ***State in Theory and Practice* (1935)** Laski speaks of the **breakdown of capitalism** under mature conditions: **the test of an economic system is... the test of its capacity to exploit to the full all the potentialities of its productive power**, and by that test it is **surely not illegitimate to speak of a 'breakdown' of capitalism both in England and the United States**. ✅ His illustration: **two million unemployed; devastated economic areas like South Wales and the North East Coast; the staple export trades, like cotton and coal, iron and steel and shipping, announcing that they verge on bankruptcy**.

✅ In ***Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (1943)** he deprecates **the undemocratic control of industry and politics by the economic overlords**:

> In modern society, large industrial corporations are controlled by a caste of economic directors, mainly remarkable for their skill in financial manipulation, who are masters alike of their shareholders and of the consumer, and are not seldom in a position to hold even the states to ransom. Their power is as massive in volume as it has largely been irresponsible in operation. We have reached a stage in historical evolution where either their power must be subordinated to the interest of the community or the interest of the community will be a tragic pseudonym for their power.
>
> — Laski, *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (1943), quoted at Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.255 (printed 236)

⚠️ Yet Laski **did not approve of doctrinaire communism or the repetition of the Russian Revolution**; he **insisted on the unity of the working class, but hoped that they could achieve their goal within the framework of a liberal democracy**, seeing **no inherent contradiction between the goals of Marxian socialism and the method of political democracy**.

✅ His evidence is the modern state's welfare record: **there is hardly a function of social welfare undertaken by governments today which is not an effort to provide the poor with some, at least, of the amenities that the rich are able to provide for themselves**; the state **seeks to convince its citizens that its action is unbiased by organizing for them the material conditions of an adequate life**. ✅ The range since 1919 includes **health, education, housing, social insurance, the regulation of hours and wages in industry, the control of factory conditions, the provision of meals for poor school children**, which he calls **the outcome of a profounder social conscience**.

✅ On that basis Laski **refuses the Marxist interpretation of the state as a class-instrument**: **no modern state would subordinate human rights to the interests of property**, and when the state **concerns itself with the quality of our food, the protection of child welfare, the safeguarding of the unemployed against industrial insecurity, the provision of educational opportunity—all of these services provided at the expense of the taxpayers—it is rhetorical exaggeration to regard it as a class-instrument**.

✅ He is **not fascinated by the Marxist vision of a 'stateless' society**, insists on **a clear distinction between the state and society**, warns against vesting society's authority in **a band of officials—fallible human beings**, yet in ***A Grammar of Politics* (1938)** calls the state **the keystone of the social arch** which **moulds the form and substance of the myriad human lives with whose destinies it is charged**. ✅ He **pins his faith on the perfectibility of the state**, with a stated test: **the claim of the state to obedience... rests upon its will and ability to secure to its citizens the maximum satisfaction of their wants**, requiring **an absence of bias**; **where the effort of the state is seriously perverted to the interest of some special group within the society it controls, sooner or later revolution is likely to occur**.

✅ **Thus Laski postulates that the state is not essentially an instrument of class power or class domination or class exploitation. If any such state exists in the real world, it represents a perverted form of the state.**

✅ His programme: refine liberal democracy politically, and economically **tone down the rigours of the capitalist system by making it welfare-oriented**, without **anything like the disappearance of private enterprise**, while eliminating **the enormous economic disparities of the present system** — **men will still be able to make fortunes; but, especially in the period of transition, they will be subject to heavy taxation upon income, and still heavier duties upon their estates at death**. ✅ In short, **the democratization of economic power**: **larger public control over vital instruments of production and distribution, reducing enormous economic disparities by a progressive system of taxation and establishing a democratic state increasingly concerned with the welfare of its citizens**.

### Part B — MacIver: the service state

✅ **R.M. MacIver is another twentieth century exponent of positive liberalism**, who **traces the evolution of the state from primitive social structures to its fuller development as a modern democratic state**.

| ✅ Claim | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| On origin | He **rejects the social contract theory** and **tends to agree with T.H. Green**, while **differing from Green in making all rights, ethical as well as political, depend on social recognition** |
| On state and society | He approves Green's distinction; political law exists **simply for the removal of obstacles in the way of free moral activity within society**, so **the state has a limited sphere and cannot be identified with the whole activity of society** |
| On associations | **It is society which meets all the needs of human personality**; **the state is only one of such associations**; many existed **even before the formation of the state** |
| On law | **Law itself exists above the state, but it is declared and enforced by the state**; **the government has power as the guardian of the constitution, as the executor of law, not in its own right**; the state **does not create law of its own will** |
| On limits | **The whole creative side of human thought and endeavour, including religion and morality in its proper sense, are outside the sphere of the state**; it should enforce only **those acts the doing of which, from whatever motive, is necessary for the good life within society** |
| On regulation | The state **comes into the picture only when the interests of one group encroach upon another**; it **can only harmonize different social interests**. In *The Web of Government* (1965) he adds that organisations serving **emotional and cultural interests** should not be regulated, while those serving **economic interests** cannot be left to mutual adjustment — so **employer and employee, trader and consumer** fall within state regulation while **religious, artistic and cultural activities** do not |
| The service state | Authority rests on function: **the state... commands only because it serves; it owns only because it owes. It creates rights not as the lordly dispenser of gifts, but as the agent of society for the creation of rights. The servant is not greater than his master** |
| On democracy | The state **can act... as a unifying agent, but only in so far as it has itself undergone evolution towards democracy**; hence **we regard democracy as the form of the state proper** |
| On history | **Historically, the interest of the state has been identified with that of the ruling class—military or landed oligarchy, or later plutocracy**; the modern democratic state **stands not for the interests of a particular class, but to serve the interests of all society** |

🔑 MacIver's peculiar function for the state is worth memorising: **its own peculiar function is no other than that of giving a form of unity to the whole system of social relationships**, achievable **without arrogating to itself again that omnicompetence which it has vainly sought to establish**.

### Part C — The division's closing verdict

✅ **This line of argument shows that MacIver has no apprehension of the evils of the capitalist system as long as democratic mechanism of the state is kept intact.** ✅ Some recent champions of the liberal state have gone further, holding that **under the capitalist system of production actual power has shifted from the hands of the capitalist class** to **a 'managerial class' as James Burnham (1907-87) has maintained**, or **to a variety of power elites, as C. Wright Mills (1916-62) has pointed out**.

✅ Then the division's unheaded conclusion:

> The great merit of the liberal theory of the state lies in evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government. Its tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system, with its inherent contradictions—the conditions of dominance and dependence it creates in the economic sphere, in spite of its policy of social welfare and incremental change. The needs of social justice demand a thorough transformation of the economic system as well. It is for the genius of the present-day world to evolve more effective structures to secure social justice.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, PDF p.260 (printed 241)

🔑 **Merit** and **tragedy** in consecutive sentences, both in the author's own voice. ⚠️ This is the hinge into division IV: the demand for **a thorough transformation of the economic system** is exactly what the class perspective supplies.

### Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Laski and MacIver complete the welfare division and supply the strongest liberal answers to the Marxist charge taught next.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on whether the state is a class instrument should set Laski's refusal against Lesson 35's case.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating MacIver's service state as a theory of welfare provision. ✅ It is a theory of the *ground* of authority: command is justified by service.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Close any liberal-state answer with the merit/tragedy pair; it is balanced, textual and memorable.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Laski's formative events: **First World War (1914-18)**, **Russian Revolution (1917)**, **League of Nations (1920)**, **fascism in Italy (1919-20)**, **the Great Depression (1929)**. (PDF p.255)
2. ✅ He **sought to achieve the socialist goal through the mechanism of liberal democracy**.
3. ✅ *State in Theory and Practice* (1935): a **'breakdown' of capitalism both in England and the United States**, with **two million unemployed** and **South Wales and the North East Coast** devastated; *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (1943): **a caste of economic directors... are not seldom in a position to hold even the states to ransom**.
4. ✅ Welfare range since 1919: **health, education, housing, social insurance, the regulation of hours and wages in industry, the control of factory conditions, the provision of meals for poor school children** — **the outcome of a profounder social conscience**.
5. ✅ **It is rhetorical exaggeration to regard it as a class-instrument.**
6. ✅ *A Grammar of Politics* (1938): **The State is the keystone of the social arch.**
7. ✅ The obedience test: the state's claim **rests upon its will and ability to secure to its citizens the maximum satisfaction of their wants** with **an absence of bias**; perversion to a special group makes **revolution... likely**.
8. ✅ Programme: **heavy taxation upon income**, **still heavier duties upon their estates at death**, and **the democratization of economic power**, without **the disappearance of private enterprise**.
9. ✅ MacIver **rejects the social contract theory**; differs from Green by making **all rights, ethical as well as political, depend on social recognition**. (PDF p.258)
10. ✅ **The state is only one of such associations**; **law itself exists above the state**; government **has power as the guardian of the constitution... not in its own right**.
11. ✅ Outside the state's sphere: **religion and morality in its proper sense**; inside: **employer and employee, trader and consumer**.
12. 🔑 **It commands only because it serves; it owns only because it owes.**
13. ✅ **Democracy** is **the form of the state proper**; the state's **peculiar function** is **giving a form of unity to the whole system of social relationships** without **omnicompetence**.
14. ✅ **Burnham (1907-87)** on a **managerial class**; **C. Wright Mills (1916-62)** on **power elites**.
15. 🔑 The **great merit** of liberal theory is **evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government**; its **tragedy** is that it **is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Laski denies that the modern state is a class instrument; MacIver denies that it is superior to other associations. State the evidence each offers, and explain how the chapter's closing verdict qualifies both.

**Model answer:** ✅ Laski's evidence is the welfare record: **there is hardly a function of social welfare undertaken by governments today which is not an effort to provide the poor with some, at least, of the amenities that the rich are able to provide for themselves**, and when the state concerns itself with **the quality of our food, the protection of child welfare, the safeguarding of the unemployed... it is rhetorical exaggeration to regard it as a class-instrument**. ✅ MacIver's evidence is functional and legal: **law itself exists above the state**, government **has power as the guardian of the constitution, as the executor of law, not in its own right**, and the state **commands only because it serves; it owns only because it owes**. ⚠️ The chapter qualifies both by observing that MacIver **has no apprehension of the evils of the capitalist system as long as democratic mechanism of the state is kept intact**, and by its closing verdict that liberal theory's **tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system, with its inherent contradictions—the conditions of dominance and dependence it creates in the economic sphere**. 🔑 Welfare and constitutionalism are real and may still leave economic domination untouched.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Laski's refusal of the class-instrument thesis as a defence of capitalism. ⚠️ The same thinker speaks of a **'breakdown' of capitalism**, of directors who **hold even the states to ransom**, and demands **the democratization of economic power**; what he rejects is the inference from capitalist inequality to the impossibility of a non-class state.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Assess the welfare-state perspective as a liberal response to the inequalities of capitalism. *(Assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The perspective begins by conceding the charge: the free-market society **created large inequalities** and produced **child labour, slum housing** and **labour... treated as a commodity**, failing even **the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists**. ✅ It answers from within liberalism. ✅ Mill replaces indifferent utility with **the maximum development and use of human capacities** and denies that land and rent, not being **a product of an individual's effort**, lie beyond taxation; Hobhouse generalises this, since **the basis of property is social** and **society... is an indispensable partner in its original creation**. ✅ Green makes liberty **a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying** and charges the state with **removal of the obstructions that hamper human capacity**, justifying compulsory education. ✅ Laski seeks **the socialist goal through the mechanism of liberal democracy**, by **heavy taxation upon income**, death duties and **the democratization of economic power**. ✅ MacIver grounds authority in function — **it commands only because it serves** — and makes **democracy... the form of the state proper**. ⚠️ The achievement is substantial: a defensible theory of the activist state that preserves constitutional limits. ⚠️ The limitation is the chapter's own. ✅ Its **tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**, whose **conditions of dominance and dependence** persist **in spite of its policy of social welfare and incremental change**, so that **the needs of social justice demand a thorough transformation of the economic system as well**. 🔑 It repairs the symptoms and leaves the structure standing.

**Rubric [15]:** the concession that opens the division **2** · at least three exponents with their distinctive moves **6** · Laski's programme or MacIver's service state **2** · the chapter's merit/tragedy verdict **3** · a reasoned assessment rather than restatement **2**.

## Lesson 33 — What the class perspective denies

Progress: 33 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's class-theory definition and the Advanced companion §4's "class instrument" entry; read against the restored division heading **IV. CLASS PERSPECTIVE** at PDF p.260 (printed 241), the chapter's opening denial at PDF pp.260-261, and the boxed definition of **Marxism** at PDF p.261 (printed 242).
CA search: "Marxist theory of the state class instrument contemporary assessment 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's statement of the class perspective. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. A perspective defined by three refusals

✅ **Class perspective on the state is associated with Marxism.** ✅ **It is different from the mechanistic theory as well as from the organic theory.** ✅ **It treats the state neither as a 'natural institution' nor as an 'ethical institution' as the organic theory has held.** ✅ **It, of course, treats the state as an artificial device. But unlike the mechanistic theory, it treats the state neither as a manifestation of the will of the people, nor as an instrument of reconciliation of conflicting interests.**

```text
   DENIES (organic, Lesson 11-12)   "natural institution"
   DENIES (organic, Lesson 12)      "ethical institution"
   ACCEPTS (mechanistic, L15)       "artificial device"
   DENIES (contract, L15)           "manifestation of the will of
                                     the people"
   DENIES (MacIver, L32)            "instrument of reconciliation of
                                     conflicting interests"
            |
   LEAVES   the state as something ARTIFICIAL, made by SOME people,
            for THEIR OWN benefit
```

🔑 Four denials and one acceptance. ⚠️ Writing the position as a set of refusals is more accurate than writing it as a set of assertions, because it shows exactly which earlier division each refusal answers.

### 2. The box: Marxism

✅ The chapter supplies a boxed definition at PDF p.261 (printed 242):

> A set of political and economic principles founded by Karl Marx (1818-83) and Friedrich Engels (1820-95) in order to lay scientific foundations of socialism. It seeks to understand the problems of human society through historical analysis, and treats history as a process of conflict between antagonistic forces and classes. This conflict arises from the faults in the mode of production in which one class comes to gain ownership and control of the means of social production (land, buildings, mines, forests, machinery and capital, etc.) and compels the other class to work on the terms and conditions dictated by itself. This conflict can be resolved only by overthrowing capitalism, placing all means of social production under social ownership and control, enforcing universal labour and ensuring full development of the forces of production.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, boxed definition, PDF p.261 (printed 242)

⚠️ Four examinable elements sit inside that box and are easy to miss because it is a box:

| ⚠️ Element | ✅ Content |
|---|---|
| Purpose | **To lay scientific foundations of socialism** |
| Method | **Historical analysis**; history as **a process of conflict between antagonistic forces and classes** |
| Cause | **Faults in the mode of production** by which one class gains **ownership and control of the means of social production** |
| What counts as means of production | **Land, buildings, mines, forests, machinery and capital** |
| Remedy, in four parts | **Overthrowing capitalism**; **social ownership and control**; **enforcing universal labour**; **ensuring full development of the forces of production** |

🔑 The four-part remedy is the most useful list in the division, because the Gandhian comparison at Lesson 46 contests each part separately.

### 3. The generative claim

✅ **According to the class theory, the state comes into existence when society is divided into two antagonistic classes, one owning the means of social production and the other being constrained to live on its labour.** ✅ **In other words, it is the emergence of 'private property' that divides society into two conflicting classes.** ✅ **Those owning the means of production acquire the power to dominate the other class not only in the economic sphere but in all spheres of life.**

⚠️ Three steps, in order: property → classes → domination beyond the economy. 🔑 The third step is the one that makes this a theory of the *state* rather than a theory of the economy, and it is the step Weber will deny at Lesson 37.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Opening a Marxist-state answer with the four denials places the theory in the chapter's argument instead of in a vacuum.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems asking to "evaluate Marxism as a political ideology" want the box's four-part remedy as the positive content.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating the class theory as a version of the mechanistic theory because both call the state artificial. ✅ The chapter separates them expressly.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Quote the three-step generative claim; it is short, exact and carries the whole doctrine.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Class perspective on the state is associated with Marxism.** (PDF p.260)
2. ✅ It is **different from the mechanistic theory as well as from the organic theory**.
3. ✅ It denies that the state is a **'natural institution'** or an **'ethical institution'**.
4. ✅ It accepts that the state is **an artificial device**.
5. ✅ It denies that the state is **a manifestation of the will of the people**.
6. ✅ It denies that the state is **an instrument of reconciliation of conflicting interests**.
7. ✅ Marxism was founded by **Karl Marx (1818-83)** and **Friedrich Engels (1820-95)** **in order to lay scientific foundations of socialism**.
8. ✅ It treats history as **a process of conflict between antagonistic forces and classes**.
9. ✅ The conflict arises from **the faults in the mode of production**.
10. ✅ Means of social production: **land, buildings, mines, forests, machinery and capital**.
11. ✅ One class **compels the other class to work on the terms and conditions dictated by itself**.
12. 🔑 Remedy: **overthrowing capitalism**, **social ownership and control**, **enforcing universal labour**, **full development of the forces of production**.
13. ✅ The state **comes into existence when society is divided into two antagonistic classes**.
14. ✅ **It is the emergence of 'private property' that divides society into two conflicting classes**, and owners dominate **not only in the economic sphere but in all spheres of life**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The class perspective shares one claim with the mechanistic theory and rejects four. Identify them, and explain what the shared claim commits both theories to.

**Model answer:** ✅ Shared: the state is **an artificial device**. ✅ Rejected: that it is a **'natural institution'**; that it is an **'ethical institution'**; that it is **a manifestation of the will of the people**; and that it is **an instrument of reconciliation of conflicting interests**. ⚠️ The first two rejections answer the organic division, the third answers contract theory, and the fourth answers MacIver's harmonising state. ⚠️ The shared claim commits both theories to historicity and to assessment by results: an artefact appears at a datable point, serves identifiable purposes, and can in principle be altered or abolished. ⚠️ That is why the class theory can look forward to a condition in which the state becomes redundant, which the organic theory cannot even formulate. ⚠️ The two then diverge completely on the maker: liberal theory says the state is **the product of the will of society**, whereas the class theory makes it **an instrument devised by a dominant class for its own benefit**, **imposed on society from above**. 🔑 Agreement about what kind of thing the state is; total disagreement about whose thing it is.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the boxed definition of Marxism as background. ⚠️ It supplies the chapter's only statement of the four-part remedy — **overthrowing capitalism**, **social ownership and control**, **enforcing universal labour** and **full development of the forces of production** — and each part is separately contested in the Gandhian comparison later in the chapter.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** State the class perspective on the state and distinguish it from the perspectives it rejects. *(State and distinguish — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ On the class perspective the state **comes into existence when society is divided into two antagonistic classes, one owning the means of social production and the other being constrained to live on its labour**; **it is the emergence of 'private property' that divides society**, and owners then dominate **not only in the economic sphere but in all spheres of life**. ✅ It is distinguished by four denials. ✅ Against the organic theory it denies that the state is a **'natural institution'** or an **'ethical institution'**. ✅ Against contract theory it denies that the state is **a manifestation of the will of the people**. ✅ Against the liberal-pluralist account it denies that the state is **an instrument of reconciliation of conflicting interests**. ⚠️ It retains only the mechanistic claim that the state is **an artificial device** — but artificial by class imposition rather than by common consent. 🔑 Same ontology as liberalism, opposite authorship.

**Rubric [10]:** the three-step generative claim **3** · all four denials with the perspective each answers **4** · the retained mechanistic claim **2** · a closing contrast on authorship **1**.

## Lesson 34 — The state as an instrument of the dominant class

Progress: 34 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's spine entry on Marxism and the Advanced companion §3's note that the state is historically specific; read against the chapter's sub-heading **THE STATE AS AN INSTRUMENT OF THE DOMINANT CLASS** at PDF pp.261-262 (printed 242-243).
CA search: "political power derived from economic power primitive communism surplus production 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's derivation of political from economic power. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Political power as an invention

✅ **With the emergence of 'private property', society is divided into 'dominant' and 'dependent' classes.** ✅ **The dominant class, in order to maintain its stronghold on economic power, invents a new form of power—political power.** ✅ **The state is the embodiment of political power. It is, therefore, essentially subservient to economic power.**

🔑 The verb **invents** is the whole claim. ⚠️ Political power is not a separate species of power that economic power happens to capture; it is a device created *by* holders of economic power *for* the protection of economic power — which is why, on this account, reforming political institutions cannot by itself change the relation.

✅ Hence the state **neither originates in the will of the people, nor does it stand for the benefit of all society, but is an instrument devised by a dominant class for its own benefit**; it is **imposed on society from above to serve the interests of a particular class**. ✅ **The state has not existed from eternity. It came into existence at a particular stage of historical development. It is a product of the conscious effort of the dominant class which first acquires the means of production and thereafter political power.** ✅ **The state is, therefore, by no means a natural institution as the organic theory has maintained.**

### 2. Society natural, state artificial

✅ **Unlike the organic theory, again, the class theory makes a clear distinction between the state and society.** ✅ **Society and the state do not come into existence together.** ✅ **The class theory treats society as a natural institution, and the state as an artificial device.** ✅ **In other words, man is by nature a social animal, but not a 'political animal' as Aristotle had assumed.**

✅ The reason society is natural is productive: **it is an essential condition for the production of material goods which are indispensable for the survival of man**; **production is the most important of all human activities**; and **since men in association produce more than men in isolation, society is a natural means of securing the necessities of life**.

⚠️ Notice the precise form of the reply to Aristotle. 🔑 The chapter does not deny that human beings are incomplete in isolation; it relocates the completion from the *polis* to *production*. ⚠️ Aristotle's premise survives; his conclusion does not.

### 3. The historical sequence

```text
  PRIMITIVE COMMUNISM
    "the state has not yet made its appearance"
    means of production "meagre and COMMUNALLY OWNED"
    "no private property; hence society is not divided into
     antagonistic classes"
            |
  DEVELOPMENT OF THE MEANS OF PRODUCTION
    hunting, fishing and food-gathering REPLACED BY
    "animal husbandry, domestic agriculture and small industry"
            |
  SURPLUS PRODUCTION
    "cornered by a class owning the means of production"
            |
  CLASS DIVISION
    "'dominant' and 'dependent' classes come into existence"
            |
  "The structure of society is always determined by the prevalent
   form of production"
```

✅ The chapter's illustration is the classic one: **The hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord; the steam-mill gives you society with the industrial capitalist.** ✅ And the generalisation: **the attitudes and outlook of society—the legal, political and intellectual relations as well as the religious and social systems—are also determined by the material conditions of life**. ✅ **Whatever the form of the state, it is invariably an instrument of the dominant class.**

⚠️ The hand-mill sentence is doing more than illustrating. 🔑 It asserts that a technique of production carries a whole social structure with it, which is the strongest form of the determination claim — and precisely the form that Gramsci, Miliband and Poulantzas will later soften at Lesson 38.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the derivation of the state from property and the Marxist answer to Aristotle in one page.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on historical materialism and its relevance to social change begin here and are completed at Lesson 37.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying Marxism denies that man is a social being. ✅ It affirms it — **man is by nature a social animal** — and denies only that he is a **'political animal'**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** The hand-mill/steam-mill line is the most compact statement of economic determination available; use it and then immediately note who qualified it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The dominant class **invents a new form of power—political power**. (PDF p.261)
2. ✅ **The state is the embodiment of political power... essentially subservient to economic power.**
3. ✅ It is **an instrument devised by a dominant class for its own benefit**, **imposed on society from above**.
4. ✅ **The state has not existed from eternity**; it **came into existence at a particular stage of historical development**.
5. ✅ It is **a product of the conscious effort of the dominant class which first acquires the means of production and thereafter political power**.
6. ✅ **Society and the state do not come into existence together**; society is **a natural institution**, the state **an artificial device**.
7. ✅ **Man is by nature a social animal, but not a 'political animal' as Aristotle had assumed.**
8. ✅ Society is natural because **production is the most important of all human activities** and **men in association produce more than men in isolation**.
9. ✅ Under **primitive communism** means of production are **meagre and communally owned**; **there is no private property**.
10. ✅ Development replaces hunting, fishing and food-gathering with **animal husbandry, domestic agriculture and small industry**.
11. ✅ **Surplus production** is **cornered by a class owning the means of production**, producing **'dominant' and 'dependent' classes**.
12. ✅ **The structure of society is always determined by the prevalent form of production.**
13. 🔑 **The hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord; the steam-mill gives you society with the industrial capitalist.**
14. ✅ **Whatever the form of the state, it is invariably an instrument of the dominant class.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how the class theory answers Aristotle without denying that human beings are incomplete in isolation.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ It concedes the incompleteness and relocates its source. ✅ The chapter affirms that **man is by nature a social animal**, and grounds that in production: society **is an essential condition for the production of material goods which are indispensable for the survival of man**, **production is the most important of all human activities**, and **since men in association produce more than men in isolation, society is a natural means of securing the necessities of life**. ⚠️ So the isolated individual is indeed not self-sufficing, exactly as Aristotle argued. ✅ What is denied is the next step: man is **not a 'political animal' as Aristotle had assumed**, because the association that completes him is society, not the state, and **society and the state do not come into existence together**. ✅ The state appears later, when **surplus production** is **cornered by a class owning the means of production**, and is therefore **an artificial device**, **a product of the conscious effort of the dominant class**. 🔑 Aristotle's premise retained, his identification of society with the state rejected.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say the Marxist state arises from scarcity or from disorder. ⚠️ The chapter makes its trigger **surplus production** cornered by owners — abundance, not want — and its function the protection of that cornering, which is why **the state is the embodiment of political power... essentially subservient to economic power**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How does the class theory derive the state from the ownership of the means of production? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ In four steps. ✅ Under **primitive communism** the means of production are **meagre and communally owned**, there is **no private property**, and society is **not divided into antagonistic classes**. ✅ As production develops from hunting and food-gathering to **animal husbandry, domestic agriculture and small industry**, there is **surplus production which is cornered by a class owning the means of production**, so **'dominant' and 'dependent' classes come into existence**. ✅ The dominant class then **invents a new form of power—political power**, and **the state is the embodiment of political power**, **essentially subservient to economic power**. ✅ Hence the state is **imposed on society from above to serve the interests of a particular class** and **has not existed from eternity**. ⚠️ The determination claim is strong — **the structure of society is always determined by the prevalent form of production**, since **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord**. 🔑 Ownership first, political power second.

**Rubric [10]:** the four-step sequence **4** · the invention of political power **2** · the subservience of political to economic power **2** · the hand-mill illustration or the determination claim **1** · a closing line on sequence **1**.

## Lesson 35 — The state as an instrument of class exploitation

Progress: 35 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Marx-Engels-Lenin entry and the Advanced companion §5's note that the state secures exploitation and eventually withers away; read against the chapter's sub-heading **THE STATE AS AN INSTRUMENT OF CLASS EXPLOITATION** at PDF pp.262-263 (printed 243-244), with the four quoted texts of 1848, 1880, 1884 and 1917.
CA search: "withering away of the state dictatorship of the proletariat classless stateless society 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of exploitation and withering away. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Suppression, not resolution

✅ **The dominant class uses the machinery of the state to serve its own interests which involve the exploitation of the dependent class.** ✅ **The state is, therefore, an instrument of oppression and exploitation, an embodiment of injustice.** ✅ **It does not rest on moral foundations as the organic theory believes.** ✅ **It is not even an instrument of harmonizing the interests of various individuals or groups as the mechanistic theory claims.**

✅ Then the formulation that carries the division: **Instead of being a means of conflict-resolution, the state, according to the class theory, is a device for the suppression of class conflict.**

✅ And the two instruments by which it works: **it maintains order in society not because it is able to secure the willing obedience of its subjects, but because it uses its coercive power to secure compliance from the dependent class**; and **the state also uses its 'ideological power' to create an illusion of 'consent' of the governed as also to offer moral justification for its existence**.

```text
                  ORDER IN SOCIETY
                        |
        +---------------+---------------+
        |                               |
   COERCIVE POWER                 IDEOLOGICAL POWER
   secures COMPLIANCE             creates "an illusion of 'consent'
   from the dependent class       of the governed" + "moral
                                  justification for its existence"
        |                               |
        '--------- NOT "willing obedience" ---------'
```

🔑 **Ideological power** is named here, at PDF p.262, long before Gramsci appears at PDF p.266. ⚠️ The chapter therefore has the concept before it has the theorist, which is useful: an answer can note that Gramsci systematises a mechanism classical Marxism already asserted.

### 2. The four canonical texts

| ✅ Text | ✅ Year | ✅ Statement |
|---|---|---|
| Marx and Engels, *Communist Manifesto* | **1848** | **Political power, properly to called, is merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another** |
| Engels, *Socialism—Utopian and Scientific* | **1880** | **The State is an organization of the particular class which was pro-tempore the exploiting class** |
| Engels, *The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State* | **1884** | **The state of antiquity was above all the state of the slave-owners for the purpose of holding down the slaves, as the feudal state was the organ of the nobility for holding down the peasant serfs and bondsmen, and the modern representative state is an instrument of exploitation of wage labour by capital** |
| Lenin, *The State and Revolution* | **1917** | **According to Marx, the State is an organ of class rule, an organ for the oppression of one class by another, it is the creation of 'order', which legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict between the classes** |

⚠️ Engels's 1884 formulation is the most useful of the four because it is a three-stage history in one sentence — slave-owning, feudal, capitalist — and it demonstrates the theory's claim that the *form* changes while the *character* does not.

⚠️ Lenin's addition is also precise: order is **the creation of 'order', which legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict between the classes**. 🔑 Moderation is the mechanism of perpetuation, not its opposite — which is exactly the objection to any welfare defence of the capitalist state.

### 3. The route out

✅ **Since the class theory views the state as an embodiment of social injustice, it looks forward to the emancipation of mankind in a classless and stateless society.**

✅ **Historical forces till the establishment of capitalism have introduced changes in the form of the state—from slave-owning to feudal to capitalist state—but they have failed to transform the class character of the state itself.**

✅ The transformation is to be achieved **by a socialist revolution by which the 'proletariat' (propertyless workers) would expropriate the 'bourgeoisie' (capitalists) and 'socialize' the major means of production**. ✅ **The state would still be retained as an instrument of 'class exploitation', but the hitherto 'dominant' and 'dependent' classes would change their positions** — the socialist state, the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'**, **would use the state apparatus for the suppression of the former capitalists**.

✅ **When all remnants of capitalism are cleared, work is made compulsory for everyone, and the forces of production are fully developed, society would become classless, and the state would become redundant. This would result in the 'withering away' of the state.** ✅ The result is a classless and stateless society **characterized by 'cooperation' instead of conflict, 'equality' instead of domination and 'justice' instead of oppression and exploitation of one class by another**.

⚠️ Three conditions are stated for withering away, and candidates routinely give only the first. 🔑 **All remnants of capitalism... cleared**, **work... made compulsory for everyone**, and **the forces of production... fully developed** — the second and third are substantive and are precisely where the Gandhian comparison at Lesson 46 and the critical estimate at Lesson 36 press.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This is the core doctrinal content for any Marxist-state stem and for historical-materialism stems.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "Critically examine the Marxist theory of the state" expects this lesson plus Lesson 36's three weaknesses plus Lesson 38's internal debate.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying the state is abolished by the revolution. ✅ It is **retained**, with the classes reversed, and withers only when three conditions are met.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Cite Engels 1884 for the three historical forms; it compresses an entire argument.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The state is **an instrument of oppression and exploitation, an embodiment of injustice**. (PDF p.262)
2. ✅ It does not rest on **moral foundations**, nor harmonise interests.
3. 🔑 **Instead of being a means of conflict-resolution, the state... is a device for the suppression of class conflict.**
4. ✅ It uses **coercive power to secure compliance from the dependent class**.
5. ✅ It uses **'ideological power' to create an illusion of 'consent' of the governed** and to offer **moral justification for its existence**.
6. ✅ *Communist Manifesto* (**1848**): political power is **merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another**.
7. ✅ Engels (**1880**): the state is **an organization of the particular class which was pro-tempore the exploiting class**.
8. ✅ Engels (**1884**): **the state of antiquity was above all the state of the slave-owners**, **the feudal state was the organ of the nobility**, and **the modern representative state is an instrument of exploitation of wage labour by capital**.
9. ✅ Lenin (**1917**): the state is **an organ of class rule**, **the creation of 'order', which legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict between the classes**.
10. ✅ Changes in form **from slave-owning to feudal to capitalist** have **failed to transform the class character of the state itself**.
11. ✅ The **proletariat (propertyless workers)** expropriates the **bourgeoisie (capitalists)** and socialises the major means of production.
12. ✅ The state is **still... retained as an instrument of 'class exploitation'**, now as the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'**.
13. 🔑 Withering away requires three conditions: **all remnants of capitalism are cleared**, **work is made compulsory for everyone**, and **the forces of production are fully developed**.
14. ✅ The outcome: **'cooperation' instead of conflict, 'equality' instead of domination and 'justice' instead of oppression**.

### Concept check

**Question:** The class theory calls the state a device for the suppression of class conflict rather than for its resolution. Explain the difference, and show how the same distinction governs the account of the socialist state.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Resolution would remove the conflict's cause; suppression manages its expression while leaving the cause in place. ✅ The chapter says the state **maintains order in society not because it is able to secure the willing obedience of its subjects, but because it uses its coercive power to secure compliance from the dependent class**, supplemented by **'ideological power' to create an illusion of 'consent'**. ✅ Lenin makes the mechanism explicit: order **legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict between the classes** — moderation is how perpetuation works. ⚠️ The same distinction governs the socialist state. ✅ After the revolution **the state would still be retained as an instrument of 'class exploitation'**, with **'dominant' and 'dependent' classes... change their positions**, the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'** suppressing **the former capitalists**. ⚠️ Suppression therefore continues; what ends it is not a better state but the removal of the cause — **when all remnants of capitalism are cleared, work is made compulsory for everyone, and the forces of production are fully developed**. 🔑 The state cannot resolve what it exists to manage.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not say the proletarian revolution abolishes the state. ⚠️ The chapter is explicit that the state is **retained**, and that it **would become redundant** only when three stated conditions are satisfied — after which it **withers away** rather than being abolished by decree.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain the Marxist claim that the state is an instrument of class exploitation, and the conditions under which it is said to wither away. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The state is **an instrument of oppression and exploitation, an embodiment of injustice**, and **a device for the suppression of class conflict** rather than of its resolution, working through **coercive power** and **'ideological power' to create an illusion of 'consent'**. ✅ The *Manifesto* (1848) calls political power **merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another**; Engels (1884) traces the same character through **the state of the slave-owners**, **the organ of the nobility** and **the modern representative state... an instrument of exploitation of wage labour by capital**; Lenin (1917) adds that order **legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict**. ✅ Revolution does not end it: the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'** retains the apparatus to suppress **the former capitalists**. ✅ The state becomes redundant only when **all remnants of capitalism are cleared, work is made compulsory for everyone, and the forces of production are fully developed**. 🔑 Three conditions, then withering.

**Rubric [10]:** the suppression-not-resolution claim **2** · both instruments, coercive and ideological **2** · at least two of the four canonical texts with years **3** · the retention of the state after revolution **1** · all three withering conditions **2**.
