## Lesson 36 — A critical estimate of the class theory

Progress: 36 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §9's Marxist traps and the Advanced companion §7's note that Marxism faces social-mobility, welfare-capitalist and authoritarian objections read from the book's 2009 vantage point; read against the chapter's second **A CRITICAL ESTIMATE** at PDF pp.263-265 (printed 244-246), with its three named weaknesses.
CA search: "middle class expansion welfare capitalism new class critique of Marxism 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's critical estimate. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. The concession, stated generously

✅ **The chief contribution of class theory of the state lies in discovering and demonstrating the role of economic forces in shaping history.** ✅ **The earlier historians had hardly paid attention to this role. By focusing on this role, Marx opened up new possibilities of historical writing.**

✅ **In fact the class theory of the state was a very powerful attack on the complacency of social thinkers who held that the state existed for the benefit of all social groups, and that the different conditions of the rich and the poor were of their own making.** ✅ **This theory sought to trace the roots of social injustice in the conditions created by the social system itself which was claimed to be an instrument of the 'common good' and justice.** ✅ **The class theory has demonstrated that the poverty of the masses is the outcome of their oppression and exploitation by property-owners.** ✅ **It has also shown how the conditions of this injustice could be terminated by a proletarian revolution.**

⚠️ Note which earlier division is named in that concession: the complacent view that **the different conditions of the rich and the poor were of their own making** is exactly the laissez-faire position of division II. 🔑 The chapter is using Marxism as the corrective to a doctrine it has already taught.

✅ Then the turn: **However, the class theory, too, is not free from weaknesses, which need examination.**

```text
  CONCESSION
    "the role of economic forces in shaping history"
    + an attack on the view that the conditions of rich and poor
      "were of their own making"
    + "the poverty of the masses is the outcome of their oppression
      and exploitation by property-owners"
          |
  WEAKNESS 1  "No Rigid Class Division in Society"
              social mobility | the MIDDLE CLASS grows, not disappears
              | BURNHAM 1941: a new MANAGERIAL class
          |
  WEAKNESS 2  "Capitalism with a Human Face"
              labour legislation | progressive taxation | social
              services => "class-conflict and revolutionary spirit
              are becoming irrelevant and outdated"
          |
  WEAKNESS 3  "No Signs of Classless Society in the Socialist
              Countries"
              USSR and PRC | "much more repressive" | "a pyramid of
              power" | DJILAS 1957: "a new privileged class"
              | the theory itself termed 'UTOPIAN'
          |
  RESIDUE
    look instead for "STRUCTURES OF DOMINATION" and "FORMS OF
    DOMINATION", and attack them "in order to restore freedom,
    equality and justice in society"
```

### 2. Weakness one — No Rigid Class Division in Society

✅ Critics **have pointed out that classes are not fixed and rigidly maintained blocks within society as this theory assumes**. ✅ **Liberal thinkers have pointed to the constant process of social mobility—changing status of men and women by dint of their effort and other social circumstances.**

✅ The specific prediction attacked is polarisation: **the class theory holds that under the capitalist system there will be a sharp polarization in which the 'middle class' will eventually disappear**. ✅ **Some liberal researchers have, however, shown that under the capitalist system the size of the middle class has actually been steadily increasing, without any symptoms of its disappearance.**

✅ And an alternative account is named: **James Burnham, in his *The Managerial Revolution* (1941), has attempted to demonstrate that power has, in fact, shifted from the hands of capitalists to a new managerial class—a phenomenon never envisaged by the class theory of the state.**

⚠️ Mark the verbs. 🔑 **Liberal researchers have... shown** and Burnham **has attempted to demonstrate** are reported attributions in the chapter's distancing register, identified at Lesson 9 — not the author's own findings.

### 3. Weakness two — Capitalism with a Human Face

✅ **The supporters of capitalism have pointed out that twentieth century capitalism was different from nineteenth century capitalism when Marxism emerged.** ✅ **These thinkers claimed that capitalism had transformed itself by adopting the model of 'welfare state', and had itself become an instrument of social justice.**

✅ The evidence cited: **the extensive labour legislation, a progressive system of taxation and elaborate provision of common social services, especially for the needy sections, are cited as the glowing examples of capitalism with a human face**. ✅ **It is claimed that with the mitigation of the rigours of capitalism, the class-conflict and revolutionary spirit are becoming irrelevant and outdated.**

⚠️ This is Laski's argument from Lesson 32 returned as an objection to Marxism, and the chapter presents it as a claim of **the supporters of capitalism** rather than as a finding. 🔑 It is therefore available in an answer as a reported position with an identifiable interest, which is a stronger handling than treating it as fact.

### 4. Weakness three — No Signs of Classless Society in the Socialist Countries

✅ **It is alleged that in the former USSR and the People's Republic of China, where socialist revolutions along Marxist lines had taken place, there were no indications of the emergence of a classless and stateless society.** ✅ **On the contrary, the state-apparatus in socialist countries proved to be much more repressive than that in liberal democracies, and their social set-up was characterized by a pyramid of power, instead of moving towards classlessness.**

✅ The named evidence: **M. Djilas (1911-95), a Yugoslav thinker, in his *The New Class* (1957), pointed out that although major property holdings in the Soviet Union and Eastern Europe had been abolished, those who held senior positions in state administration and in the Communist Party had emerged as a new privileged class, relegating the idea of a classless society to the background.**

✅ **Many thinkers even termed the class theory of the state as 'Utopian' although this theory itself was advanced to repudiate 'utopian socialism' of the nineteenth century by 'scientific socialism'.** ✅ **As the later developments have shown, the popular protest against administrative excesses and corruption have led to the winding up of communism in the Soviet Union as well as Eastern Europe.**

🔑 Djilas's objection is the most dangerous of the three because it is internal: it uses the Marxist criterion — a group's relation to the means of production and its share of the surplus — to identify a new dominant class produced by socialism itself.

⚠️ ❌ Note the irony the chapter marks: a theory that defined itself by repudiating **'utopian socialism'** through **'scientific socialism'** is itself **termed... 'Utopian'**.

### 5. The residue

✅ **In fact, class theory of the state is now being revised by its champions.** ✅ **It is now being increasingly realized that instead of looking for a rigid class division in the present-day society, it would be more fruitful to look for the different 'structures of domination' and 'forms of domination' and launch a systematic attack on such structures and forms in order to restore freedom, equality and justice in society.**

⚠️ That is the chapter's residue, and it matters beyond this division. 🔑 **Structures of domination** is precisely the vocabulary in which the feminist and post-colonial perspectives operate — so the Marxist revision opens the door to the last four divisions of the chapter.

### 6. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** The three named weaknesses plus the residue is the complete critical half of any Marxist-state answer.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "How far is Marxism relevant today?" is answered by the residue, not by the weaknesses alone.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating the collapse of communism as a refutation of the class analysis. ✅ The chapter treats it as a refutation of a prediction about socialist states, and records the analysis continuing in revised form.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Always end with **structures of domination**; it is the chapter's own statement of what survives.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The chief contribution is **discovering and demonstrating the role of economic forces in shaping history**. (PDF p.263)
2. ✅ **Marx opened up new possibilities of historical writing.**
3. ✅ It attacked the complacency that **the different conditions of the rich and the poor were of their own making**.
4. ✅ It showed that **the poverty of the masses is the outcome of their oppression and exploitation by property-owners**.
5. ✅ Weakness 1: **classes are not fixed and rigidly maintained blocks within society**; liberals point to **the constant process of social mobility**.
6. ✅ The theory predicted that **the 'middle class' will eventually disappear**; researchers showed its size **steadily increasing**.
7. ✅ **James Burnham**, ***The Managerial Revolution* (1941)**: power shifted **to a new managerial class—a phenomenon never envisaged by the class theory**.
8. ✅ Weakness 2: **capitalism had transformed itself by adopting the model of 'welfare state'**.
9. ✅ Cited evidence: **extensive labour legislation, a progressive system of taxation and elaborate provision of common social services**.
10. ✅ The claim is that **class-conflict and revolutionary spirit are becoming irrelevant and outdated**.
11. ✅ Weakness 3: in the former **USSR** and the **People's Republic of China** there were **no indications of the emergence of a classless and stateless society**.
12. ✅ The socialist state-apparatus **proved to be much more repressive than that in liberal democracies**, with **a pyramid of power**.
13. ✅ **M. Djilas (1911-95)**, ***The New Class* (1957)**: senior **state administration** and **Communist Party** figures **emerged as a new privileged class**.
14. ✅ The theory advanced to replace **'utopian socialism'** with **'scientific socialism'** was itself **termed... 'Utopian'**; communism was **wound up** in the USSR and Eastern Europe.
15. 🔑 Residue: look instead for **'structures of domination' and 'forms of domination'** and attack them **in order to restore freedom, equality and justice in society**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Of the three weaknesses the chapter names, explain which is internal to Marxism and why that makes it the most difficult to answer.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The third — the absence of classlessness in socialist states — and specifically Djilas's version of it. ✅ **M. Djilas (1911-95)**, in ***The New Class* (1957)**, **pointed out that although major property holdings in the Soviet Union and Eastern Europe had been abolished, those who held senior positions in state administration and in the Communist Party had emerged as a new privileged class**. ⚠️ It is internal because it applies the theory's own test. ⚠️ Marxism identifies a class by its control of the means of production and its appropriation of the surplus; Djilas shows that abolishing private title transferred that control to officials rather than dissolving it, so a new dominant class appeared by the theory's own criteria. ⚠️ The other two objections can be met from outside: social mobility and a growing middle class may be described as changes in the form of class relations, and welfare capitalism may be read as a concession extracted by organised labour. ⚠️ Djilas cannot be met that way, which is why the chapter records that the theory is **now being revised by its champions** toward **structures of domination**. 🔑 An objection framed in the theory's own vocabulary cannot be deflected by redescription.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not present these weaknesses as the chapter's own verdicts. ⚠️ Two are explicitly reported — **the supporters of capitalism have pointed out** and **it is alleged that** — and only the closing sentence on revision toward **structures of domination** is delivered in the author's own register.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Critically examine the Marxist theory of the state. *(Critically examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The theory makes the state appear with **private property**, as a device the dominant class **invents** to protect economic power, operating through **coercive power** and **'ideological power'** as **a device for the suppression of class conflict**; Engels (1884) traces one character through **the state of the slave-owners**, **the organ of the nobility** and **the modern representative state**, and the remedy is revolution, the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'**, and **'withering away'** once three conditions are met. ⚠️ Its contribution is substantial. ✅ It lies in **discovering and demonstrating the role of economic forces in shaping history**, in attacking the complacent view that the conditions of rich and poor **were of their own making**, and in showing that **the poverty of the masses is the outcome of their oppression and exploitation by property-owners**. ⚠️ Three weaknesses are pressed. ✅ Classes are **not fixed and rigidly maintained blocks**; the predicted disappearance of the **middle class** did not occur; and Burnham identified a **new managerial class**. ✅ Twentieth-century capitalism claimed transformation through **the model of 'welfare state'**, with **extensive labour legislation** and **progressive... taxation**. ✅ And socialist states showed **no indications of the emergence of a classless and stateless society**, Djilas identifying in **The New Class (1957)** a **new privileged class** of administrators and party officials. ⚠️ The third is decisive because it is internal. ✅ Hence the theory **is now being revised by its champions** toward **'structures of domination' and 'forms of domination'**. 🔑 The prediction failed; the method of locating domination survived.

**Rubric [15]:** the doctrine compactly stated before criticism **3** · the chapter's concession in its own terms **3** · all three named weaknesses with their named evidence **5** · the recognition that two are reported rather than endorsed **1** · the residue of **structures of domination** **2** · a qualified verdict **1**.

## Lesson 37 — Base, superstructure and Weber's challenge

Progress: 37 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §4's spine entry on neo-Marxism and the Advanced companion §3's note that relative autonomy complicates the class account; read against the chapter's sub-heading **RECENT DEVELOPMENTS** at PDF p.265 (printed 246), the boxed definition of **Base and Superstructure** on the same page, and the Weber passage that follows.
CA search: "base and superstructure relative autonomy of the state Weber bureaucracy 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's treatment of base and superstructure. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Why the state was under-theorised

✅ **Marx and Engels regarded the mode of production as the 'base' and the 'state' as a part of the 'superstructure'.** ✅ **Accordingly the state was hardly worthy of independent analysis.**

✅ The capitalist state **was rooted in the clash between the interests of the bourgeoisie (the owners of the means of production who constituted the dominant class) and the proletariat (the propertyless workers who formed the dependent class)**; and **the state could not represent the 'common interest' as no such interest could exist in the capitalist society**, as the *Communist Manifesto* says: **The executive of the modern state is but a committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie.**

✅ Then the admission that opens the whole of neo-Marxism: **However, Marx and Engels threw little light on the mechanism by which the dominant class controls the state power.**

🔑 That gap is the subject of Lesson 38. ⚠️ Gramsci, Miliband and Poulantzas are not revisionists tinkering with a complete theory; they are supplying a mechanism the founders did not specify.

### 2. The box: Base and Superstructure

✅ The chapter's boxed definition at PDF p.265 (printed 246):

> A building-like metaphor, used in Marxist theory to explain the relation between economic structure of society and other aspects of social life. According to this model of analysis, mode of production (i.e. the economic structure of society) comprises base or foundation (also called substructure) of society. It conditions forms of state and social consciousness which belong to the superstructure, including law, politics, religion, morals, social customs and other expressions of culture. In the course of social development, changes in the mode of production give rise to corresponding changes in all parts of the superstructure.
>
> — Gauba, Ch.10, boxed definition, PDF p.265 (printed 246)

✅ And the box's own statement of the dispute: **Classical Marxism treats superstructure as wholly dependent on the base.** ✅ **Neo-Marxists have sought to redefine this relationship in different ways.** ✅ **Some writers like Antonio Gramsci (1891-1937) and Nicos Poulantzas (1936-79) have conceded relative autonomy of the superstructure in the capitalist society whereas others like Louis Althusser (1918-90) have upheld interdependence of base and superstructure.**

| ⚠️ Position | ✅ Holder | ✅ Claim |
|---|---|---|
| Full dependence | **Classical Marxism** | Superstructure **wholly dependent on the base** |
| Relative autonomy | **Gramsci (1891-1937)**, **Poulantzas (1936-79)** | The superstructure has **relative autonomy** in capitalist society |
| Interdependence | **Althusser (1918-90)** | **Interdependence of base and superstructure** |

⚠️ **Althusser** appears in this chapter only inside the box, and only for this third position. 🔑 Candidates who read only the running text will not have him at all — a direct consequence of the chapter's heavy use of boxed material.

### 3. Weber's challenge

✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920), a liberal thinker, rejected Marx's economic determinism and sought to demonstrate the autonomy of state power (*The Theory of Economic and Social Organization*; 1922).**

✅ His argument has two resources and one conclusion:

```text
  RESOURCE 1  "exclusive control over legitimate use of force in
               society which was not accessible to private interests"
  RESOURCE 2  "a strong organization in the form of BUREAUCRACY which
               could formulate, implement and monitor the policies of
               the state"
                          |
  CONCLUSION  "The state power, therefore, could not be reduced to an
               instrument of class interests."
```

⚠️ The structure of the argument is worth noticing. 🔑 Weber does not deny that classes exist or that they seek to use the state; he argues that the state possesses **powerful resources of its own** that no private interest commands, so it is a competitor rather than merely an instrument.

⚠️ Note also that Weber is introduced here as **a liberal thinker**, which places his challenge outside Marxism — unlike the three neo-Marxist responses that follow at Lesson 38, which are internal.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Base and superstructure is the core of any historical-materialism stem, and Weber is the standard external objection.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "Explain historical materialism and discuss its relevance" wants the box, the three positions and Weber in sequence.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating relative autonomy as a single neo-Marxist view. ✅ The box distinguishes Gramsci and Poulantzas from Althusser's interdependence.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Quote the **committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie** line, then immediately note that the founders **threw little light on the mechanism**; the pairing shows command of the problem rather than of the slogan.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Marx and Engels made the **mode of production** the **'base'** and the **state** part of the **'superstructure'**. (PDF p.265)
2. ✅ **Accordingly the state was hardly worthy of independent analysis.**
3. ✅ **The bourgeoisie** are **the owners of the means of production who constituted the dominant class**; **the proletariat** are **the propertyless workers who formed the dependent class**.
4. ✅ The state **could not represent the 'common interest' as no such interest could exist in the capitalist society**.
5. ✅ **The executive of the modern state is but a committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie.**
6. 🔑 **Marx and Engels threw little light on the mechanism by which the dominant class controls the state power.**
7. ✅ The base is **the economic structure of society**, also called **substructure**.
8. ✅ The superstructure includes **law, politics, religion, morals, social customs and other expressions of culture**.
9. ✅ **Changes in the mode of production give rise to corresponding changes in all parts of the superstructure.**
10. ✅ **Classical Marxism treats superstructure as wholly dependent on the base.**
11. ✅ **Gramsci (1891-1937)** and **Poulantzas (1936-79)** concede **relative autonomy of the superstructure**.
12. ✅ **Louis Althusser (1918-90)** upholds **interdependence of base and superstructure**.
13. ✅ **Max Weber (1864-1920)**, **a liberal thinker**, **rejected Marx's economic determinism** in ***The Theory of Economic and Social Organization* (1922)**.
14. ✅ Weber's resources: **exclusive control over legitimate use of force... not accessible to private interests**, and **bureaucracy which could formulate, implement and monitor the policies of the state**.
15. 🔑 **The state power, therefore, could not be reduced to an instrument of class interests.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why classical Marxism gave the state so little independent analysis, and state Weber's reason for holding that state power cannot be reduced to class interest.

**Model answer:** ✅ Because of the base–superstructure model: **Marx and Engels regarded the mode of production as the 'base' and the 'state' as a part of the 'superstructure'**, and **accordingly the state was hardly worthy of independent analysis**. ⚠️ If the superstructure is **wholly dependent on the base**, then studying the state adds nothing to studying the economy, and the *Manifesto*'s formula suffices: **the executive of the modern state is but a committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie**. ⚠️ The cost is that **Marx and Engels threw little light on the mechanism by which the dominant class controls the state power**. ✅ Weber's reason is that the state **had powerful resources of its own**: **exclusive control over legitimate use of force in society which was not accessible to private interests**, and **a strong organization in the form of bureaucracy which could formulate, implement and monitor the policies of the state**. ⚠️ Possessing capacities no private interest can match, the state can pursue purposes of its own. 🔑 An instrument has no resources; the state has two.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not attribute relative autonomy to all neo-Marxists alike. ⚠️ The chapter's box separates **Gramsci** and **Poulantzas**, who **conceded relative autonomy of the superstructure**, from **Althusser**, who **upheld interdependence of base and superstructure** — a different correction with different consequences.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain the base-superstructure model and indicate how later writers have modified it. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The model is **a building-like metaphor, used in Marxist theory to explain the relation between economic structure of society and other aspects of social life**. ✅ The **mode of production** comprises the **base or foundation (also called substructure)**, which **conditions forms of state and social consciousness** belonging to the superstructure — **law, politics, religion, morals, social customs and other expressions of culture** — so that **changes in the mode of production give rise to corresponding changes in all parts of the superstructure**. ✅ **Classical Marxism treats superstructure as wholly dependent on the base.** ✅ Modifications divide: **Gramsci** and **Poulantzas** **conceded relative autonomy of the superstructure in the capitalist society**, while **Althusser** **upheld interdependence of base and superstructure**. ✅ From outside, **Weber** rejected economic determinism, citing the state's **exclusive control over legitimate use of force** and its **bureaucracy**, so that state power **could not be reduced to an instrument of class interests**. 🔑 One metaphor, three internal repairs and one external challenge.

**Rubric [10]:** the metaphor and the content of base and superstructure **3** · the determination claim **1** · the three positions correctly assigned **3** · Weber's two resources and his conclusion **2** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 38 — Gramsci, Miliband and Poulantzas

Progress: 38 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block E — IV. Class perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's reconstructed Miliband-Poulantzas debate and the Advanced companion §7's note that neo-Marxism itself splits; read against the chapter's *Recent Developments* continuation at PDF pp.266-267 (printed 247-248), including Gramsci's two-level superstructure and the chapter's closing question.
CA search: "hegemony civil society instrumentalist structuralist theory of the state 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the neo-Marxist debate as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Gramsci: two levels of superstructure

✅ **Antonio Gramsci (1891-1937), a neo-Marxist, was first to concede relative autonomy of the state in the tradition of Marxist thought.**

✅ He **made a clear distinction between two levels of superstructure**:

| ✅ Level | ✅ What it is | ✅ What it relies on |
|---|---|---|
| **Political society** | **Represented state power** | **Force for exercising its domination** |
| **Civil society** | **Closer to the base** | **Consent for exercising its domination** |

✅ Correspondingly, **the structures of domination in the culture of the capitalist society comprised of two elements**: **the structures of coercion which belonged to the realm of political society**, and **the structures of legitimation which belonged to the realm of civil society**.

✅ **Gramsci paid special attention to the functioning of civil society which was exemplified in the institutions of family, school and church, etc.** ✅ **These institutions familiarized the individual with the rules of behaviour and inculcated a natural deference to the authority of the ruling class.**

✅ The contrast in his own vocabulary: **while political society or 'the state' exercised 'direct domination' or command through the institution of 'juridical' government, civil society exercised 'hegemony' throughout society which represented a web of beliefs and institutional as well as social relations**. ✅ **In effect civil society legitimized the rule of the bourgeoisie so that nobody would challenge its supremacy.**

✅ And the conclusion that reverses the usual picture: **capitalist society largely depends on the efficiency of these institutions for its stability**; **it is only when civil society fails to prevent dissent that political society is required to resort to coercion**.

🔑 Coercion is the *fallback*, not the foundation. ⚠️ That inversion is the single most examinable point in the Gramsci material and is developed again at Lesson 60.

⚠️ ❌ Remember the terminological hazard from Lesson 8: Gramsci's **civil society** manufactures consent; the pluralist **civil society** at Lesson 49 is **the bulwark of democratic order**. ⚠️ Never use one while citing the other.

### 2. Miliband: the instrumentalist position

✅ **Ralph Miliband (1924-94)** and **Nicos Poulantzas (1936-79)** are **other neo-Marxist thinkers who have expressed conflicting views on the relative autonomy of the state**.

✅ Miliband, in ***The State in Capitalist Society* (1969)**, **has accepted the unity of state power and class power**. ✅ **His view is described as 'instrumentalist theory of the state'.** ✅ **He adheres to classical Marxist position that the state is invariably an instrument of class power.**

✅ Three mechanisms perpetuate the class character of the capitalist state:

```text
  (1) "the middle class or upper class BACKGROUND of the state
       officials"
  (2) "the ECONOMIC POWER of the capitalist class"
  (3) "the inherent DESIRE of the politicians and bureaucrats to
       continue the existing economic system on which their own
       position depends"
```

⚠️ The three are of different kinds — sociological, structural and motivational — and an answer that labels them so shows control of the argument rather than recall of a list.

### 3. Poulantzas: the structuralist reply

✅ Poulantzas, in ***Political Power and Social Classes* (1973)**, **has drawn a clear distinction between the position of the capitalist class and the state power**. ✅ **His view is described as 'structuralist theory of the state'.** ✅ **He has conceded the relative autonomy of the state.**

✅ His claims, in order:

- ✅ **Class domination is not automatically translated into state power and the state cannot properly be regarded simply as the instrument of a class.**
- ✅ **The state enhances its legitimacy by invoking authority of 'the people'.**
- ✅ **The capitalist class enhances its legitimacy by dissociating itself from state repression.**
- ✅ **Relative autonomy of the state helps in improving its economic performance on the one hand and promoting capitalist interests on the other.**
- ✅ **Rejecting Miliband's concept of the unity of class and state power, Poulantzas treats the state itself as an arena of class struggle.**

🔑 The fourth claim is the one that makes the position genuinely Marxist rather than liberal: autonomy is *functional for* capital, not a departure from serving it. ⚠️ A state that visibly served one class would be less useful to that class than a state that visibly serves everyone.

### 4. The chapter's closing question

✅ **It may be questioned whether overemphasis on the autonomy of state power, as distinguished from the power of the dominant class, does not amount to departure from the original tenets of Marxism.**

⚠️ The chapter does not answer it. 🔑 That is the correct shape for an answer too: present Miliband's claim, Poulantzas's objection, and then the limit — the point beyond which autonomy stops being a Marxist refinement and becomes a liberal concession.

⚠️ Note also that the question is a **caution about a direction**, not a verdict on Poulantzas. ❌ Do not write that Gauba rejects Poulantzas.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This debate is where most of the marks lie in any neo-Marxist stem, and it is reconstructed fully at Lesson 59.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** "Critically examine the Marxist theory of the state" expects the internal debate, not only the founders.
- ❌ **Trap.** Treating Miliband and Poulantzas as saying the same thing. ✅ One asserts **the unity of state power and class power**; the other expressly rejects it.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Run claim → objection → limit: Miliband's three mechanisms, Poulantzas's relative autonomy, and the chapter's question about departure.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Gramsci (1891-1937)... was first to concede relative autonomy of the state in the tradition of Marxist thought.** (PDF p.266)
2. ✅ **Political society... represented state power and relied on force**; **civil society... was closer to the base and relied on consent**.
3. ✅ **Structures of coercion** belong to political society; **structures of legitimation** belong to civil society.
4. ✅ Civil society is exemplified in **family, school and church**, which **inculcated a natural deference to the authority of the ruling class**.
5. ✅ Political society exercises **'direct domination' or command through the institution of 'juridical' government**, while civil society exercises **'hegemony'**, **a web of beliefs and institutional as well as social relations**.
6. ✅ Civil society **legitimized the rule of the bourgeoisie so that nobody would challenge its supremacy**.
7. 🔑 **It is only when civil society fails to prevent dissent that political society is required to resort to coercion.**
8. ✅ **Miliband (1924-94)**, ***The State in Capitalist Society* (1969)**: **the unity of state power and class power**; the **'instrumentalist theory of the state'**.
9. ✅ Three mechanisms: officials' **middle class or upper class background**; **the economic power of the capitalist class**; politicians' and bureaucrats' **inherent desire... to continue the existing economic system**.
10. ✅ **Poulantzas (1936-79)**, ***Political Power and Social Classes* (1973)**: the **'structuralist theory of the state'**, conceding **relative autonomy**.
11. ✅ **Class domination is not automatically translated into state power.**
12. ✅ The state gains legitimacy **by invoking authority of 'the people'**; the capitalist class gains it **by dissociating itself from state repression**.
13. ✅ Relative autonomy **helps in improving its economic performance on the one hand and promoting capitalist interests on the other**.
14. ✅ Poulantzas **treats the state itself as an arena of class struggle**.
15. 🔑 The chapter's closing question: does **overemphasis on the autonomy of state power... amount to departure from the original tenets of Marxism**?

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why, on Poulantzas's account, a state that is relatively autonomous may serve capitalist interests better than a state that is directly controlled.

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Because visible service destroys the legitimacy on which compliance depends. ✅ Poulantzas holds that **class domination is not automatically translated into state power** and that **the state cannot properly be regarded simply as the instrument of a class**; instead **the state enhances its legitimacy by invoking authority of 'the people'**, which it cannot credibly do if it is seen to take instructions from owners. ✅ Symmetrically, **the capitalist class enhances its legitimacy by dissociating itself from state repression**, so neither party gains from an open identification. ✅ The payoff is stated directly: **relative autonomy of the state helps in improving its economic performance on the one hand and promoting capitalist interests on the other** — an autonomous state can discipline individual capitalists, regulate competition and concede welfare, all of which stabilise the system that no single capitalist would voluntarily fund. ⚠️ Hence the state becomes **an arena of class struggle** rather than a tool. 🔑 Distance is an asset, not a defection.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat Gramsci's **civil society** as a check on power. ⚠️ In his analysis it is where **hegemony** is produced through **family, school and church**, which **inculcated a natural deference to the authority of the ruling class**; coercion is needed only **when civil society fails to prevent dissent**. ⚠️ The pluralist sense of civil society as **the bulwark of democratic order** belongs to a different division.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine the debate between the instrumentalist and structuralist theories of the capitalist state, and state the limit the chapter places on it. *(Examine — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ The debate addresses a gap the founders left: **Marx and Engels threw little light on the mechanism by which the dominant class controls the state power**. ✅ Gramsci opens the field by distinguishing **political society**, which **relied on force**, from **civil society**, which **relied on consent**, and by locating **hegemony** in **family, school and church**, so that coercion is needed only **when civil society fails to prevent dissent**. ✅ Miliband, in ***The State in Capitalist Society* (1969)**, defends the **unity of state power and class power** through three mechanisms: officials' **middle class or upper class background**; **the economic power of the capitalist class**; and politicians' and bureaucrats' **inherent desire... to continue the existing economic system on which their own position depends**. ✅ Poulantzas, in ***Political Power and Social Classes* (1973)**, replies that **class domination is not automatically translated into state power**, that the state gains legitimacy **by invoking authority of 'the people'** while the capitalist class gains it **by dissociating itself from state repression**, and that **relative autonomy** therefore **helps in improving its economic performance on the one hand and promoting capitalist interests on the other**; the state becomes **an arena of class struggle**. ⚠️ The reply is strong where state action visibly departs from immediate capitalist preference, as with welfare and regulation. ✅ The limit is the chapter's own question: whether **overemphasis on the autonomy of state power... does not amount to departure from the original tenets of Marxism**. ⚠️ Autonomy must remain functional for capital; once it is autonomy simpliciter, the analysis has become liberal. 🔑 A bounded concession, not an open one.

**Rubric [15]:** Gramsci's two levels and the hegemony/coercion ordering **4** · Miliband's three mechanisms **3** · Poulantzas's four claims including the functional payoff **4** · the chapter's limiting question stated as a question **2** · a closing judgment on where the concession must stop **2**.

## Lesson 39 — The unencumbered self and possessive individualism

Progress: 39 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block F — V. Communitarian perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §3's communitarian definition and the Advanced companion §4's "situated self" entry; read against the restored division heading **V. COMMUNITARIAN PERSPECTIVE** at PDF p.267 (printed 248) and the chapter's opening page for the division, which introduces Macpherson's *possessive individualism*.
CA search: "communitarian critique of liberal self possessive individualism contemporary 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's statement of the communitarian target. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. What is being attacked

✅ **Communitarian perspective on the state indicates a recent mode of thought.** ✅ **It marks the departure from the philosophy of liberalism because it places the relation between individual and society in a new perspective.** ✅ **It is based on the philosophy of communitarianism which repudiates the idea of the 'self' as envisaged in the liberal theory.**

✅ The liberal self is named twice over. ✅ **Liberal theory implied an 'unencumbered self' detached from preexisting social forms, as exemplified by the concept of 'possessive individualism'.** ✅ The term **was coined by C.B. Macpherson in his notable work *The Political Theory of Possessive Individualism: Hobbes to Locke* (1962) to describe the main assumption underlying modern liberal theory**: that **individual is the sole proprietor of his own person or capacities for which he owes nothing to society**.

✅ Three consequences follow from that assumption. ✅ **Such a view denies his commitment to other individuals, traditions, practices and conceptions of the good.** ✅ **It holds that self is prior to its ends.** ✅ **It is fully competent to choose its ends as well as its roles and dispositions.**

```text
  "individual is the SOLE PROPRIETOR of his own person or capacities
   for which he OWES NOTHING to society"
                     |
     +---------------+----------------+
     |               |                |
  denies          holds that       holds it is
  COMMITMENT      "SELF IS PRIOR   "FULLY COMPETENT to choose
  to others,      TO ITS ENDS"      its ends as well as its
  traditions,                       roles and dispositions"
  practices,
  conceptions
  of the good
```

🔑 The second consequence is the technical one. ⚠️ **Self is prior to its ends** means the person can be identified independently of what they care about — which is what makes the liberal picture of free choice intelligible, and what communitarians deny.

### 2. The answer: the situated self

✅ **In contrast to this 'atomistic' view of individual, communitarianism advances the concept of 'situated self', as constituted by his social roles, practices and situations.** ✅ **In other words, communitarianism holds that an agent's identity is constituted by specific commitments to his social situation.**

| ⚠️ Question | ✅ Liberal | ✅ Communitarian |
|---|---|---|
| What is the self? | **Unencumbered**, **sole proprietor of his own person or capacities** | **Situated**, **constituted by his social roles, practices and situations** |
| What comes first? | **Self is prior to its ends** | Identity is **constituted by specific commitments** |
| What does politics protect? | **'Liberty' of individual, his interests and rights** | **His social identity** |
| What grounds authority? | Consent | **Acceptance of 'authority' because it expresses our common will or reflects our common identity, our shared values and beliefs** |
| Society is like... | An **association**, **based on impersonal and contractual relations** | A **community**, a **network of relationships... characterized by intimacy and durability** |

✅ The chapter also states the historical point communitarians concede: **liberalism had won liberty of the individual, but atomistic view of society held by liberalism led to the erosion of the sense of responsibility and the moral standards attached thereto**. ✅ **Communitarianism seeks to restore that sense of responsibility and reconstruct moral standards on that basis.**

🔑 The concession matters. ⚠️ Communitarianism is presented as a correction *after* a victory, not as an alternative to it — which is why it does not propose to withdraw individual liberty.

### 3. Two genealogies

✅ **While liberalism chiefly draws on the ideas of Adam Smith, John Locke and Jeremy Bentham, communitarianism is inspired by the ideas of Aristotle, J.J. Rousseau, G.W.F. Hegel and T.H. Green.**

✅ **While contemporary exponents of liberalism or 'libertarianism' include Isaiah Berlin, F.A. Hayek, Milton Friedman and Robert Nozick, contemporary sources of communitarianism may be found in the views of Michael Oakeshott (1901-90) and Hannah Arendt (1906-75).**

✅ **Its present-day exponents include Alasdair MacIntyre (*After Virtue*; 1981), Michael Sandel (*Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*; 1982), Charles Taylor (*Hegel*, 1975; *Sources of the Self*, 1989) and Michael Walzer (*Spheres of Justice*; 1983).**

⚠️ The first genealogy is the chapter's own cross-reference system working. 🔑 **Aristotle, Rousseau, Hegel and Green** are the four thinkers taught in divisions I, II and III as, respectively, the organic theorist, the hinge, the idealist and the positive liberal — so communitarianism is explicitly a recovery of material this chapter has already taught, not an import.

✅ MacIntyre's own contribution begins here: he **constructs an idea of the narrative self: a notion of personal identity that comes from the web of social and communal bonds**, and argues that **'individuality' owes its origin to the framework of an established community; it cannot be the product of an individual's choice**.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** This lesson supplies the precise target of communitarian criticism, which most answers state far too loosely.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on group rights against individual rights, or on the limits of liberal individualism, begin here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying communitarians reject individual liberty. ✅ The chapter says they accept that **liberalism had won liberty of the individual** and attack only its **atomistic** account of society.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Name **self is prior to its ends** as the precise proposition in dispute; it is sharper than any general talk of individualism.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Communitarianism **indicates a recent mode of thought** and **repudiates the idea of the 'self' as envisaged in the liberal theory**. (PDF p.267)
2. ✅ The liberal self is **an 'unencumbered self' detached from preexisting social forms**.
3. ✅ **'Possessive individualism'** was **coined by C.B. Macpherson** in ***The Political Theory of Possessive Individualism: Hobbes to Locke* (1962)**.
4. ✅ On that view **individual is the sole proprietor of his own person or capacities for which he owes nothing to society**.
5. ✅ It **denies his commitment to other individuals, traditions, practices and conceptions of the good**.
6. 🔑 It holds that **self is prior to its ends** and is **fully competent to choose its ends as well as its roles and dispositions**.
7. ✅ Communitarianism advances the **'situated self', as constituted by his social roles, practices and situations**.
8. ✅ **An agent's identity is constituted by specific commitments to his social situation.**
9. ✅ Liberalism insists on **'liberty' of individual, his interests and rights**; communitarianism focuses on **his social identity**.
10. ✅ It upholds **acceptance of 'authority' because it expresses our common will or reflects our common identity, our shared values and beliefs**.
11. ✅ **Liberalism had won liberty of the individual**, but atomism **led to the erosion of the sense of responsibility and the moral standards attached thereto**.
12. ✅ Liberal lineage: **Adam Smith, John Locke and Jeremy Bentham**; communitarian lineage: **Aristotle, J.J. Rousseau, G.W.F. Hegel and T.H. Green**.
13. ✅ Contemporary liberal/libertarian: **Berlin, Hayek, Friedman, Nozick**; communitarian sources: **Michael Oakeshott (1901-90)** and **Hannah Arendt (1906-75)**.
14. ✅ Exponents: **MacIntyre (*After Virtue*; 1981)**, **Sandel (*Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*; 1982)**, **Taylor (*Hegel*, 1975; *Sources of the Self*, 1989)**, **Walzer (*Spheres of Justice*; 1983)**.
15. ✅ MacIntyre's **narrative self**: **'individuality' owes its origin to the framework of an established community; it cannot be the product of an individual's choice**.

### Concept check

**Question:** State precisely what communitarians mean by the claim that the liberal self is "unencumbered", and explain why the proposition "self is prior to its ends" is the real point at issue.

**Model answer:** ✅ They mean a self **detached from preexisting social forms**, exemplified by Macpherson's **'possessive individualism'**, on which **individual is the sole proprietor of his own person or capacities for which he owes nothing to society** — a view that **denies his commitment to other individuals, traditions, practices and conceptions of the good**. ⚠️ The decisive proposition is **self is prior to its ends**, because it is what makes the rest of the liberal picture work. ⚠️ If a person can be identified independently of what they value, then values are objects of choice, choice can be protected by rights, and the state can remain neutral among competing conceptions of the good. ⚠️ If identity is instead **constituted by specific commitments to his social situation**, then stripping away roles and attachments leaves no chooser behind, so neutrality has nothing to protect and the political order must help shape the good. 🔑 The argument is about what remains when you subtract a person's attachments — everything, on one view, and nothing, on the other.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat communitarianism as a defence of tradition against liberty. ⚠️ The chapter records that **liberalism had won liberty of the individual** and presents communitarianism as restoring **the sense of responsibility and... moral standards** that atomism eroded, while also noting that, **like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government**.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** Explain the communitarian objection to the liberal conception of the self. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Liberalism, on the communitarian reading, implies an **'unencumbered self' detached from preexisting social forms**, captured in Macpherson's **'possessive individualism'**: **individual is the sole proprietor of his own person or capacities for which he owes nothing to society**. ✅ This **denies his commitment to other individuals, traditions, practices and conceptions of the good**, holds that **self is prior to its ends**, and treats the self as **fully competent to choose its ends as well as its roles and dispositions**. ✅ Communitarians answer with the **'situated self', as constituted by his social roles, practices and situations**, so that **an agent's identity is constituted by specific commitments to his social situation**. ✅ MacIntyre adds the **narrative self**, holding that **'individuality' owes its origin to the framework of an established community**. ⚠️ The objection is not to liberty but to atomism, which **led to the erosion of the sense of responsibility**. 🔑 No chooser exists prior to its commitments.

**Rubric [10]:** the unencumbered self and possessive individualism with attribution **3** · the three consequences of the liberal assumption **2** · the situated self stated in the chapter's terms **2** · MacIntyre's narrative self **1** · the clarification that liberty is not the target **1** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 40 — The situated self — MacIntyre and Sandel

Progress: 40 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block F — V. Communitarian perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's MacIntyre-Sandel-Walzer-Taylor entry and the Advanced companion §5's note that political order must begin from embedded selves; read against the chapter's communitarian division at PDF pp.268-269 (printed 249-250), covering community against association, the two definitions of common good, and Sandel's critique of Rawls.
CA search: "Sandel critique of Rawls original position embeddedness community good 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on the chapter's account of MacIntyre and Sandel. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Community against association

✅ **Broadly speaking, communitarians have attacked the liberal mode of thought on the ground that it is too focused on the importance of individual liberty, and insufficiently appreciative of the way in which human beings require a place in a well-functioning community in order to flourish.**

✅ The two key terms are then fixed. ✅ **The term 'community' stands for a form of society whose members are informed by the 'community spirit' or 'a sense of community'.** ✅ **It denotes a 'network of relationships' which are characterized by intimacy and durability.** ✅ **It may be distinguished from 'association' which is based on impersonal and contractual relations.** ✅ **Liberal theory equates society with 'association', whereas communitarian theory equates society with 'community' to determine the nature and extent of social obligation.**

✅ **Communitarians argue that an individual cannot ensure full development of his personality unless he is committed to the spirit of community toward his fellow-beings.**

🔑 The disagreement is therefore about what *kind of thing* society is, and the stakes are stated in the last clause of the chapter's sentence: **to determine the nature and extent of social obligation**. ⚠️ If society is a contract, obligations are what you agreed to; if it is a community, obligations attach to membership you did not choose.

### 2. Right and good — two disagreements, not one

✅ **Liberals believe that each person should define and seek his own 'good' within a political structure which defines and enforces what is 'right'.** ✅ **On the other hand, communitarians hold that a political structure has an important role to define what is 'right' as well as 'good' and to help the citizens to seek the good.**

✅ **Liberals define 'common good' as a sum total of the good of all individuals which is exemplified by the reconciliation of their conflicting interests.** ✅ **On the contrary, communitarians define the 'common good' as a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge.** ✅ **They believe that government should strive to create a well-functioning society which would enable all citizens to achieve a good life by participating in its functioning.** ✅ **However, like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government.**

| ⚠️ Issue | ✅ Liberal | ✅ Communitarian |
|---|---|---|
| Who defines the good? | Each person, within a structure that fixes the **right** | The political structure defines **'right' as well as 'good'** and helps citizens seek it |
| What is the common good? | **A sum total of the good of all individuals**, reached by **reconciliation of their conflicting interests** | **A uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge** |
| Form of government | Democratic | Democratic — **like liberals** |

⚠️ The last row is the chapter's own guard against a standard misreading. 🔑 Communitarianism is not authoritarian by design; what it rejects is state neutrality about the good, not popular government.

⚠️ Note also how close the communitarian **common good** is to Green's claim at Lesson 30 that **the ideal objects of all are common objects**. ⚠️ The genealogy the chapter supplied at Lesson 39 is doing visible work.

### 3. MacIntyre

✅ **Communitarians insist that each of us, as an individual, develops an identity, talents and pursuits in life only as a member of the community and by sharing in its corporate life.** ✅ **Political life should, therefore, focus on the rights of the community, not on those of individual.**

✅ **MacIntyre (*After Virtue*; 1981) ridicules the liberals' concept of individual as an 'autonomous moral agent', disconnected from the social fabric.** ✅ **He argues that individuals flourish only within an atmosphere of 'socially established cooperative human activity'.** ✅ **The state must promote and protect this activity and thereby encourage the development of human excellence.**

✅ And the prediction: **MacIntyre and other communitarians believe that if the state treats individuals as disconnected beings and lets them loose to realize their rights (as liberals seem to wish), the result would be social disintegration and moral disaster.** ✅ **Such disaster has already become visible in modern liberal states as evident in the prevalence of crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the rampant drug abuse.**

⚠️ That last sentence is a sociological claim reported by the chapter. ⚠️ It is also causally ambitious: it attributes three complex phenomena to a philosophical doctrine. 🔑 In an answer, report it as the communitarian claim and note that the chapter does not supply evidence for the causal link.

### 4. Sandel

✅ **Michael Sandel (*Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*; 1982) attacked John Rawls's *A Theory of Justice* (1971) for his conception of rational negotiators deliberating as the disconnected and disembodied individuals in the so-called 'original position' who have come together to find out the principles of justice.**

✅ **It is a typical representation of the liberal attitude which tries to understand human beings independently of all activities, desires, ideas, roles and pursuits that characterize human lives in actual society.** ✅ **Sandel observes that after subtracting all these characteristics, nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand.**

✅ **Sandel asserts that the person can only be understood in the context of his 'embeddedness' in a particular time, place and culture.** ✅ **Only with this understanding, a political theory can generate laws, institutions and practices that would be genuinely good for us and conducive to a fully just society.** ✅ **This alone will create a 'deeper commonality' which will be informed by 'shared self-understanding' as well as affection.**

🔑 Sandel's argument is a subtraction argument, and it is the cleanest statement of the whole communitarian case: remove every role, desire and pursuit, and **nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand**. ⚠️ If so, a theory of justice built on what such a remainder would choose is built on nothing.

### 5. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Community against association, and right against good, are the two axes every communitarian stem turns on.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on multicultural societies balancing group rights with individual rights draw directly on this lesson.
- ❌ **Trap.** Saying communitarians prefer authority to democracy. ✅ **Like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government.**
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use Sandel's subtraction argument; it converts a general preference for community into a specific objection.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Liberalism is **too focused on the importance of individual liberty**, and insufficiently appreciative of the need for **a place in a well-functioning community in order to flourish**. (PDF p.268)
2. ✅ **Community** is a **network of relationships... characterized by intimacy and durability**, informed by **'community spirit'**, while **association** is **based on impersonal and contractual relations**.
3. ✅ **Liberal theory equates society with 'association', whereas communitarian theory equates society with 'community' to determine the nature and extent of social obligation.**
4. ✅ Liberals: each defines his own **good** within a structure that fixes the **right**.
5. ✅ Communitarians: the structure must define **'right' as well as 'good'** and **help the citizens to seek the good**.
6. ✅ Liberal common good: **a sum total of the good of all individuals** via **reconciliation of their conflicting interests**.
7. ✅ Communitarian common good: **a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge**.
8. ✅ **Like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government.**
9. ✅ **Political life should... focus on the rights of the community, not on those of individual.**
10. ✅ MacIntyre **ridicules the liberals' concept of individual as an 'autonomous moral agent', disconnected from the social fabric**.
11. ✅ Individuals flourish only within **'socially established cooperative human activity'**, which the state **must promote and protect**.
12. ✅ Otherwise the result is **social disintegration and moral disaster** — **crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the rampant drug abuse**.
13. ✅ Sandel attacks Rawls's **'original position'** of **disconnected and disembodied individuals**.
14. 🔑 **After subtracting all these characteristics, nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand.**
15. ✅ The person is understood only through **'embeddedness' in a particular time, place and culture**, creating a **'deeper commonality'** informed by **'shared self-understanding' as well as affection**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain Sandel's objection to Rawls's original position, and say what the communitarian alternative requires a political theory to begin from.

**Model answer:** ✅ Sandel attacks the conception of **rational negotiators deliberating as the disconnected and disembodied individuals in the so-called 'original position'**, which he treats as **a typical representation of the liberal attitude which tries to understand human beings independently of all activities, desires, ideas, roles and pursuits that characterize human lives in actual society**. ⚠️ The objection is a subtraction argument: ✅ **after subtracting all these characteristics, nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand**. ⚠️ If the parties in the original position are not recognisably persons, then what they would choose cannot settle what justice requires for persons. ✅ The alternative requires theory to begin from **'embeddedness' in a particular time, place and culture**, because only then can it **generate laws, institutions and practices that would be genuinely good for us and conducive to a fully just society**, creating a **'deeper commonality'** informed by **'shared self-understanding' as well as affection**. 🔑 Begin from someone in particular, or you are theorising about nobody.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not treat the communitarian common good as a majority preference. ⚠️ The chapter defines it as **a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge** — a convergence claim, not a counting claim — which is why its difficulty is that convergence may not occur, not that minorities are outvoted.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [10]:** How do liberals and communitarians differ on the common good and on the role of the political structure? *(Compare — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Liberals hold that **each person should define and seek his own 'good' within a political structure which defines and enforces what is 'right'**, and define the **common good** as **a sum total of the good of all individuals which is exemplified by the reconciliation of their conflicting interests**. ✅ Communitarians hold that **a political structure has an important role to define what is 'right' as well as 'good' and to help the citizens to seek the good**, and define the common good as **a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge**. ⚠️ The difference rests on a prior one: **liberal theory equates society with 'association'**, impersonal and contractual, **whereas communitarian theory equates society with 'community'**, marked by **intimacy and durability**. ✅ Both nonetheless **subscribe to democratic form of government**. 🔑 Same institutions, opposite accounts of what they are for.

**Rubric [10]:** both accounts of the political structure's role **3** · both definitions of common good **3** · the community/association distinction as the underlying issue **2** · the shared commitment to democracy **1** · a closing line **1**.

## Lesson 41 — Walzer, Taylor and the communitarian difficulty

Progress: 41 / 67 | Stage: Core | Block F — V. Communitarian perspective

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: Core owner §6's Walzer and Taylor entries and the Advanced companion §7's note that communitarianism remains stronger as moral philosophy than as political philosophy; read against the chapter's communitarian division at PDF pp.269-270 (printed 250-251), covering Walzer's spheres, Taylor's rejection of atomism, and the closing difficulty paragraph.
CA search: "spheres of justice dominance of money complex equality contemporary 2025 2026"
CA found: ⚠️ nothing returned that bears on Walzer's argument as the chapter gives it. ❌ No linkage is carried into this lesson.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

---

### 1. Walzer: justice has spheres

✅ **Michael Walzer (*Spheres of Justice*; 1983) has sought to reconstruct the liberal approach to justice—as the problem of determining suitable criteria of distribution—by introducing a communitarian approach to this problem.**

✅ The core claim: **criteria of distribution should correspond to the 'spheres' in which distribution is being considered, so that, for instance, economic justice will be different from political justice**. ✅ **According to Walzer, each sphere of justice will have its own right reason (or relevant reasons) for distribution of the good that it distributes.**

✅ The illustrations are precise and worth holding exactly:

| ✅ Sphere | ✅ What must not contaminate it | ✅ Where that thing properly rules |
|---|---|---|
| **Politics, health, education** | **The domination of money** | **The sphere of commodities** |
| **Office** | **Nepotism** (beyond **a certain limited point**) | **The sphere of kinship and love** |
| **Kinship** | **Male domination** | — |

✅ **The market properly conceived as the place for the distribution of various social goods on a reasonable basis should be free for all.** ✅ **As the dominance of money (above all) is incompatible with the integrity of politics, merit, kinship, etc., so the dominance of money in all these spheres must finally disappear.**

🔑 The structure is an anti-conversion rule: a good legitimately held in one sphere must not be convertible into advantage in another. ⚠️ Money may buy commodities and must not buy office, health or votes.

⚠️ Note the third row. ✅ Walzer's list includes **the sphere of kinship should not be contaminated by male domination** — so the communitarian division contains, in one clause, a claim the feminist division at Lesson 48 will develop at length. ⚠️ That internal cross-link is worth making in an answer.

✅ Walzer's institutional vision is then listed in full: **a decentralized democratic socialism: a strong welfare state run, in part at least, by local and amateur officials; a constrained market; an open and demystified civil service; independent public schools; the sharing of hard work and free time; the protection of religious and familial life; a system of public honouring and dishonouring free from all considerations of rank and class; workers' control of companies and factories; a politics of parties, movements, meetings and public debate.**

⚠️ Ten items, and they are not decorative. 🔑 This is the only place in the chapter where a communitarian supplies a concrete institutional programme — which matters directly for the closing difficulty below.

### 2. Taylor: atomism rejected

✅ **Charles Taylor (*Philosophical Papers*; 1985), a brilliant spokesman of communitarianism, has echoed MacIntyre's attack on the liberal conception of human beings as autonomous choosers.**

✅ **He rejects 'atomistic' concept of individuals which focuses on their 'will' and sets aside the complexities of the human personality.** ✅ **Taylor asserts that if human beings want their genuine development, they must acknowledge first that they are situated in a society.**

✅ And a corroboration from outside the school: **some feminist writers and religious thinkers have also confirmed that real communities cannot be created out of an aggregate of 'freely choosing adults'.**

⚠️ The phrase **focuses on their 'will'** identifies the target precisely. 🔑 The objection is not to choice but to a theory that treats willing as the whole of personality, leaving out formation, attachment and inheritance.

### 3. The difficulty the chapter names

✅ **The difficulty with communitarianism is that in spite of its strong ethical base, it has no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour.** ✅ **In a nutshell, communitarianism represents a strong moral philosophy, but it does not provide for an equally strong political philosophy.**

⚠️ This is the chapter's own verdict, in the author's register identified at Lesson 9. 🔑 It is also a precise objection rather than a dismissal: the complaint is about the absence of a **mechanism**, not about the truth of the ethics.

⚠️ Set it against Walzer's ten-item programme and a genuine tension appears. ⚠️ Walzer does supply institutions. ⚠️ What he does not supply — and what the objection asks for — is an account of how a society that currently permits **the dominance of money** across spheres would come to adopt the anti-conversion rule. 🔑 The gap is between a specification of the destination and an account of the route.

### 4. Using this in an answer

- ⚠️ **Syllabus relevance.** Walzer supplies the most usable communitarian account of justice; the difficulty paragraph supplies the criticism every stem expects.
- ⚠️ **Probable framing.** Stems on group rights, on multicultural balance, or on whether community can replace rights route here.
- ❌ **Trap.** Stating the difficulty as "communitarianism is vague". ✅ The objection is specific: no **mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**.
- ✅ **Answer use.** Use Walzer's spheres to show that communitarianism can be institutionally concrete, then concede the mechanism problem; the pairing is more honest and scores better than either alone.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Walzer reconstructs justice as **the problem of determining suitable criteria of distribution** by a communitarian route. (PDF p.269)
2. ✅ **Criteria of distribution should correspond to the 'spheres' in which distribution is being considered.**
3. ✅ **Economic justice will be different from political justice.**
4. ✅ Each sphere has **its own right reason (or relevant reasons) for distribution of the good that it distributes**.
5. ✅ **Politics, or health, or education, should be uncontaminated by the domination of money, for money properly rules in the sphere of commodities.**
6. ✅ **The sphere of office should not (beyond a certain limited point) be contaminated by nepotism, which belongs to the sphere of kinship and love.**
7. ✅ **The sphere of kinship should not be contaminated by male domination.**
8. ✅ **The dominance of money in all these spheres must finally disappear.**
9. ✅ Walzer's programme: **a strong welfare state run, in part at least, by local and amateur officials; a constrained market; an open and demystified civil service; independent public schools; the sharing of hard work and free time; the protection of religious and familial life; a system of public honouring and dishonouring free from all considerations of rank and class; workers' control of companies and factories; a politics of parties, movements, meetings and public debate**.
10. ✅ **Charles Taylor (*Philosophical Papers*; 1985)** echoes MacIntyre's attack on **human beings as autonomous choosers**.
11. ✅ He rejects the **'atomistic' concept of individuals which focuses on their 'will'** and **sets aside the complexities of the human personality**.
12. ✅ Genuine development requires acknowledging **that they are situated in a society**.
13. ✅ **Some feminist writers and religious thinkers have also confirmed that real communities cannot be created out of an aggregate of 'freely choosing adults'.**
14. 🔑 The difficulty: **it has no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**.
15. 🔑 **Communitarianism represents a strong moral philosophy, but it does not provide for an equally strong political philosophy.**

### Concept check

**Question:** State Walzer's principle of separate spheres and explain what it forbids, then assess it against the chapter's own objection to communitarianism.

**Model answer:** ✅ The principle is that **criteria of distribution should correspond to the 'spheres' in which distribution is being considered**, each sphere having **its own right reason (or relevant reasons) for distribution of the good that it distributes**, so that **economic justice will be different from political justice**. ⚠️ What it forbids is conversion across spheres. ✅ **Politics, or health, or education, should be uncontaminated by the domination of money, for money properly rules in the sphere of commodities**; **office should not... be contaminated by nepotism, which belongs to the sphere of kinship and love**; and **the sphere of kinship should not be contaminated by male domination**. ⚠️ The chapter's objection is that communitarianism **has no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**. ⚠️ Walzer is the hardest case for that objection, since he specifies institutions in detail — **a constrained market**, **independent public schools**, **workers' control of companies and factories**. ⚠️ But a specification of the destination is not an account of the route, and nothing in the programme explains how a money-dominated society would come to adopt it. 🔑 The objection survives even its best counter-example.

**Misconception to avoid:** ❌ Do not read Walzer as abolishing the market. ⚠️ He holds that **the market properly conceived as the place for the distribution of various social goods on a reasonable basis should be free for all**, and demands only that **the dominance of money** disappear from spheres where money is not the relevant reason.

### Lesson Mains practice

**Mains question [15]:** Examine the communitarian perspective on the state, and assess the judgment that it is a strong moral philosophy but not an equally strong political philosophy. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Mains model:** ✅ Communitarianism **repudiates the idea of the 'self' as envisaged in the liberal theory** — the **'unencumbered self'** of **'possessive individualism'**, which holds that **self is prior to its ends** — and substitutes the **'situated self', as constituted by his social roles, practices and situations**. ✅ Society is accordingly a **community**, marked by **intimacy and durability**, not an **association** of **impersonal and contractual relations**, and the political structure must define **'right' as well as 'good'**, seeking a common good that is **a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge**. ✅ MacIntyre insists individuals flourish only within **'socially established cooperative human activity'**; Sandel argues that **after subtracting all these characteristics, nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand**; Taylor rejects an **'atomistic' concept of individuals which focuses on their 'will'**; Walzer requires each sphere to keep **its own right reason**, so that **politics, or health, or education** remain **uncontaminated by the domination of money**. ⚠️ The ethical case is strong and the diagnosis is accurate: atomism did erode **the sense of responsibility and the moral standards attached thereto**. ⚠️ The political case is weaker. ✅ The chapter's objection is that it **has no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**. ⚠️ Walzer partly answers it with a programme — **a constrained market**, **independent public schools**, **workers' control of companies and factories** — but supplies no account of the transition, and the convergence on a single common good is asserted rather than shown. 🔑 A persuasive account of the person, an incomplete account of power.

**Rubric [15]:** the self-critique with the technical proposition **3** · community against association and the two common goods **3** · at least three exponents with their distinctive moves **4** · the chapter's objection quoted **2** · Walzer used as the strongest counter-case and then bounded **2** · a reasoned verdict **1**.
