## Anchor 1 — 2026, `KVMS-B-PHL`, Q1(b) [10]

> ✅ "Is Plato's vision of an ideal state compatible with the rights and liberties of individual citizens ? Discuss."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Individual and State* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Discuss* a compatibility question — a judgment with both sides set out.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** whether a perfectionist account of the state can leave room for individual claims against it.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lessons 10-13** in full — the organism analogy and its two implications; the state as **a natural institution** and as **an ethical institution**; the biological school's three marks, including that an organism **exists as an end-in-itself**; the conclusion that **individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state**; and the three named weaknesses, **Subordination of Man to the State**, **Distorted View of Freedom** and **Denial of Equality**, with MacIver's totalitarianism warning and the finding that there is **no scientific evidence** of natural differences in excellence.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** Plato's own text, the theory of the forms, the tripartite soul, the guardians, or any account of the *Republic*. ➡️ All of that belongs to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here**, because the stem's primary owner is not this topic.

## Anchor 2 — 2024, `PHKM-B-PHL`, Q3(a) [20]

> ✅ "Do you agree with the view that Aristotle was more successful than Plato in steering a middle course between 'Statism' and 'individualism'? Discuss with arguments."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Individual and State* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Do you agree... discuss with arguments* — a comparative verdict requiring grounds on both sides.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** a criterion for what a middle course between statism and individualism would be, before either thinker is assessed against it.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lesson 11**'s reconstruction of Aristotle's priority-of-wholes argument — **the whole is of necessity prior to the part**, the **stone hand**, **the individual, when isolated, is not self-sufficing**, and **a beast or a god** — with the chapter's insistence that the priority is **not... a historical fact, but... a logical point**; **Lesson 12**'s account of the ethical state resting on **the differentiation of functions**, including **from the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule** and the defence of slavery; and **Lesson 13**'s three weaknesses, which supply the case that Aristotle's own position leaves no rights against the state.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** Plato's position, any comparison of the two in their own texts, and any account of Aristotle's constitutional typology, his ethics or his account of citizenship. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 3 — 2024, `PHKM-B-PHL`, Q1(b) [10]

> ✅ "Present a brief account of origin and development of Social Contract Theory."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Individual and State* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Present a brief account* — exposition, with a developmental sequence.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** origin, principal exponents and the line of development, compressed.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lesson 15** entirely — the mechanistic premise, the state as **an artificial contrivance** implying **two life-patterns**, the rudimentary traces in **Kautilya's *Arthashastra***, the **ancient Greek sophists** and **ancient Roman law**, the chapter's denial that the theory is therefore ancient, its systematic formulation **to drop a curtain on the values of the feudal system**, the three exponents with their works, dates and political purposes, the later users **Kant**, **Spencer**, **Rawls** and **Nozick**, and the admission that the theory is **based on speculation**. ✅ **Lessons 16-21** supply the development through the state of nature and the terms of the contract; **Lesson 22** supplies the appraisal.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** any treatment of contract theory outside the European tradition, of Rawls's own contractarianism, or of consent theory in contemporary political philosophy. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 4 — 2021, `HXS-B-PHLY`, Q4(c) [15]

> ✅ "Evaluate whether the social contract theory adequately addresses the different issues of human rights."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Individual and State* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Evaluate* an adequacy claim — a verdict with criteria stated.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** what contract theory can and cannot establish about rights.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lesson 17**'s account of Lockean natural rights to **his life, liberty and estate**, their partial surrender and the retention of **life, liberty and property**, with W.T. Jones's reading that natural law states **how they ought to behave**; **Lesson 19**'s Hobbesian counter-case, in which natural rights are **nothing more than the natural powers of men, used to oppress others** and are surrendered **once and for all**; **Lesson 20**'s trust, consent and right of revolution; and **Lesson 22**'s objections — Burke's view that rights **are the product of a developed social consciousness and are enforced by institutionalized law**, Paine's view that no contract is **eternally binding on all generations**, and the chapter's own point that **dominant sections** become **self-styled representatives of the will of society**.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** any account of human rights as a doctrine, their classification, instruments or justification outside contract theory. ➡️ That belongs to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 5 — 2026, `KVMS-B-PHL`, Q3(c) [15]

> ✅ "Negative liberty seems to embody the idea of ultimate personal freedom, whereas positive liberty enables individuals to achieve their full potential. Discuss."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Social and Political Ideals* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Discuss* a stated contrast — the relation between the two conceptions, not merely their definitions.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** whether the two are rivals, or whether one supplies the content of the other.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lesson 23**'s laissez-faire freedoms of **trade, contract, bargain and enterprise** and the state as **a 'necessary evil'**; **Lesson 25**'s libertarian revival and its charge that the welfare state is **inimical to individual liberty**; **Lesson 26**'s evidence of what the free market produced; **Lesson 30**'s definitions — negative freedom as **acting according to one's own choice or sweet will**, positive freedom as **acting according to reason, achieving self-realization or self-perfection** — with Barker's argument that liberty **is no negative absence of restraint, any more than beauty is the absence of ugliness**; and **Lesson 53**'s six-way comparison.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** Berlin's two concepts as a text, the literature on freedom as non-domination, or any formal analysis of freedom. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 6 — 2025, `SLPM-B-PHL`, Q2(b) [15]

> ✅ "Evaluate Marxism as a Political Ideology."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Political Ideologies* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Evaluate* — a judgment on merits and defects together.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** what Marxism claims, what it achieved and where it failed.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** the boxed definition of **Marxism** at **Lesson 33**, with its four-part remedy; **Lessons 34-35**'s derivation of the state from private property and the four canonical texts of **1848**, **1880**, **1884** and **1917**; **Lesson 36**'s concession that its chief contribution lies in **discovering and demonstrating the role of economic forces in shaping history**, together with its three named weaknesses and the revision toward **structures of domination**; and **Lessons 37-38**'s base-superstructure model, Weber's challenge and the Miliband-Poulantzas debate.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** alienation, the labour theory of value, dialectical method, or Marxism's account of religion, art or science. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file and to **Topic 04**.

## Anchor 7 — 2023, `SKYC-B-PHLY`, Q3(a) [20]

> ✅ "Explain Historical Materialism and discuss its relevance in the context of social development and change."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Political Ideologies* file.
- ⚠️ **Directive:** *Explain... and discuss* — exposition followed by an assessment of continuing relevance.
- ⚠️ **Conceptual demand:** the model itself, and whether it still explains change.
- ✅ **What this chapter supplies:** **Lesson 34**'s historical sequence from **primitive communism** through **surplus production** to class division, with **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord; the steam-mill gives you society with the industrial capitalist** and **the structure of society is always determined by the prevalent form of production**; **Lesson 37**'s boxed definition of **Base and Superstructure**, the three positions on their relation — classical dependence, **relative autonomy** in Gramsci and Poulantzas, and **interdependence** in Althusser — and Weber's objection that state power **could not be reduced to an instrument of class interests**; and **Lesson 36**'s record of what the model predicted and what occurred.
- ❌ **What this chapter does not supply:** the stages of history as a general theory, the forces/relations distinction in detail, or any philosophy of history. ➡️ Those belong to the owner file.
- ❌ **No answer approach is offered here.**

## Anchor 9 — 2019, `SDF-B-PHLY`, Q1(c) [10]

> ✅ "Evaluate Mahatma Gandhi as a political anarchist."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Political Ideologies* file.

## Anchor 10 — 2026, `KVMS-B-PHL`, Q2(b) [15]

> ✅ "Does providing land and property rights to women constitute a necessary condition or a sufficient condition or both for ensuring social equality among the genders ? Discuss."

- ✅ **Ledger's primary owner:** the Philosophy *Gender Discrimination* file.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

⚠️ Eight checks. ⚠️ Each requires material from at least three lessons, which is the skill an examiner tests whenever a stem spans the chapter's divisions. ⚠️ They are ordered so that the first three consolidate the chapter's structure, the next three its arguments, and the last two the chapter as a whole.

---

## Check A — one chapter, nine divisions, four silent

**Prompt:** Reconstruct the chapter's structure from the restored headings, say which divisions are introduced without any visible heading, and explain what a text-only reading loses.

**Worked response:** ✅ Nine numbered divisions: **I. ORGANIC THEORY OF THE STATE** (PDF 220), **II. LIBERAL-INDIVIDUALIST PERSPECTIVE** (225), **III. WELFARE STATE PERSPECTIVE** (244), **IV. CLASS PERSPECTIVE** (260), **V. COMMUNITARIAN PERSPECTIVE** (267), **VI. POST-COLONIAL PERSPECTIVE** (270), **VII. GANDHIAN PERSPECTIVE** (273), **VIII. FEMINIST PERSPECTIVE** (277), **IX. PLURALIST PERSPECTIVE** (278). ⚠️ All nine are image-only. ⚠️ Divisions **V**, **VI**, **VIII** and **IX** additionally have no text-layer sub-heading of any kind and begin mid-page with an ordinary sentence. ⚠️ A text-only reading therefore sees a run of capitalised sub-headings — *THE STATE AS A NATURAL INSTITUTION*, *THEORY OF THE SOCIAL CONTRACT*, *TERMS OF THE CONTRACT*, *THEORY OF LAISSEZ-FAIRE INDIVIDUALISM*, *TRANSITION TO POSITIVE LIBERALISM*, *EXPONENTS OF POSITIVE LIBERALISM*, *NATURE OF THE STATE*, *CONCEPT OF SWARAJ* — with **A CRITICAL ESTIMATE** occurring twice and **RECENT DEVELOPMENTS** twice, and no indication of which perspective any of them belongs to. 🔑 The loss is the chapter's entire top-level structure, and with it the fact that the welfare state is division **III** rather than a sub-head of **II**.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not infer that the four silent divisions are less important. ⚠️ Three of them — communitarian, feminist and pluralist — supply the chapter's most contemporary material, and the ninth supplies its closing sentence.

---

## Check B — one method, six divisions

**Prompt:** The chapter uses a single critical method repeatedly. Name it, and show it operating in at least four divisions.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ The method is to test an ideal description against the institution's actual personnel, inputs or outcomes. ✅ **Organic (L13):** the theory **visualizes the attributes of an 'ideal' state**, but in practice the state is **a set of men** with **no mechanism** guaranteeing their **wisdom, sincerity and responsibility**. ✅ **Contract (L22):** the theory makes the state **an instrument of harmonizing the interests of all individuals and all sections of society**, but **dominant sections or the chosen few** become **self-styled representatives of the will of society**. ✅ **Welfare (L31):** Green's framework is sound, but **he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system**, blaming **landed property** and thereby exempting capitalism. ✅ **Feminist (L48):** the liberal state **represents itself as fully autonomous**, and **such a state is not bothered to ascertain the actual share of power enjoyed by women**. ✅ **Pluralist (L49):** the model **implies that all groups would bargain on equal footing, which is not the case in actual practice**, conceded by Lindblom in **1977** and Dahl in **1982**. 🔑 Five divisions, one argument: the claim survives as a standard and fails as a report.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat this as scepticism about ideals. ⚠️ In every case the chapter retains the ideal as the measure, which is why Dahl's response was **redistribution of power in society** rather than abandonment of impartiality.

---

## Check C — six boxes, six functions

**Prompt:** The chapter carries six boxed definitions. Name them with their pages, and state what each contributes that the running text does not.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Mercantilism** (PDF 239) supplies the system laissez-faire was argued against — a state that **upheld the state's intervention in economy for the protection of indigenous industries** and **recommended lowering of wages**. ✅ **Marxism** (261) supplies the only statement of the four-part remedy: **overthrowing capitalism**, **social ownership and control**, **enforcing universal labour**, **full development of the forces of production**. ✅ **Base and Superstructure** (265) supplies the metaphor's content and the three positions on it, including **Althusser**, who appears nowhere else in the chapter. ✅ **Colonial Powers** (270) names **Britain, France, Belgium, Spain, Portugal** and the period. ✅ **Neo-colonial Powers** (270) names six instruments, including **transfer of pollution**. ✅ **Moral Individualism** (275) supplies the three clauses on which Gandhi's rejection of compulsory labour rests. 🔑 Two of the six — Althusser and the four-part remedy — exist only inside boxes, so a reader who skips boxes loses them entirely.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not treat boxes as optional glossary material. ⚠️ The Gandhi-Marx comparison at Lesson 46 is unintelligible without the **Moral Individualism** box, because the principle that **no human being can accept an obligation except at his own free will** is what forbids **enforcing universal labour**.

---

## Check D — four thinkers who appear in more than one division

**Prompt:** Identify thinkers the chapter uses across divisions, and say what each cross-appearance shows.

**Worked response:** ✅ **Rousseau** appears in divisions II (contract theorist and then idealist), I-II (MacIver's lineage from **'forcing men to be free'**), III (Green's idealist source) and V (communitarian inspiration), and shows that the chapter is one argument rather than nine. ✅ **MacIver** appears in I (the totalitarianism warning), III (the service state) and IX (an originator of pluralism), and shows that the same author can supply a criticism, a doctrine and a tradition. ✅ **Macpherson** appears in III (Bentham's dilemma and the criticism of Green) and V (coining **'possessive individualism'**), and shows that the communitarian target was named by a critic of liberalism from within the welfare debate. ✅ **Laski** appears in II (joining the liberal stream **without subscribing to the mechanistic concept**), III (the welfare architect) and IX (an originator of pluralism), and shows that the chapter's divisions are analytical rather than biographical. 🔑 No thinker belongs to only one division merely because the chapter prints him there.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not assign a thinker to a division as though it were a school membership. ⚠️ Green is an exponent of positive liberalism and an ancestor of communitarianism at the same time, and the chapter says both.

---

## Check E — three unresolved collisions

**Prompt:** Identify three places where the chapter's own material conflicts and no resolution is offered, and state what each would require to settle.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **One.** MacIver's warning that failing to separate society from the state is **the dangerous road to totalitarianism** against the feminist demand that **the sphere of purely personal relations between man and woman will also have to be regulated**. ⚠️ Settling it requires a criterion for when state reach into the private sphere protects rather than absorbs — Barker's **compulsory enforcement** test being the best available candidate, though the chapter does not apply it there. ⚠️ **Two.** The welfare state's provision of capacity through **health, education, housing, social insurance** against Gandhi's claim that **swaraj government will be a sorry affair if people look up to it for the regulation of every detail of life**. ⚠️ Settling it requires deciding whether capacity supplied by another counts as freedom. ⚠️ **Three.** The chapter's finding that **there is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people** against its own assertion that **women by nature are nature-friendly** and **peace-loving by nature**. ⚠️ Settling it requires evidence the chapter does not supply, and the safe course is to rebuild the feminist conclusion from Eisenstein instead. 🔑 Three collisions, three different kinds of deficit: criterion, concept, evidence.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present an unresolved collision as a chapter error without saying which kind of deficit it is. ⚠️ A missing criterion, a conceptual disagreement and an unsupported empirical claim call for three different responses in an answer.

---

## Check F — the chapter's own verdicts

**Prompt:** Distinguish criticisms the chapter reports from judgments it delivers, and list at least four of the latter.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ Reported criticism uses distancing constructions: **it is alleged that** in the socialist-states objection; **the supporters of capitalism have pointed out** in the welfare-capitalism objection; **some liberal researchers have, however, shown** on the middle class; **James Burnham... has attempted to demonstrate** on the managerial class. ⚠️ Authorial judgment is unmarked and direct. ✅ **One:** **there is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people**, and **most differences... are the product of the socio-economic system**. ✅ **Two:** of Green, **he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system**. ✅ **Three:** of liberal theory, its **tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**, and **the needs of social justice demand a thorough transformation of the economic system as well**. ✅ **Four:** of communitarianism, **it has no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**. 🔑 Write "the chapter records the criticism that..." for the first kind and "Gauba holds that..." only for the second.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not attribute MacIver's lineage from Rousseau through **Fichte and Hegel** to **Fascists and Nazis** to the chapter. ⚠️ It is quoted, and the chapter's own framing blames those who **invoked** the reasoning.

---

## Check G — the whole chapter in one causal chain

**Prompt:** Trace a single line of development from the organic theory to the chapter's final sentence, naming the dissatisfaction that produces each step.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Organic** treats the state as natural and ethical, and leaves no rights against it; dissatisfaction with individuals as **so many related atoms** had earlier produced the mechanistic turn, and dissatisfaction with subordination produces the liberal one. ⚠️ **Contract** makes the state an **artificial contrivance** resting on consent, but is **bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy** and hides the fact that **dominant sections** speak for society. ⚠️ **Laissez-faire** supplies the economic case for non-interference, but the free market produces **child labour, slum housing** and outcomes that are **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number**. ⚠️ **Welfare liberalism** answers that by making liberty **a positive power** and property **social** in basis, but its **tragedy** is its use to justify capitalism. ⚠️ **Marxism** supplies the structural account, but predicts a polarisation that did not occur and a classlessness that socialist states did not produce, so it revises toward **structures of domination**. ⚠️ **Communitarianism** answers liberal atomism but has **no mechanism**. ⚠️ **Post-colonial**, **Gandhian** and **feminist** analyses add inheritance, coercion and gender as further structures of domination. ⚠️ **Pluralism** closes by dispersing power, then concedes that **business and property enjoyed a privileged position**, so that **the descriptive and normative sides... come very close to each other**. 🔑 Each step is a response to a specific failure, which is why the chapter is cumulative without being progressive.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not present the chain as progress toward pluralism. ⚠️ The chapter records the revival of organic thinking, of laissez-faire and of Aristotelian community, so the chain loops rather than terminates.

---

## Check H — the topic in one question

**Prompt:** You are given a stem that names no perspective: "Critically examine the role of the state in securing justice." Decide what the stem is really asking and construct the answer's spine.

**Worked response:** ⚠️ **Step one.** The stem turns on **purpose** and **inequality**, not on origin. ⚠️ **Step two.** Put two perspectives in genuine conflict: the welfare-liberal account, on which the state removes **the obstructions that hamper human capacity** and secures welfare **particularly of their vulnerable sections**, against the class account, on which it is **a device for the suppression of class conflict**. ⚠️ **Step three.** Use one internal debate as the depth marker: Miliband's **unity of state power and class power** against Poulantzas's **relative autonomy**, since the question of whether the state can secure justice against propertied interests is exactly what that debate is about. ⚠️ **Step four.** State what the chapter settles: liberal theory's **great merit** is constitutional government and its **tragedy** is its use to justify capitalism, so that **the needs of social justice demand a thorough transformation of the economic system as well**. ⚠️ **Step five.** State what it leaves open: whether autonomy sufficient for justice is compatible with the class account, which the chapter poses as a question. ⚠️ **Step six.** Grade the verdict: the state can secure procedural and welfare justice, is structurally constrained in securing distributive justice, and the constraint is a matter of degree that the chapter does not quantify. 🔑 Six steps, no listing, one judgment.

**Trap:** ❌ Do not answer a perspective-neutral stem by surveying all nine. ⚠️ Select by the dimension the stem names, and say in one clause which perspectives you are setting aside and why.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ Six questions, distinct from every lesson-local question in this file. ⚠️ Stated word ceilings are this session's, not the examination's: **[10] about 150 words**, **[15] about 250 words**, **[20] about 300 words**. ❌ No printed word limit is asserted for any 15- or 20-mark part from any paper.

---

## Question 1 [10 marks]

**Question:** "The organic theory explains belonging but cannot explain disobedience." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The proposition is accurate on both limbs. ✅ Belonging is explained well: a part **has no existence apart from its position within that organism**, so **the existence and worth of individuals depend on the existence of the state**, and Aristotle's argument that **the individual, when isolated, is not self-sufficing** makes political community a condition of agency rather than a convenience. ✅ Disobedience cannot be explained, because the same premises entail that **the interests of individuals could not be distinguished from the interest of the state**, that **individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state**, and that **true freedom of the individual lies in obedience to the laws of the state**. ⚠️ The chapter adds the practical cost: **the individual is not allowed to take a stand at the instance of his own conscience**, though officials **may be as imperfect as their subjects**. 🔑 A theory of membership with no theory of refusal.

**Rubric [10]:** the belonging argument reconstructed **3** · the three-step route to no-rights-against **3** · the conscience consequence **2** · the officials objection **1** · a closing judgment **1**.

---

## Question 2 [10 marks]

**Question:** Why does Gauba treat the welfare state as a separate perspective rather than as a phase of liberalism? *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Three reasons, two textual and one substantive. ✅ First, the chapter's opening enumeration lists **(b) Liberal-individualist perspective** and **(c) Welfare State perspective** as separate items, and the restored heading **III. WELFARE STATE PERSPECTIVE** ranks it as a numbered division equal to the others. ✅ Second, the division opens with the sub-heading *TRANSITION TO POSITIVE LIBERALISM*, so the transition passage begins the welfare division rather than closing the liberal one — placing the emphasis on failure rather than on self-repair. ✅ Third, the substance differs: negative liberalism produced **large inequalities**, **labour... treated as a commodity**, **child labour, slum housing**, and outcomes that were **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number**, and positive liberalism **pleaded for positive role of the state**. ⚠️ The continuity is real — both are liberal — but the perspective is defined by a reversal. 🔑 Separate because it begins where its predecessor fails.

**Rubric [10]:** the enumeration and the restored heading as textual grounds **3** · the placement of the transition passage **2** · the substantive reversal with at least two illustrations **3** · the acknowledged continuity **1** · a closing line **1**.

---

## Question 3 [15 marks]

**Question:** "Every perspective in this chapter locates domination somewhere." Examine this claim, and assess whether the perspectives can be combined. *(Examine and assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The claim holds for seven of the nine and fails for two, and the pattern is informative. ✅ The **class** perspective locates domination in ownership: the state is **an instrument of oppression and exploitation**, protecting a class that first acquires **the means of production and thereafter political power**. ✅ **Gramsci** relocates part of it outside the state, in **family, school and church**, which **inculcated a natural deference to the authority of the ruling class**. ✅ The **post-colonial** perspective locates it in inheritance — borders drawn **for their administrative convenience**, and a **bureaucracy, particularly the police** that was **used to oppression of the people**. ✅ The **feminist** perspective locates it in gender, reaching **the most intimate relations**, with the liberal state **not bothered to ascertain the actual share of power enjoyed by women**. ✅ The **Gandhian** perspective locates it in coercion as such: the state is **a soulless machine**. ✅ The **communitarian** perspective locates a subtler domination — the erosion of **the sense of responsibility** by atomism — and Walzer names conversion across spheres, including **male domination** in kinship. ✅ Even **pluralism** ends by locating it: **business and property enjoyed a privileged position**. ⚠️ The exceptions are the **organic** theory, which calls the same relation natural hierarchy and justifies it, and the **liberal-individualist** perspective, which treats market outcomes as non-dominating because voluntarily produced. ⚠️ Combination is possible precisely because most of these name *different* structures, and the chapter's own residue licenses it: look for **'structures of domination' and 'forms of domination'**. ⚠️ What cannot be combined are the two exceptions with the seven, since they deny that the relation is domination at all. 🔑 Seven lenses on one object, and two refusals to look.

**Rubric [15]:** at least five perspectives with the domination each locates **6** · the two exceptions correctly identified with their reasons **3** · the **structures of domination** residue used as the warrant for combination **3** · a stated limit on combinability **2** · a closing judgment **1**.

---

## Question 4 [15 marks]

**Question:** Assess whether the chapter's treatment of the feminist and Gandhian perspectives is weaker than its treatment of the others, and say what a candidate should do about it. *(Assess — answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ It is weaker in a specific and demonstrable respect. ⚠️ The chapter's standard method is exposition followed by a critical section and a residue: the organic and class divisions carry **A CRITICAL ESTIMATE** with three named weaknesses each; the contract carries **A CRITICAL APPRAISAL**; the welfare, communitarian, post-colonial and pluralist divisions carry unheaded critical passages, including the verdicts that liberal theory's **tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system** and that communitarianism offers **no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted**. ❌ The **Gandhian** and **feminist** divisions carry no critical section at all. ⚠️ The feminist division is additionally weakened from inside: its closing argument rests on the claim that **women by nature are nature-friendly** and are **peace-loving by nature**, which is asserted without support and conflicts with the chapter's own finding that **there is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people**. ⚠️ A candidate should do three things. ⚠️ First, say that the chapter supplies no criticism, because knowing what a source omits is examinable. ⚠️ Second, construct criticism from the chapter's own materials — for Gandhi, the objection that trusteeship depends on a **'change of heart'** with no mechanism, which is the chapter's own objection to communitarianism; for feminism, the question of which instrument can deliver the remedy when the state is itself identified as a site of embedded power. ⚠️ Third, rebuild the feminist conclusion from Eisenstein's institutional argument rather than from temperament. 🔑 Name the gap, borrow the tools, and label the borrowing.

**Rubric [15]:** the chapter's standard method stated with examples **4** · the identification of the two uncriticised divisions **3** · the internal weakness of the feminist closing argument **3** · three specific remedial moves **4** · a closing judgment **1**.

---

## Question 5 [20 marks]

**Question:** "The history of theories of the state is a history of relocating where power really lies." Examine this proposition across the chapter's nine perspectives, and deliver a reasoned conclusion. *(Examine — answer in about 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ The proposition organises the chapter better than chronology does. ✅ The **organic** theory locates power in the whole, to which the part is subordinate, and treats its distribution as natural, since **from the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule**. ✅ **Contract** theory relocates it in the consent of individuals, making the state **the product of the will of society** and, in Locke, placing government **under the control of society** as a **trust**. ✅ **Laissez-faire** relocates it again, into the market, so that **the businessman knows his own interests far better than any government can tell him** and the state becomes **a necessary evil**. ✅ **Welfare liberalism** relocates it into social conditions: **the basis of property is social**, rent is **the effect of natural monopoly**, and freedom is **a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying**. ✅ **Marxism** relocates it into ownership, with political power **invented** by those who already hold economic power. ✅ **Gramsci** relocates part of it out of the state entirely, into **family, school and church**, where **hegemony** is produced. ✅ **Communitarianism** relocates the formative power into shared practice, since **'individuality' owes its origin to the framework of an established community**. ✅ **Post-colonial** analysis relocates it historically and externally, into colonial inheritance and **neo-colonial** instruments. ✅ **Gandhi** relocates it into character, so that real swaraj is **the acquisition of the capacity by all to resist authority when it is abused**. ✅ **Feminism** relocates it into gender and into the intimate sphere. ✅ **Pluralism** relocates it into associations and then concedes that **business and property enjoyed a privileged position**. ⚠️ The proposition therefore holds, with one qualification: the relocations are not successive discoveries but competing claims that coexist, which is why **the old and new principles of political theory exist simultaneously**. ⚠️ The conclusion is methodological: a theory of the state is a hypothesis about where to look for power, and should be selected by the question asked rather than by date. 🔑 Nine addresses for one force.

**Rubric [20]:** at least eight perspectives with the relocation each performs **8** · chapter wording used for at least five **4** · the qualification that relocations coexist rather than succeed **3** · the coexistence claim grounded in the chapter's own statement **2** · a methodological conclusion about selection **3**.

---

## Question 6 [20 marks]

**Question:** "A theory of the state should be judged by what it can explain and by what it must leave out." Apply this test to three perspectives of your choice and deliver a graded verdict. *(Apply and assess — answer in about 300 words.)*

**Model answer:** ⚠️ Take the liberal-individualist, class and feminist perspectives, since they answer different questions and so test the criterion fairly. ✅ **Liberal-individualist.** It explains obligation and limit: the state is **an artificial device or instrument invented by men for their mutual benefit**, government is a **trust** that may be replaced when it **falters from its duty**, and property is defended as the **fruit of labour**. ⚠️ It must leave out structure. ✅ The chapter's own objections are that **dominant sections or the chosen few** become **self-styled representatives of the will of society**, that the theory is **bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy**, and that its **tragedy is that it is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**. ✅ **Class.** It explains why reform of political institutions leaves economic domination intact, since political power is **invented** by those who already hold economic power, and it supplies the chapter's acknowledged contribution — **the role of economic forces in shaping history**. ⚠️ It must leave out institutional autonomy and non-class axes of power. ✅ Weber shows the state holds **exclusive control over legitimate use of force** and a **bureaucracy**, Burnham identifies a **managerial class**, and Djilas identifies **a new privileged class** under socialism. ✅ **Feminist.** It explains what both others omit: domination reaching **the most intimate relations**, and a state that **is not bothered to ascertain the actual share of power enjoyed by women**. ⚠️ It must leave out the choice of instrument, since the state it indicts is also the state it petitions, and in this chapter it leaves out criticism of itself altogether. ⚠️ **Verdict, graded.** The criterion works, but it does not rank the three, because what each leaves out is what another supplies. ⚠️ It ranks them only relative to a question: for obligation, the first; for structure, the second; for the reach of domination, the third. 🔑 Judge a lens by its field of view and its blind spot together.

**Rubric [20]:** three perspectives, each with a genuine explanatory strength **6** · each with a genuine and named omission **6** · chapter evidence for at least four of the six claims **4** · a verdict that is graded and relativised to a question **3** · a closing formulation of the criterion **1**.

---

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Five sets, mapped to the lesson blocks. ⚠️ Each entry states a predictable error, the correction and the lesson where the material is taught.

---

## Set 1 — the chapter's frame and the organic theory (Lessons 1-13)

| ⚠️ Predictable error | ✅ Correction | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|---|
| "Political thought is the study of government." | ✅ It is **thought about the state, its structure, its nature, and its purpose** — four heads, not one. | L1 |
| "Later theories replaced earlier ones." | ✅ **The old and new principles of political theory exist simultaneously, claiming their rightful place.** | L1, L3 |
| "The chapter covers eight perspectives." | ✅ Nine, lettered **(a)** to **(i)**, with all nine headings image-only. | L2 |
| "The welfare state is part of liberalism in the chapter's scheme." | ✅ It is division **III**, separately numbered and headed. | L2 |
| "Organic theory means patriotism." | ✅ It is two structural claims: dependence of worth, and natural superiority of some groups. | L10 |
| "'State is prior to man' is a historical claim." | ✅ **Not intended to describe a historical fact, but to make a logical point.** | L11 |
| "Aristotle's ethical state is benign perfectionism." | ✅ **The ethical foundations... rest on the differentiation of functions**, and slavery is defended as natural. | L12 |
| "Organic theory is the same as fascism." | ✅ The chapter's claim is narrower: it leaves **no safeguards for his liberty**, and the inter-war regimes show the result when absolute obedience is enforced. | L13 |
| "Gauba says Rousseau caused fascism." | ❌ That is MacIver, quoted; the chapter blames those who **invoked** the logic. | L13 |
| "The organic theory's hierarchy claim is merely dated." | ✅ The chapter rebuts it: **there is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people**. | L13 |

---

## Set 2 — the liberal-individualist perspective (Lessons 14-25)

| ⚠️ Predictable error | ✅ Correction | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|---|
| "Liberalism begins with Locke's politics." | ✅ The chapter begins it with seventeenth-century physical science and the idea that **nature itself was a machine**. | L14 |
| "Contract theory is ancient because Kautilya mentions it." | ✅ **All these references should not be taken to mean that the theory... has been prevalent from the ancient times.** | L15 |
| "Locke wrote the *Two Treatises* to celebrate 1688." | ⚠️ 🔶 Most of the text was composed **c.1679-1683**; say the work **came to be read** as the Revolution's defence. | L15, L17, L20 |
| "Hobbes thinks people are wicked." | ✅ They are moved **by their appetites, desires and passions** in a condition with no umpire, and are nearly equal in power. | L16 |
| "Locke's men surrender nothing." | ✅ They surrender the right **to be judges in case of breaches of law**. | L17 |
| "'Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains' is Rousseau's conclusion." | ✅ It is his opening; he **immediately proceeds to 'ignore this question'** and justifies the change. | L18 |
| "Hobbes's sovereign rules by force." | ✅ **Government does not rest on sheer force; it rests on the will of the people.** | L19 |
| "Locke's right of revolution is unconditional." | ✅ It arises only **if in any case the government falters from its duty**. | L20 |
| "The general will is the majority view." | ✅ It is **not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor, but an expression of the highest in every man**. | L21 |
| "The Mayflower Pact proves the social contract." | ✅ Its signatories were **already familiar with government**; **illustration is no evidence**. | L15, L22 |
| "Adam Smith wanted no state economic role." | ✅ His third duty is **public works... which could not be undertaken by an individual** because profit **would never repay the expenditure**. | L23 |
| "Bentham rejected equality." | ✅ **Each to count as one, and no one for more than one** — equality as an aggregation rule derived from utility. | L24 |
| "Libertarianism is the same as Spencer's laissez-faire." | ✅ It argues from Lockean **indefeasible** rights, not from **survival of the fittest**. | L25 |
| "Nozick admits no limits on entitlement." | ✅ **If there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it.** | L25 |

---

## Set 3 — the welfare state and the class perspective (Lessons 26-38)

| ⚠️ Predictable error | ✅ Correction | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|---|
| "Socialism produced the welfare state." | ✅ The transformation occurs inside liberalism, which failed its own test — **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists**. | L26 |
| "Utilitarianism has no argument for equality." | ✅ Diminishing utility plus equal counting entails that **the aggregate utility of the whole society would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth**. | L27 |
| "Bentham's escape from that conclusion was principled." | ✅ It was empirical — **the criterion of productivity**, including **a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation**. | L27 |
| "Mill's self-regarding sphere is absolute." | ✅ The state may act where conduct is **very injurious to individual himself** — the unsafe bridge. | L28 |
| "Barker agrees with Mill's distinction." | ✅ He rejects it: **we cannot separate two different compartments of individual conduct**. | L28 |
| "Mill became a socialist." | ✅ He **showed socialist leanings** in the economic sphere while remaining a constitutionalist in the political one. | L28, L29 |
| "Taxing property violates liberal principles." | ✅ Mill and Hobhouse tax what labour did not produce; **the basis of property is social**. | L29 |
| "Green makes the state the source of rights." | ✅ **State recognizes and maintains rights but it is not the source of rights.** | L30 |
| "Green's 'negative function' means a minimal state." | ✅ **He is by no means a champion of negative liberalism**; removing obstacles **is a positive function**. | L31 |
| "Laski thought the state was a class instrument." | ✅ He held it **rhetorical exaggeration to regard it as a class-instrument**. | L32 |
| "MacIver's service state is a welfare doctrine." | ✅ It is a theory of authority: it **commands only because it serves; it owns only because it owes**. | L32 |
| "Marxism is a version of the mechanistic theory." | ✅ It denies that the state is **a manifestation of the will of the people** or **an instrument of reconciliation**. | L33 |
| "Marxism denies that man is a social being." | ✅ **Man is by nature a social animal, but not a 'political animal'.** | L34 |
| "The revolution abolishes the state." | ✅ It is **retained**; withering requires three stated conditions. | L35 |
| "The collapse of communism refutes the class analysis." | ✅ It refutes a prediction; the analysis is **revised by its champions** toward **structures of domination**. | L36 |
| "All neo-Marxists accept relative autonomy." | ✅ **Althusser** upholds **interdependence of base and superstructure** instead. | L37 |
| "Miliband and Poulantzas say the same thing." | ✅ Poulantzas expressly rejects **Miliband's concept of the unity of class and state power**. | L38 |

---

## Set 4 — communitarian, post-colonial, Gandhian, feminist and pluralist (Lessons 39-49)

| ⚠️ Predictable error | ✅ Correction | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|---|
| "Communitarians reject individual liberty." | ✅ They concede that **liberalism had won liberty of the individual** and attack atomism. | L39 |
| "Communitarians prefer authority to democracy." | ✅ **Like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government.** | L40 |
| "Walzer wants to abolish the market." | ✅ He holds the market **should be free for all** and objects only to **the dominance of money** in other spheres. | L41 |
| "Communitarianism is vague." | ✅ The objection is precise: **no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**. | L41 |
| "The post-colonial perspective is a theory of the state." | ✅ It **does not embody any new theory of the state**. | L42 |
| "Colonial institutions were purely exploitative, or a net gift." | ✅ Both: **deliberately designed to facilitate and stabilize their rule**, and productive of **awareness and consciousness**. | L43 |
| "Nation-building is symbol-making." | ✅ Without tackling **mass poverty, illiteracy, unemployment, ill-health, housing**, **nothing would prove effective**. | L44 |
| "Gandhi condemned the state on economic grounds." | ✅ **On moral ground, and not on historical or economic grounds.** | L45 |
| "Enlightened anarchy is Gandhi's programme." | ✅ **The ideal is never fully realized in life**; the working principle is Thoreau's. | L45 |
| "Trusteeship is gradual socialisation." | ✅ It requires the **'change of heart' of capitalists**, making the owner the agent of change. | L46 |
| "Bread labour is compulsory labour." | ✅ **No human being can accept an obligation except at his own free will.** | L46 |
| "Swaraj means national independence." | ✅ It includes **cultural and moral independence** and independence of one's **national** government. | L47 |
| "Millett and Eisenstein make the same argument." | ✅ Millett redefines politics through culture and intimacy; Eisenstein attacks the state's self-description as **fully autonomous**. | L48 |
| "Women are naturally peace-loving, so exclusion causes militarism." | ❌ Asserted without support and against the chapter's own denial of natural differences; rebuild from Eisenstein. | L48, L63 |
| "Gramsci's civil society and the pluralists' are the same." | ✅ One produces **hegemony**; the other is **the bulwark of democratic order**. | L8, L38, L49 |
| "Polyarchy is the pluralist conclusion." | ✅ Lindblom (**1977**) and Dahl (**1982**) conceded the central descriptive claim. | L49, L64 |

---

## Set 5 — synthesis, comparison and answer method (Lessons 50-67)

| ⚠️ Predictable error | ✅ Correction | ✅ Taught at |
|---|---|---|
| "The six tests are the chapter's own framework." | 🔶 They are the owners' apparatus; mark them as such. | L6, L50 |
| "Every perspective answers every test." | ⚠️ Empty cells are findings: the post-colonial perspective contests no origin; communitarianism has no mechanism of change. | L50 |
| "Only the organic theory treats the state as datable." | ❌ The reverse: only the organic theory treats it as **not** datable. | L51 |
| "Marxist and feminist accounts of purpose are prescriptions." | ✅ They are descriptions of what the institution serves; their prescriptions are separate. | L52 |
| "There are two conceptions of liberty." | ✅ At least six, applying six different tests. | L53 |
| "Green's functional inequality is the same as Nozick's entitlement." | ✅ One is justified by social yield, the other by pedigree. | L54 |
| "The private sphere is simply a safeguard." | ⚠️ MacIver's warning and the feminist demand collide, and the chapter resolves neither. | L55 |
| "The three clusters are a taxonomy." | ⚠️ They cannot place Rousseau and miscategorise communitarianism. | L56 |
| "Gandhi's and Marx's routes differ only in method." | ✅ Three of Marx's four remedial parts fail Gandhi's premise of **moral individualism**. | L57 |
| "Rousseau is really a liberal, or really a totalitarian." | ✅ **Liberals and idealists adore him alike; they also condemn him alike.** | L58 |
| "Poulantzas denies the state serves capital." | ✅ Autonomy **helps... promoting capitalist interests on the other**. | L59 |
| "Hegemony is propaganda." | ✅ It is habituation through **family, school and church**; coercion is the fallback. | L60 |
| "Showing bias refutes constitutional government." | ✅ The **great merit** of liberal theory remains **evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government**. | L61 |
| "The social self refutes liberalism." | ✅ Green and MacIver concede it and remain liberals. | L62 |
| "Parliamentary share measures women's share of state power." | ❌ The chapter's claim concerns **different branches of the state apparatus and... state policies**. | L63 |
| "Dahl and Lindblom abandoned pluralism." | ✅ Dahl **recommended those policies which would promote redistribution of power in society**. | L64 |
| "Fill the post-colonial silences from constitutional sources." | ➡️ Route them; the perspective works **on the experience of post-colonial societies themselves** at a conceptual level. | L65 |
| "Manufacture a citation to authenticate an unfamiliar quotation." | ❌ Never; use the three safe registers. | L66 |
| "Conclude that all perspectives are partly right." | ❌ The chapter delivers six specific verdicts and names its open questions. | L67 |

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

⚠️ Seven maps. ⚠️ Each does work no table in the lessons does: they are cross-divisional, and together they make the chapter reconstructible from a single page.

---

## Map 1 — the nine divisions as one rail

```text
  "CONCEPT OF THE STATE comprises the CORE of political thought"
  no final verdict | provisional solution | debate must go on
        |
  I. ORGANIC (220-225)  state NATURAL + ETHICAL; organs have no
     separate interests => NO RIGHTS AGAINST THE STATE; freedom =
     OBEDIENCE
     critical estimate: subordination | distorted freedom | denial of
     equality -- "no scientific evidence" of natural differences
        |
        v  [individuals are not atoms... but nor are they organs]
  II. LIBERAL-INDIVIDUALIST (225-244)  state ARTIFICIAL
     (a) CONTRACT: state of nature -> terms -> sovereignty
         Hobbes ONE contract, absolute | Locke STAGES, trust,
         revolution | Rousseau ONE contract, POPULAR, general will
         appraisal: "bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy";
         function: feudal -> capitalist
     (b) LAISSEZ-FAIRE: reason -> four freedoms -> property ->
         "necessary evil"
         Smith (3 duties) | Bentham (calculus, 6+1) | James Mill |
         Spencer (survival of the fittest)
         revival: LIBERTARIANISM -- Hayek, Berlin, Friedman, Nozick
        |
        v  [the free market produced child labour and slum housing]
  III. WELFARE STATE (244-260)  POSITIVE role of the state
     Bentham's dilemma: diminishing utility argues EQUALITY;
     productivity overrides it -- "fear of starvation"
     Mill: QUALITY of pleasures; land and rent taxable
     Hobhouse: "the basis of property is SOCIAL"
     Green: positive freedom; rights from MORAL CONSCIOUSNESS;
       state REMOVES OBSTACLES; blames LANDED property (Macpherson:
       "exempted capitalism")
     Laski: breakdown of capitalism; DEMOCRATIZATION of economic power
     MacIver: SERVICE STATE -- "commands only because it serves"
     VERDICT: merit = constitutional government; TRAGEDY = justifying
       capitalism; "a thorough transformation of the economic system"
        |
        v  [transformation of the economic system = the next division]
  IV. CLASS (260-267)  private property -> classes -> POLITICAL POWER
     INVENTED; state = "device for the SUPPRESSION of class conflict";
     coercive + IDEOLOGICAL power
     1848 | 1880 | 1884 | 1917; revolution -> dictatorship ->
     WITHERING AWAY (3 conditions)
     critical estimate: no rigid classes | capitalism with a human
     face | no classless society (Djilas 1957)
     => revise toward "STRUCTURES OF DOMINATION"
     recent: base/superstructure; WEBER (force + bureaucracy);
     GRAMSCI (political vs civil society, HEGEMONY);
     MILIBAND (instrumentalist, 3 mechanisms) vs POULANTZAS
     (structuralist, RELATIVE AUTONOMY, "arena of class struggle")
        |
        v  [structures of domination -- which structures?]
  V. COMMUNITARIAN (267-270)  against the UNENCUMBERED SELF and
     "possessive individualism"; the SITUATED SELF; COMMUNITY not
     ASSOCIATION; structure must define RIGHT and GOOD
     MacIntyre narrative self | Sandel subtraction argument | Taylor
     anti-atomism | Walzer SPHERES
     difficulty: "no mechanism"; strong moral, weak political
     philosophy
        |
  VI. POST-COLONIAL (270-273)  NOT a new theory; pattern of
     domination; civilising claim; MACAULAY 1835; DOUBLE EFFECT;
     'grant' of independence; NATION-BUILDING + STATE-BUILDING;
     inherited police; professional elites
        |
  VII. GANDHIAN (273-277)  coercion; "SOULLESS MACHINE"; MORAL ground
     only; ENLIGHTENED ANARCHY with its own limit; Tolstoy vs
     Proudhon/Bakunin/Kropotkin; GANDHI vs MARX (7 differences);
     MORAL INDIVIDUALISM; SWARAJ -- political, cultural, moral;
     "capacity by all to resist authority when it is abused"
        |
  VIII. FEMINIST (277-278)  (a) regulate the PUBLIC SPHERE and the
     personal: MILLETT, "the personal is political";
     (b) state as an INSTRUMENT OF POWER: EISENSTEIN, "fully
     autonomous"; socialist-feminist charges; post-colonial and
     socialist variants; naturalistic implications (unsupported)
        |
  IX. PLURALIST (278-279)  MULTIPLE centres; impartial arbitrator;
     freedom of association -> CIVIL SOCIETY as "the bulwark of
     democratic order"; Solidarity 1980
     origin: protest against SOVEREIGNTY -- DUGUIT, Laski, MacIver
     contemporary: Dahl & Lindblom -> POLYARCHY; Polsby "nobody
     really dominates"
     TWO CONFESSIONS: Lindblom 1977 (privileged position of business);
     Dahl 1982 (not "truly equal political forces") -> redistribution
        |
  "the DESCRIPTIVE and NORMATIVE sides... come very close to each other"
```

```text

        |
```

## Map 4 — where power is located, division by division

| ✅ Division | ✅ Power is located in | ⚠️ What that makes invisible |
|---|---|---|
| Organic | The whole, naturally distributed | Coercion, and the officials who exercise it |
| Contract | Consent of individuals | Who actually speaks for "society" |
| Laissez-faire | The market and the individual's reason | Structural dependence and destitution |
| Welfare | Social conditions and enabling capacity | Deeper economic domination, per the division's own verdict |
| Class | Ownership of the means of production | Institutional autonomy; non-class axes |
| Gramscian | Civil society, through hegemony | The possibility of genuine consent |
| Communitarian | Shared practice that forms the self | Power inside the community |
| Post-colonial | Colonial inheritance and external dependence | Mechanism; the inherited elite's interests |
| Gandhian | Character, and the capacity to resist | Scale, and the institutional route |
| Feminist | Gender, public and intimate | The choice of instrument |
| Pluralist | Associations, dispersed | Privilege — until conceded in 1977 and 1982 |
