# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ Compressed recall notes covering every subtopic in the order taught. ❌ They do not repeat the teaching prose; they carry definitions, formulations, sequences, dates, criticisms and traps.

## The chapter's frame, method and structure

- ✅ **Concept of the state comprises the core of political thought**; political thought is **thought about the state, its structure, its nature, and its purpose**.
- ✅ **When new ideas appeared, old ideas were criticized or modified**; in political philosophy **it is not necessary that old ideas be dead before the new ideas become acceptable**.
- ✅ Unlike natural science, **the old and new principles of political theory exist simultaneously, claiming their rightful place**; **none of the current political ideas can claim absolute authority or validity**; **merits and demerits need constant examination**.
- ✅ Purpose: **to bring out complexity of the problem, and not to pronounce any final verdict**; theory supplies only a **provisional solution or a working formula in the light of our understanding of a given situation**; **debate must go on as and when a new genius enters the fray**.
- ✅ Nine perspectives, lettered **(a)** organic, **(b)** liberal-individualist, **(c)** welfare state, **(d)** class, **(e)** communitarian, **(f)** post-colonial, **(g)** Gandhian, **(h)** feminist, **(i)** pluralist.
- ⚠️ All nine division headings are image-only; divisions V, VI, VIII and IX also lack any text-layer sub-heading.
- ⚠️ Recurring shape: exposition → critical section → residue; absent in the Gandhian and feminist divisions.
- ⚠️ Reported criticism uses **it is alleged that**, **the supporters of capitalism have pointed out**; authorial judgment is unmarked.

## Organic theory of the state (I)

- ✅ The state is **an organism or a living body**, individuals its **organs**.
- ✅ Implication 1: **the existence and worth of individuals depend on the existence of the state**.
- ✅ Implication 2: groups are **naturally fit to perform different functions**; some are **destined to enjoy a superior position**.
- ✅ Natural institution: no **existence of man as man, that is as a civilized being, without the existence of the state**; the state **comes into existence for the sake of life, and continues for the sake of good life**.
- ✅ Aristotle: **man by nature is a 'political animal'**; otherwise **either a beast or a god**; **'State is prior to man'** — **a logical point**, not **a historical fact**.
- ✅ *Politics* I: **the whole is of necessity prior to the part**; a **stone hand**; **the individual, when isolated, is not self-sufficing**.
- ✅ Challenge: the **mechanistic theory** of the seventeenth century; revival through dissatisfaction with individuals as **so many related atoms**.
- ✅ **Burke (1729-97)**, **chief exponent of conservatism**: historical growth; the state **could not survive its dissection**.
- ✅ **Hegel (1770-1831)**: **'State is march of God on earth.'**
- ✅ Biological school: **tissues of the State**; **systems of nutrition and circulation**; **Bluntschli (1801-81)** — state masculine, Church feminine.
- ✅ Ethical institution: **a moralizing effect**; moral excellence through **performing his duties and enjoying his rights as a citizen**.
- ✅ Its foundation is **the differentiation of functions**: **from the hour of birth some men are marked out for subjection and others for rule**; slavery as **a natural institution** securing good life **both for the masters and the slaves**.
- ✅ Three marks of an organism: intrinsic part-whole relation (wheel against hand); **organic unity** and growth from within, so it **cannot be altered by substituting new parts for old**; and it **exists as an end-in-itself**.
- ✅ Honorifics: **'Moral Organism'**, **'Super-organism'**, **'Organism of Organisms'**, **'Real Person'**, **'Super Person'**.
- ✅ Conclusion: **individuals could have any rights within the state but they could never have any rights against the state**; **true freedom of the individual lies in obedience to the laws of the state**.
- ✅ Critical estimate — concession: revival is **ample proof of its strength**; it **helps man achieve the highest development of his potential**.
- ✅ Weakness 1 **Subordination of Man to the State**: personality **submerged**; man as **a conduit pipe**; conscience disabled; officials **as imperfect as their subjects**; absolute authority **bound to destroy the state itself**; **the Nazi and Fascist regimes in Germany and Italy**.
- ✅ Weakness 2 **Distorted View of Freedom**: liberty reduced to **obedience to the commands of the state**; MacIver, *The Web of Government* (1965) — **the dangerous road to totalitarianism**; the lineage through **'forcing men to be free'**, **Fichte and Hegel**, to **Fascists and Nazis**.
- ✅ Weakness 3 **Denial of Equality**: **the toes cannot dictate to the brain**; **wisdom and authority become the monopoly of the chosen few**; **a clever device for preserving the inequality of status and opportunity**; used to justify **slavery, racial supremacy, imperialism**.
- 🔑 **There is no scientific evidence of any fundamental differences in the excellence of different categories of people**; **most differences... are the product of the socio-economic system**.

## Liberal-individualist perspective (II)

- ✅ Based on the **mechanistic view**: nature **was a machine, governed by universal laws**, so society is a mechanism; interference is **detrimental to its smooth functioning**; non-interference **highly suited the interests and aptitudes of the new middle class**; **the liberal theory represented the social and economic philosophy of this class**.
- ✅ Strengthened from two sides: **social contract** and **laissez-faire individualism**.
- ✅ Contract: the state is **the product of the mutual agreement of men, created with a definite purpose**; **an artificial contrivance**; **two life-patterns**; an expression of **'common will'**.
- ✅ **Green** and **Laski** **joined the liberal stream without subscribing to the mechanistic concept**.
- ✅ Traces in **Kautilya's *Arthashastra***, **Greek sophists**, **Roman law** — but **systematically formulated** to replace **the values of the feudal system** with **the new values of the capitalist system**.
- ✅ **Hobbes (1588-1679)**, *Leviathan* (**1651**), tutor to Charles II, condemned **the Civil War of 1642**; **Locke (1632-1704)**, *Two Treatises of Civil Government* (**1690**), justified **the Glorious Revolution of 1688**, sought monarchy **'in the consent of the people'**; **Rousseau (1712-78)**, **the source of inspiration of the great French Revolution (1789)**.
- ✅ Later users: **Kant (1724-1804)**, **Spencer (1820-1903)**, **Rawls (1921-2002)**, **Nozick (1938-2002)**.
- ✅ The theory is **based on speculation**; **illustration is no evidence**.
- ✅ State of nature: life **without the authority of civil law, state or political control**; **no industry**; **natural rights** but **no recognized rights**.
- ✅ Hobbes: selfish man; **appetites, desires and passions**; powers **nearly alike**; **perpetual struggle**; **every man is enemy to every man**; **'solitary, poor, nasty, brutish and short'**; **'Might is right'**; **perfect anarchy**; natural rights as **natural powers... used to oppress others**; natural law's four rules — peace, surrender in concert, **keep their contracts**, **return beneficence for beneficence**.
- ✅ Locke: **'peace, goodwill, mutual assistance and preservation'**; **liberty, not... licence**; **the law of inward morality**; defect is **inconvenient** adjudication — **if men become judges of their own cases, justice would not be secured**; rights to **'his life, liberty and estate'**; W.T. Jones (1971) — natural law states **how they ought to behave**.
- ✅ Rousseau: **'noble savage'**, *Discourse on Inequality* (**1755**); **equal, self-sufficient and contented**; **private property comes into existence, with the consequent division of labour**; **the state is thus an evil**; **seems to anticipate Marx as regards the origin of the state**; *The Social Contract* (**1762**) — **'Man is born free, but he is everywhere in chains'**; **agreements are the basis for all legitimate authority among men**; lost **natural liberty and unlimited right**, gained **civil liberty and the property of what he possesses**.
- ✅ Terms — Hobbes: **a single contract**; society and state together; **the sovereign is not a party**; **I authorize and give up my right of governing myself to this man**; **'keep them in awe'**; surrender **once and for all**; no **right to revolt or revolution**; **absolute, indivisible and inalienable**; **unlimited political obligation**; flawless only with **a perfect and infallible person or assembly**.
- ✅ Terms — Locke: Waldron's **three stages** (unanimous community, majority institutions, taxation by consent); society before government; **if the government is dissolved, society does not disintegrate**; government as a **'trust'**; **no scope for absolutism**; surrender only the right **to judge of and punish the offenders of natural law**; the recipient as **'umpire'**; **consent of the people**; **the people have the right to overthrow that government**; the **'bloodless' or 'Glorious' revolution of 1688**; property as **'fruit of labour'**; taxation beyond necessity requires **the owner's consent**.
- ✅ Terms — Rousseau: single contract, **popular sovereignty**; **what they lose in their individual capacity, they get back in their corporate capacity, in improved form**; **under the supreme direction of the general will**; MacIver — **the substitution, for a sovereign, of the sovereign**, **one and indivisible, inerrant, indestructible, omnipotent**; **the precarious liberty of the state of nature is well lost**; law as **the very form of its realization**; **each, coalescing with all, may nevertheless obey only himself**; **'man can be forced to be free'**; Riley — **'the general will is always right'**, **'the will that one has as a citizen'**; actual will **transient, unstable and inconsistent** against real will **stable, constant, consistent and determinate**; the general will **not a 'compromise' or the lowest common factor, but an expression of the highest in every man**, **the spirit of citizenship in its concrete shape**.
- 🔑 Rousseau **starts with the mechanistic view of the state but ends with the organic view**; **starts as an ardent liberal but ends as an ardent idealist**; **liberals and idealists adore him alike; they also condemn him alike**.
- ✅ Critical appraisal: **'bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy'**; **Hume (1711-76)** — **historically improbable and philosophically unnecessary to explain allegiance**; Scruton — **tacit consent is inapplicable**; the **Mayflower Pact of 1620** fails; **Maine** — **from one of 'status' to one of 'contract'**; contract is **an individualist idea**, unknown to primitive man governed by **custom** in **family, clan, phratry, tribe, gen**; a contract **requires the force or sanction of the state**; **Burke** — rights **are the product of a developed social consciousness**; **Paine (1737-1809)** — **a dead weight on the wheel of progress**, **Every age and generation must be as free to act for itself**; **dominant sections... become self-styled representatives of the will of society**; **the relations of the feudal society are determined by tradition; those of the capitalist society are determined by contract**.
- ✅ Laissez-faire: **reason**; **freedom of trade, freedom of contract, freedom to bargain and freedom of enterprise**; **private property as the condition of progress**; profit motive **'functional'**; **laissez-faire** = **'leave alone'**; the state a **'necessary evil'**.
- ✅ **Adam Smith (1723-90)**, *Wealth of Nations* (**1776**): **Physiocrats**, deriving **ultimately from Rousseau**, held **the goodness and bounty of nature**, and wanted **criminals, mad men, and monopolists** eliminated; Smith rejected agriculture as **the sole source of the wealth**; **economic man**; **led by an invisible hand to promote an end which was no part of his intention**; **Smith never gave a blanket endorsement to the idea of beneficence of self-interest**; **natural liberty** — every man **left perfectly free to pursue his own interest in his own way**; three duties — **defence**, **exact administration of justice**, and **public works** whose profit **would never repay the expenditure**.
- ✅ **Mercantilism** box: national power from prosperity; **maximizing exports and minimizing imports**; **cheap raw materials** in, **expensive finished goods** out; **state's intervention... for the protection of indigenous industries**; **lowering of wages**.
- ✅ **Bentham (1748-1832)**: Utilitarianism revives **the classical hedonism of Epicurus**; only **absolute expediency**; institutions **judged by their fruits**; **'the greatest happiness of the greatest number'**; **two sovereign masters, pain and pleasure**; utility as a **tendency to cause pleasure and to avert pain**; **quantitative** only — **pushpin is as good as poetry**; calculus — **intensity, duration, certainty, proximity or propinquity, fecundity, purity, extent**, with the first six private and **extent** public; no **super-person, called a social organism**; **the business of government is to promote the happiness of society by a system of punishments and rewards**; **each to count as one, and no one for more than one**; **the state cannot improve character of the people**.
- ✅ **James Mill (1773-1836)**: **'philosophical radicalism'**; *Essays on Government* (**1825**, as printed); **universal suffrage, secret ballot and periodic elections**; favoured **the middle rank**.
- ✅ **Spencer (1820-1903)**: *Social Statics* (**1850**, as printed), *The Man versus the State* (**1884**), *The Principles of Ethics* (**1892-93**); universal evolution to **the blessedness of final anarchy**; **a 'joint-stock protection-company for mutual assurance'**; no **public health**, no **poor relief**; **survival of the fittest**; negative liberty stretched to **elimination of the weak**.
- ✅ Libertarian revival: **neo-liberalism, neo-classical liberalism, or libertarianism**; welfare found **inimical to individual liberty**; **liberation** from **religion, family and customs of social conformity**; **man as maker of his destiny**; Lockean **indefeasible** rights; taxation as **the forced transfer of fruit of one man's labour to another**; extreme libertarians use **private protective agencies**, moderates uphold a **'nightwatchman state'**; **Hayek (1899-1992)**, **Berlin (1909-97)**, **Friedman (1912-2006)**, **Nozick (1938-2002)**; *Anarchy, State and Utopia* (**1974**) — **the dominant protective agency** becomes **a 'state-like entity'**; **entitled** not deserving; **no... rectified at the level of distribution**; **if there is only one source of water in a desert, nobody should be allowed to monopolize it**.

## Welfare state perspective (III)

- ✅ **Liberal theory which stood for negative liberalism in its early phase, was transformed into positive liberalism in its later phase.**
- ✅ The free market produced **large inequalities**, **hire and fire**, **labour... treated as a commodity**, **child labour, slum housing, and free sale of poisoned meat, bad gin**; outcomes were **not the greatest happiness of the greatest number held so dear by the classical economists**.
- ✅ Pressure: a growing working class whose **voice could not be suppressed any longer**; socialists **incessantly pressing**; liberals forced to see that their **insistence on freedom and human rights had created conditions of oppression**; otherwise **the whole edifice would tumble down**.
- ✅ Bentham's dilemma (Macpherson, *Democratic Theory—Essays in Retrieval*, **1973**): **each individual's satisfaction was to count as one** plus **diminishing utility** entails that **aggregate utility... would be greatest if everyone had equal amounts of wealth**; escape via **the criterion of productivity** — **security for unequal property**, and **a large labour force whose incentive was fear of starvation**.
- ✅ **J.S. Mill (1806-73)**: began in laissez-faire, became **the chief exponent of positive liberalism**; politically constitutional, economically **socialist leanings**; Joad (**1924**) — **it is the business of Government actively to promote the happiness of individuals**; pleasures differ in **quality**; Macpherson — substitute **the maximum development and use of human capacities—moral, intellectual, aesthetic, as well as material productive capacities**.
- ✅ Liberty of thought on two grounds: social utility through **free discussion and debate**, extended even to **'cranks'**; and dignity — **individual self-determination is a basic human right** (Kramnick and Watkins, **1979**).
- ✅ Conduct: **'self-regarding'** and **'other-regarding'** actions; the unsafe bridge; Barker (**1951**) — **we cannot separate two different compartments of individual conduct; but we can separate the sphere of Society from that of the State**; **free cooperation** to society, **compulsory enforcement** to the state; a shift **from an individualistic to a pluralistic base**.
- ✅ Property: *Principles of Political Economy* attacked socialism in **1848** and revised in **1866**; **the right to property was not absolute or sacrosanct**; land not sacrosanct **because no man made the land**; **rent was the effect of natural monopoly**, a **fit subject for taxation**; the increase belongs to **the working class who were the real producers of wealth from the land**.
- ✅ **Hobhouse (1864-1929)**, *Liberalism* (**1911**): **the basis of property is social**; **society... is an indispensable partner in its original creation**; **the true function of taxation is to secure to society the element in wealth that is of social origin**; **no case of robbing Peter to pay Paul**.
- ✅ **T.H. Green (1836-82)**: revises liberalism under idealist influence **derived from the teachings of Rousseau, Kant and Hegel**; **moral freedom** as the distinctive quality of man; negative freedom as **one's own choice or sweet will**, positive freedom as **acting according to reason, achieving self-realization or self-perfection**; Barker — liberty **is no negative absence of restraint, any more than beauty is the absence of ugliness**, it is **a positive power of doing or enjoying something worth doing or enjoying**.
- ✅ Rights emanate **from the moral character of man himself**; **the ideal objects of all are common objects**; **there can be no unrecognized rights**; **the recognizing authority... is not the state, but the moral consciousness of the community**, signifying **an eternal consciousness**.
- ✅ **Human consciousness postulates liberty; liberty involves rights; rights demand the state**; the state is **an instrument of perfection... not an embodiment of perfection**; it **must obey** the community's moral consciousness; its function is **removal of the obstructions that hamper human capacity**; **no positive moral function of making its members better**; yet **he is by no means a champion of negative liberalism**, and the state **is entitled to make education compulsory**.
- ✅ Property: defends **property in capital** and **inequality of property** as **'functional' from the point of view of the social good**; dilemma where some take **an unduly large share**; blames **landed property**, which alone is limited — **one man cannot acquire more land without others having less**; Macpherson — he **exempted capitalism from responsibility for the condition of the bulk of the people**; Gauba — **he made a great mistake in locating the ills of the capitalist system**.
- ✅ **Laski (1893-1950)**: formed by **1914-18**, **1917**, **1920**, **1919-20**, **1929**; sought **the socialist goal through the mechanism of liberal democracy**; *State in Theory and Practice* (**1935**) — a **'breakdown' of capitalism**, **two million unemployed**, **South Wales and the North East Coast**; *Reflections on the Revolution of Our Time* (**1943**) — **a caste of economic directors... hold even the states to ransom**; welfare since **1919** as **the outcome of a profounder social conscience**; **it is rhetorical exaggeration to regard it as a class-instrument**; *A Grammar of Politics* (**1938**) — **the keystone of the social arch**; **perfectibility**; the obedience test and **absence of bias**; **heavy taxation upon income**, **still heavier duties upon their estates at death**; **democratization of economic power**.
- ✅ **MacIver (1882-1970)**: rejects the contract; differs from Green by making **all rights, ethical as well as political, depend on social recognition**; **the state is only one of such associations**; **law itself exists above the state**; the government **has power as the guardian of the constitution, as the executor of law, not in its own right**; **religion and morality in its proper sense** are outside its sphere; it regulates **employer and employee, trader and consumer** but not **religious, artistic and cultural activities**; **it commands only because it serves; it owns only because it owes**; **the servant is not greater than his master**; **democracy as the form of the state proper**; its **peculiar function** is **giving a form of unity to the whole system of social relationships** without **omnicompetence**.
- ✅ **Burnham (1907-87)** on a **managerial class**; **C. Wright Mills (1916-62)** on **power elites**.
- 🔑 Verdict: the **great merit** is **evolving institutions and procedures for a constitutional government**; the **tragedy** is that it **is often invoked in order to justify the capitalist system**; **the needs of social justice demand a thorough transformation of the economic system as well**.

## Class perspective (IV)

- ✅ Denies that the state is **natural**, **ethical**, **a manifestation of the will of the people**, or **an instrument of reconciliation of conflicting interests**; accepts that it is **an artificial device**.
- ✅ **Marxism** box: founded by **Marx (1818-83)** and **Engels (1820-95)** **to lay scientific foundations of socialism**; history as **conflict between antagonistic forces and classes**; means of social production are **land, buildings, mines, forests, machinery and capital**; remedy in four parts — **overthrowing capitalism**, **social ownership and control**, **enforcing universal labour**, **full development of the forces of production**.
- ✅ **Private property** divides society; the dominant class **invents a new form of power—political power**; the state is **essentially subservient to economic power**, **imposed on society from above**, **a product of the conscious effort of the dominant class**.
- ✅ Society is **a natural institution**, the state **an artificial device**; **man is by nature a social animal, but not a 'political animal'**.
- ✅ **Primitive communism**: **meagre and communally owned** means; no private property; then **surplus production** **cornered by a class**; **the hand-mill gives you society with the feudal lord; the steam-mill gives you society with the industrial capitalist**.
- ✅ Exploitation: **an instrument of oppression and exploitation, an embodiment of injustice**; **a device for the suppression of class conflict**; **coercive power** plus **'ideological power' to create an illusion of 'consent'**.
- ✅ **1848** *Communist Manifesto* — **merely the organized power of one class for oppressing another**; **1880** Engels — **an organization of the particular class which was pro-tempore the exploiting class**; **1884** Engels — slave-owners, nobility, **the modern representative state... an instrument of exploitation of wage labour by capital**; **1917** Lenin — **the creation of 'order', which legalizes and perpetuates this oppression by moderating the conflict between the classes**.
- ✅ Route: **proletariat** expropriates **bourgeoisie**; the state is **retained** as the **'dictatorship of the proletariat'**; withering requires that **all remnants of capitalism are cleared**, **work is made compulsory for everyone**, and **the forces of production are fully developed**; then **'cooperation'**, **'equality'** and **'justice'**.
- ✅ Critical estimate — contribution: **the role of economic forces in shaping history**; attack on the complacency that conditions **were of their own making**; **the poverty of the masses is the outcome of their oppression and exploitation by property-owners**.
- ✅ Weakness 1 **No Rigid Class Division in Society**: **social mobility**; the **middle class** grew instead of disappearing; **Burnham**, *The Managerial Revolution* (**1941**).
- ✅ Weakness 2 **Capitalism with a Human Face**: **extensive labour legislation, a progressive system of taxation and elaborate provision of common social services**.
- ✅ Weakness 3 **No Signs of Classless Society in the Socialist Countries**: **much more repressive** apparatus; **a pyramid of power**; **Djilas (1911-95)**, *The New Class* (**1957**) — **a new privileged class**; the theory termed **'Utopian'**; communism **wound up** in the USSR and Eastern Europe.
- 🔑 Residue: look for **'structures of domination' and 'forms of domination'**.
- ✅ **Base and Superstructure** box: **mode of production** as **base or foundation (also called substructure)**; superstructure includes **law, politics, religion, morals, social customs and other expressions of culture**; classical Marxism makes it **wholly dependent**; **Gramsci** and **Poulantzas** concede **relative autonomy**; **Althusser (1918-90)** upholds **interdependence**.
- ✅ **The executive of the modern state is but a committee for managing the common affairs of the whole bourgeoisie**; but **Marx and Engels threw little light on the mechanism**.
- ✅ **Weber (1864-1920)**, **a liberal thinker**, *The Theory of Economic and Social Organization* (**1922**): **exclusive control over legitimate use of force** plus **bureaucracy** means **state power... could not be reduced to an instrument of class interests**.
- ✅ **Gramsci (1891-1937)**: **political society** relies on **force**, **civil society** on **consent**; **structures of coercion** against **structures of legitimation**; **family, school and church** **inculcated a natural deference to the authority of the ruling class**; **'direct domination'** against **'hegemony'**, **a web of beliefs and institutional as well as social relations**; **it is only when civil society fails to prevent dissent that political society is required to resort to coercion**.
- ✅ **Miliband (1924-94)**, *The State in Capitalist Society* (**1969**): **instrumentalist**; three mechanisms — officials' **middle class or upper class background**, **the economic power of the capitalist class**, and their **inherent desire... to continue the existing economic system**.
- ✅ **Poulantzas (1936-79)**, *Political Power and Social Classes* (**1973**): **structuralist**; **class domination is not automatically translated into state power**; legitimacy **by invoking authority of 'the people'** and **by dissociating itself from state repression**; autonomy **helps in improving its economic performance on the one hand and promoting capitalist interests on the other**; the state as **an arena of class struggle**.
- 🔑 Open question: does **overemphasis on the autonomy of state power... amount to departure from the original tenets of Marxism**?

## Communitarian perspective (V)

- ✅ **A recent mode of thought**; repudiates the liberal **'self'**; the liberal self is **unencumbered**, **detached from preexisting social forms**.
- ✅ **'Possessive individualism'** coined by **Macpherson**, *The Political Theory of Possessive Individualism: Hobbes to Locke* (**1962**): **individual is the sole proprietor of his own person or capacities for which he owes nothing to society**; it **denies his commitment to other individuals, traditions, practices and conceptions of the good**; **self is prior to its ends**; it is **fully competent to choose its ends as well as its roles and dispositions**.
- ✅ Answer: the **'situated self', as constituted by his social roles, practices and situations**; identity **constituted by specific commitments to his social situation**; authority accepted because it **expresses our common will or reflects our common identity, our shared values and beliefs**.
- ✅ **Liberalism had won liberty of the individual**, but atomism **led to the erosion of the sense of responsibility and the moral standards attached thereto**.
- ✅ Lineages: liberalism from **Smith, Locke, Bentham**; communitarianism from **Aristotle, Rousseau, Hegel, Green**; contemporary sources **Oakeshott (1901-90)** and **Arendt (1906-75)**; exponents **MacIntyre** (*After Virtue*, **1981**), **Sandel** (*Liberalism and the Limits of Justice*, **1982**), **Taylor** (*Hegel*, **1975**; *Sources of the Self*, **1989**; *Philosophical Papers*, **1985**), **Walzer** (*Spheres of Justice*, **1983**).
- ✅ **Community** is **characterized by intimacy and durability**; **association** is **based on impersonal and contractual relations**; liberalism equates society with association, communitarianism with community, **to determine the nature and extent of social obligation**.
- ✅ Liberals: each defines his own **good** within a structure fixing the **right**; communitarians: the structure defines **'right' as well as 'good'**; liberal common good is **a sum total of the good of all individuals**, communitarian is **a uniform entity where the good of all individuals would converge**; **like liberals, communitarians also subscribe to democratic form of government**.
- ✅ MacIntyre: the **narrative self**; **'individuality' owes its origin to the framework of an established community**; individuals flourish only within **'socially established cooperative human activity'**; otherwise **social disintegration and moral disaster** — **crime and violence, the breakdown of the family, and the rampant drug abuse**.
- ✅ Sandel: against Rawls's **'original position'** of **disconnected and disembodied individuals**; **after subtracting all these characteristics, nothing is left of the person whom we want to understand**; understanding requires **'embeddedness' in a particular time, place and culture**, creating a **'deeper commonality'**.
- ✅ Walzer: **criteria of distribution should correspond to the 'spheres'**; each has **its own right reason**; **politics, or health, or education** must be free of **the domination of money**, **office** of **nepotism**, **kinship** of **male domination**; the programme includes **a constrained market**, **independent public schools**, **workers' control of companies and factories**.
- ✅ Taylor: rejects the **'atomistic' concept of individuals which focuses on their 'will'**; **some feminist writers and religious thinkers have also confirmed that real communities cannot be created out of an aggregate of 'freely choosing adults'**.
- 🔑 Difficulty: **no mechanism to ensure that its principles will be adopted as the general rules of behaviour**; **a strong moral philosophy, but... not... an equally strong political philosophy**.

## Post-colonial perspective (VI)

- 🔑 It **does not embody any new theory of the state**, but **enables us to understand some new aspects of state-system**.
- ✅ It **must be based on the experience of post-colonial societies themselves**, independent **from the mid of the twentieth century onwards**.
- ✅ **Colonial Powers** box: **Britain, France, Belgium, Spain, Portugal**; **eighteenth, nineteenth and early twentieth centuries**; exploiting **natural as well as human resources**.
- ✅ **Neo-colonial Powers** box: **international trade, conditional aid, investment in multinational corporations (MNCs), military intervention, cultural domination and transfer of pollution**.
- ✅ Europe had **national consolidation by the seventeenth century** and needed **cheap raw materials, cheap labour and vast areas of operation**; the colonised were rich in resources but had **little advantage of modern education or national solidarity**; **India and Egypt** had a **magnificent past** without the potential of consolidation.
- ✅ Pattern: domination → **project their own culture as superior** → claim to be **civilizing the so-called uncivilized people whom they continue to exploit** → **create institutions and elites**; **'whiteman's burden'**; **racial superiority** asserted.
- ✅ Double effect: measures **deliberately designed to facilitate and stabilize their rule** — **Macaulay's 1835 Minute** and **'brown whitemen'**, **Indian Railways, posts and telegraphs, schools, colleges, universities, hospitals** — yet they **promote an awareness and consciousness**, **dispel superstitions**, create **an urge for social reform** and **national movement**.
- ✅ End: power handed **to the local elite**; maintenance **a costly affair**; the power may **'grant'** independence and **switch over to new forms of exploitation by neo-colonial methods**; colonialist values may persist.
- ✅ **Nation-building**: transfer of **commitment and loyalty from smaller tribes, villages or petty principalities to the larger central political system**; **a national identity cutting across... religion, race, caste, language, region, culture, occupation**.
- ✅ **State-building**: **a common centre of power** to establish law and order and extend **defensive and welfare services... to each and every part of its territory**; the two are **complementary**.
- ✅ Symbols — flag, emblem, anthem, song, calendar, flower, bird, animal, tree, river — and **national heroes**; **glorification of some mythological characters... may create some problems in multi-religious countries**; **fortification of secular values is necessary in multi-communal countries like India**; without tackling **mass poverty, illiteracy, unemployment, ill-health, housing**, **nothing would prove effective**.
- ✅ Disappointment: multiplied demands; an inherited **bureaucracy, particularly the police**, **used to oppression of the people**; successors who **hardly matched their calibre and character**; politics as **a game of enlisting support of narrow interests**; but **professional elites** created **widespread awareness of democratic and human rights**.

## Gandhian perspective (VII)

- ✅ Gandhi (**1869-1948**) wrote no treatise; his view **will have to be gleaned from his relevant observations**.
- ✅ The state is **a manifestation of power and law which were based on coercion**, operating through **police force, law-courts, prisons and military power**; it casts individuals **into a uniform mould**, **destroys his sense of self-reliance**; the modern state is **more organized and more centralized**.
- 🔑 **Individual is endowed with soul, but state is a soulless machine**; its enforcers **do not feel any moral responsibility**.
- 🔑 He **condemned political power on moral ground, and not on historical or economic grounds**.
- ✅ *Young India* (**1931**): political power is **not an end but one of the means**; a self-regulated national life yields **enlightened anarchy** where **everyone is his own ruler**; **in the ideal State... there is no political power because there is no State**; **but the ideal is never fully realized in life** — hence Thoreau, **that government is best which governs the least**.
- ✅ **Tolstoy (1828-1910)**, **philosophical anarchist or pacific anarchist**, Christian; Gandhi through **Hinduism (sanatana dharma)** with **equal respect for all religions**; both gave **precedence to spiritual bliss over material satisfaction** and attacked **private property**; he differed from **Proudhon (1809-65)**, **Bakunin (1814-76)** and **Kropotkin (1842-1921)**, who **regarded religion as a hindrance to human development**.
- ✅ Gandhi against Marx: class from **a mental outlook** not ownership; **'dignity of Labour'** and **'bread labour'** **in a spirit of service to the community**; **'trusteeship'** by **'change of heart'** against **social ownership**; **replacement of huge machinery by human labour** against **full development of technology**; **limiting one's needs to the minimum**; religion as **a moralizing force** against **'opium of the people'**; **Marx professed 'scientific socialism'; Gandhi professed 'moral individualism'**.
- ✅ **Moral Individualism** box: the individual as **an end-in-itself, endowed with 'dignity'**; **no human being can be treated as a means to serve an end which lies beyond his conscious existence**; **no worldly thing can be treated as more valuable than a human being**; **no human being can accept an obligation except at his own free will**.
- ✅ **Swaraj**: literally **'self-rule', 'self-government', 'self determination' or 'independence'**, expanded to **cultural and moral independence**; a country **politically independent but culturally dependent... would be devoid of swaraj**; it **does not close the doors of learning from others**; it **rules out people's dependence on government... even to their own government**; **swaraj government will be a sorry affair if people look up to it for the regulation of every detail of life**.
- 🔑 **Real Swaraj will come not by the acquisition of authority by a few but by the acquisition of the capacity by all to resist authority when it is abused.**
- ✅ Moral regeneration through **'self-control', 'self-discipline' and 'self-purification'**, continuing **even after the independence**; **I have no desire to exchange 'king log for king stork'**; **political self-government... is no better than individual self-government**, to be attained **by precisely the same means**; **purity of means as well as ends** requires **ahimsa**.

## Feminist perspective (VIII)

- ✅ Two aspects: the state **as an instrument of regulation of the public sphere** and **as an instrument of power**.
- ✅ Male dominance **extends to all aspects of her life including the most intimate relations**; therefore **the sphere of purely personal relations between man and woman will also have to be regulated**; hence the redefinition of **'political'** and the slogan **'the personal is political'**.
- ✅ **Kate Millett**, *Sexual Politics* (**1971**): politics as **power-structured relationships, arrangements whereby one group of persons is controlled by another**; her literary study found **power, not eroticism or sexual passion, was their real subject**.
- ✅ Public sphere: politics concerns **the conflict over the distribution of scarce resources, including contests for control of state apparatus and its policies**; women are **a deprived section**, long denied **the right to property and right to vote**; the state is **a key actor** on **protection from male violence, greater reproductive rights..., changes in family and employment law, provision of pension benefits and other welfare schemes**.
- ✅ **Zillah Eisenstein**, *The Radical Future of Liberal Feminism* (**1981**): the liberal state **represents itself as fully autonomous so that all positions therein are equally open to all players**, and **is not bothered to ascertain the actual share of power enjoyed by women**.
- ✅ Socialist-feminist charges: welfare **increases women's dependence on men**; the state **seeks to free women's labour for exploitation in the labour market**; it maintains **women's unpaid labour in the home**.
- ✅ Variation: in post-colonial states, lives are **less often touched by state regulation, because of the poor implementation**, and women **often become victims of state violence**; in formerly socialist states, **'forced emancipation'**.
- ⚠️ ❌ The chapter's further claims — that exclusion **has caused environmental degradation** because women are **nature-friendly**, and that militarism follows because women are **peace-loving by nature** — are asserted without support and conflict with its own denial of natural differences in excellence.
- 📰 Tested at Lesson 63 against the IPU's *Women in parliament 1995-2025* (March **2025**): **27.2%** of national parliamentary seats held by women in **2025**, up from **11.3%** in **1995**, with growth of **0.3 percentage points** in **2024** despite **73 chamber renewals**, the slowest since **2017**. ⚠️ The exclusion premise survives; the essentialist premise is not thereby supported.

## Pluralist perspective (IX)

- ✅ The state is **a mechanism designed to serve the interests of various groups simultaneously**, with **descriptive as well as normative overtones**.
- ✅ It **repudiates a single centre of power**; it **does not hold supreme power**; it must act as **an impartial arbitrator** and **justify its authority by proving to be the guardian of justice in society**.
- ✅ Only **a democratic state, which provides the right to freedom of association**, can evolve into it; associations **counterbalance the power of the state**; their set constitutes **'civil society'**, **the bulwark of democratic order**.
- ✅ Authoritarian states forbid independent associations; **Solidarity (an independent union of workers) in Poland in 1980** and East European **environmental groups** preceded collapse.
- ✅ Origin: **a protest against the doctrine of state sovereignty**; **Leon Duguit (1859-1928), a French legal theorist**, contested sovereignty **solely resting with the state**; **Laski** and **MacIver** urged treating groups as **different centres of power**, with the state competing **for winning the loyalty of citizens by serving their interests more effectively**.
- ✅ Contemporary: **Dahl and Lindblom**, *Politics, Economics and Welfare* (**1953**); **Dahl**, *A Preface to Democratic Theory* (**1956**) and *Who Governs?* (**1961**); **'polyarchy'** — **power is not centralized but dispersed among numerous interests and groups**; government **little more than an honest broker in the middle**; **Polsby**, *Community, Power and Political Theory* (**1963**) — **at the bottom nobody really dominates**.
- ✅ Objections: groups do not **bargain on equal footing**; some **lack internal democracy**, so **dominant personalities... project their own will as the will of the whole group**.
- 🔑 Confessions: **Lindblom**, *Politics and Markets* (**1977**) — **business and property enjoyed a privileged position in the market-dominated polyarchies of the West**, exercising **undemocratic control of society**; **Dahl**, *Dilemmas of Pluralist Democracy* (**1982**) — pluralism **cannot be treated as an open competition between truly equal political forces**, with **redistribution of power in society** recommended.
- ✅ **Thus, the descriptive and normative sides of the pluralist perspective on the state come very close to each other.**

## Cross-perspective recall

- ⚠️ Origin admits three answers: natural (I), artificial by agreement (II, III), artificial by imposition (IV); VI and VIII make no origin claim.
- ⚠️ Liberty admits at least six: obedience, civil liberty, non-interference, positive power, emancipation, self-rule; feminism extends the domain.
- ⚠️ Inequality admits four verdicts: natural, functional, legitimate-if-freely-produced, structurally produced.
- ⚠️ Civil society splits the chapter: absent (I), suspect (IV, VIII), guarantee (III, IX).
- ⚠️ Three repeating structures: exposition-criticism-residue; ideal-actual-gap; displacement-revival.
- ⚠️ Six unresolved pairs: the private sphere; welfare capacity against swaraj; the limit of state autonomy; natural difference; the two senses of civil society; the two common goods.
- ✅ Six authorial verdicts: no evidence of natural differences; the contract is **bad history, bad logic and bad philosophy**; Green **made a great mistake**; liberal theory's **merit** and **tragedy**; the class theory's contribution and revision; communitarianism's missing **mechanism**.
- 🔑 Closing position: theories of the state are **provisional solutions** and **working formulae**, selected by the question asked; **debate must go on**.

## Owner corrections and extensions

⚠️ **Three owner statements are corrected or extended on the evidence, and no owner file is edited.**

⚠️ **First — the division count and structure.** ✅ Both owners treat the chapter as supplying ten "images of the state" (Advanced §1) or ten definitions (Basic §3), splitting the liberal stream into contractarian, laissez-faire and welfare-liberal strands and marking the split as the owners' own device for exam clarity. ✅ The chapter in fact has **nine numbered divisions**, and the welfare state is one of them. ⚠️ The owners' split is retained here as a teaching aid at **L14** and **L26**, but the chapter's own nine-fold structure controls the Core block, and the discrepancy is disclosed at **L2**.

⚠️ **Second — the page attributions for the communitarian and neo-Marxist material.** ✅ The Basic owner cites the communitarian division at **PDF pp.267-270** and the Gramsci-Miliband-Poulantzas material at **PDF p.266, extending to p.267**. ✅ Both are confirmed by the scan. ⚠️ The Advanced owner cites neo-Marxism at **PDF pp.265-267**; the scan shows **Recent Developments** opening at **p.265** with the base-superstructure box and Weber, and Gramsci, Miliband and Poulantzas falling on **pp.266-267**. ✅ The finer attribution is used throughout and recorded at **L37** and **L38**.

⚠️ **Third — the extensions.** ✅ The Basic owner's §6 supplies two reconstructions this chapter does not print in full: the Miliband-Poulantzas debate as **claim → objection → limit**, and the Millett-Eisenstein distinction as **personal-is-political** against **liberal-state-neutrality**. ✅ Both are adopted and extended at **L38**, **L48**, **L59** and **L63**. ✅ The owner's Locke-dating caution — composition **c.1679-1683**, publication 1689 with a 1690 title page — is carried at **L15**, **L17** and **L20** as a 🔶 owner note, because the chapter prints only **(1690)**.

⚠️ **One owner caution is carried without resolution.** ✅ Both owners record that no sentence in them is a direct quotation and that no bibliographic date beyond their own listed ones is asserted. ⚠️ This file quotes the chapter directly, subject to the normalisations below, and asserts only dates the chapter itself prints — two of which are themselves recorded as open uncertainties.

## Evidence-typing convention

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | the source's own statement, reproduced or closely reported, with a page anchor available |
| ⚠️ | this session's reading, inference, caution or reconstruction — ❌ not a claim of the source |

| 📰 | a dated statement, kept at its date and ❌ not presented as the source's own |
| 🔑 | a high-yield point, flagged for revision rather than for provenance |
| ❌ | a prohibition, boundary or explicit denial |
| ➡️ | a routing instruction to another topic's or subject's owner |

## Dated-anchor control

📰 **One dated teaching anchor is introduced at Lesson 63; its provenance is repeated only in the register notes and this ledger.** 📰 On the Inter-Parliamentary Union's own report *Women in parliament 1995-2025*, published in **March 2025**, the share of seats held by women in national parliaments rose from **11.3% in 1995 to 27.2% in 2025**, and in **2024**, despite **73 chamber renewals** globally, women's parliamentary representation increased by only **0.3 percentage points**, the slowest rate of progress since **2017**.

⚠️ It is used solely to test the chapter's own empirical premise that there is a **widespread exclusion of women from state power**. ✅ On that test the premise survives, the chapter's book-period framing understates the movement since 1995, and the 2024 slowdown supports a structural rather than an incremental reading. ❌ It is not evidence for or against the chapter's essentialist premise. ❌ It does not measure women's share of state power generally, since the chapter's claim concerns **different branches of the state apparatus and... state policies** while the figure covers legislatures only. ❌ No doctrine, lesson, concept check, Mains model or application anchor in this file depends on it. ❌ No second current linkage appears anywhere in this file, and every other lesson records a truthful nil in its pre-teach checklist.

❌ **No other post-2009 substantive, statistical, statutory or policy fact is introduced anywhere in this file.** ⚠️ Exempted from that statement: bibliographic forms, examination years and paper codes, which are bibliographic rather than substantive, and the standard-form corrections catalogued in the normalisations table.

## Subject boundary

| ✅ Belongs here | ➡️ Belongs elsewhere |
|---|---|
| ✅ Nine perspectives on what the state is, as the chapter divides them | ➡️ Sovereignty, its characteristics, the monist line and the pluralist **theory of sovereignty** — **Topic 11** |
| ✅ The pluralist perspective's one-sentence origin as a protest against sovereignty, naming **Duguit** | ➡️ The internal challenge to sovereignty and the full pluralist case — **Topic 11** |
| ✅ The post-colonial perspective's pattern of domination, nation-building and state-building | ➡️ Imperialism as a system, Hobson, Lenin, Nkrumah, decolonisation, the power blocs and globalisation — **Topic 12** |
| ✅ Gandhi as a theorist of what the state is, and swaraj as a conception of freedom | ➡️ Political obligation, resistance, civil disobedience and satyagraha as a technique — **Topic 14** |
| ✅ Gandhi's moral individualism and trusteeship as they bear on the state | ➡️ Gandhism as an ideology alongside socialism, fascism and anarchism — **Topic 05** |
| ✅ The class perspective and the neo-Marxist debate **as accounts of the state** | ➡️ Historical materialism, alienation and Marxism as a general philosophy — **Topic 04** |
| ✅ Liberal and welfare-liberal accounts **of the state** | ➡️ Liberalism and neoliberalism as ideologies — **Topic 03** |
| ✅ The feminist perspective **on the state** | ➡️ Feminism as an ideology, sex and gender — **Topic 06** |
| ✅ The communitarian perspective **on the state** | ➡️ Communitarianism and the nature of politics — **Topic 07** |
| ✅ Walzer's spheres **as a communitarian argument** | ➡️ Theories of justice and distributive principles — the Justice owners |
| ✅ Philosophy Optional PYQs as **separately disclosed, answer-neutral application anchors** | ❌ Any claim that this topic owns an examined question, and any alias, proxy, mirror or synthetic route |
| ⚠️ **Macaulay's 1835 Minute** and colonial infrastructure **only as the chapter's conceptual illustrations** | ❌ Indian constitutional articles, institutions, federalism, case law and policy — the **Polity**, **Governance** and **Social Justice** owners |
